# Sūrah al-Najm (Chapter 53) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Star That Names the Chapter**

Sūrah al-Najm, "The Star," is the fifty-third chapter of the Qur'an and the first of the six sūrahs that open the twenty-seventh juzʾ of the written muṣḥaf. It takes its name from the oath that forms its very first verse — *wa'l-najmi idhā hawā*, "By the star when it sets" — a single word, *najm*, that carries within it the whole chapter's argument. For the desert Arabs who first heard it, the star was the most public of all evidences: no one could deny it, no one could own it, no one could bend it to his purpose. Night after night it rose exactly where the order of heaven had placed it, and night after night it descended exactly when that order commanded. The Qur'an opens this chapter by swearing upon that most ordinary of miracles, and then turns the oath immediately upon the central claim of its opponents: the man they called *ṣāḥibukum* — "your companion," the man they had known for forty years, who had sat at their tables, carried their goods, and settled their disputes — *has neither strayed nor erred*. The star establishes the character of the universe; the companion establishes the character of the message.

**An Early Makkan Sūrah in the Heat of the Argument**

Al-Najm is classified by the majority of scholars among the earliest Makkan sūrahs, revealed soon after Sūrah al-Ikhlāṣ (112), although many commentators hold that verses 13–18, which describe the Ascension (al-miʿrāj), were added to the chapter at a later Makkan date — most likely in 619 CE — because the Night Journey and Ascension are generally placed late in the Makkan period, while a minority maintain the event occurred seven years before the Hijrah. Verse 32, and by some accounts verse 33 as well, is considered Madinan by certain authorities. This layering matters less than the unity it ultimately serves: from first verse to last, the sūrah is a single courtroom speech in which three questions are answered in order. Was the revelation true? Then the idols are false. And if the idols are false, then judgment — a full accounting of every deed — must be real. The classical commentators, from al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī to Ibn Kathīr, never tire of pointing out that these three movements are one argument, not three.

**The Chapter That Was the First Recited in Public**

This sūrah holds a distinction no other chapter of the Qur'an can claim in quite the same way: it is reported to be the first sūrah the opponents of Islam heard recited openly in Makkah, at the moment the command came to proclaim the message publicly (reported by Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, and al-Ālūsī after the earliest historians). The man chosen to carry that first public recitation was not the Prophet himself but ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd, may God be pleased with him, a slight, unimposing young man of the servile classes of Quraysh — chosen, as he later explained, because he reasoned that the nobles of Quraysh would never physically attack him in the way they would the Prophet. Al-Bukhārī and Muslim transmit his own account of standing in the courtyard of the Kaʿbah reciting aloud, and Muslim records his testimony in which al-Najm itself stands at the center:

> *"I recited Sūrat al-Najm before the Quraysh, and when I came to its prostration, they prostrated — until I feared they would make it a pretext against us."* (Muslim 401)

Al-Bukhārī transmits the same Companion's further remark that when he prostrated in that recitation, *the idolater prostrated, and the believer prostrated — and even Satan* prostrated, passing himself off among the crowd; which is precisely the phenomenon the later verses of this very sūrah would unmask (al-Bukhārī 3423; Muslim 402). Ibn Masʿūd also testified that he knew which sūrahs the Prophet ﷺ recited together in prayer, and singled out al-Najm as one whose prostration the Prophet himself performed (al-Bukhārī 1070). A chapter that could gather the prostrations of warring factions in one courtyard is a chapter whose rhetorical power was felt before it was analyzed; the Muslim community never ceased to recite it — it remains among the most frequently recited of the longer Makkan sūrahs in the congregational prayers of the Friday prayer and the night vigil.

**Its Place Beside Sūrah al-Ṭūr**

In the arrangement of the muṣḥaf, al-Najm follows Sūrah al-Ṭūr (52) as an answer follows a question. Al-Ṭūr had ended with the disbelievers taunting the Prophet: *"Or wish they to confer, or wish they to? Nay, they have no certainty of the Hereafter. Nay, their Lord of them is not unaware"* (52:36, 40), and with the absurd accusation, *"Or do they say: He has invented it?"* (52:33). Al-Najm opens as the formal rebuttal: the one accused of invention is vindicated in three terse verses — your companion has not strayed, has not erred, does not speak from caprice — before the chapter reveals the true source of his speech: *"It is naught but revelation revealed"* (53:4). Even the imagery is joined: al-Ṭūr closed with the command to *"glorify Him at the receding of the stars"* (52:49), and al-Najm opens with the star itself (al-Ālūsī notes this pairing). The reader who moves from 52 to 53 walks from the accusation to the acquittal without passing a single door.

**The Two Great Visions: Gabriel and the Ascension**

Al-Najm is among the most discussed chapters in all of Qur'anic commentary because its first section (verses 1–18) is read as the record of two seminal events, and the commentators have divided honestly between them. One school — al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī, and the majority — reads the passage as describing the two occasions when the Prophet ﷺ saw the Archangel Gabriel in his true form rather than the human form he ordinarily assumed: once at the first revelation on the mountain of Ḥirāʾ, when Gabriel appeared filling the horizon with six hundred wings, and once during the Ascension, at the lote tree of the boundary. The other school — strongly represented in the ḥadīth of Ibn ʿAbbās — reads it as the Prophet's vision of his Lord, with the heart or with both heart and eye, during the Night Journey and Ascension, when he was brought *"within two bows' length or nearer."* Both readings will be developed at length in their verses below, because both are carried by the classical tradition, and both teach the same essential truths: that the Prophet saw what he saw with total integrity; that his message came from beyond the visible world; and that the unseen realm is not a human invention but a human inheritance, populated by real presences whose order is God's own.

**The Anatomy of the Sūrah**

The chapter unfolds in movements the classical commentators described with near unanimity. The first movement (verses 1–18) authenticates the revelation by authenticating the revelator: the star swears; the companion is defended; Gabriel — or the Lord Himself — is described as He stood upon the highest horizon, drew near, disclosed what was disclosed, while the Prophet's heart never lied about what it saw. The second movement (verses 19–30) turns from authentication to refutation: Have you considered al-Lāt, al-ʿUzzā, and Manāt? Are these names to be given daughters while you keep sons? They are nothing but names you have named; and the angels you have feminized in your conjecture are themselves helpless to intercede without God's leave. The third movement (verses 31–54) builds the moral architecture: everything in the heavens and the earth belongs to God precisely so that He may requite every evil and reward every virtue; the human being receives only what he strives for; and the perpetual truths written in the scriptures of Moses and Abraham — no bearer of burdens bears another's; man has only his endeavor; his endeavor will be seen; laughter and weeping, death and life, enrichment and impoverishment are all His — are sealed by the memory of the peoples who ignored those truths: ʿĀd the Former, Thamūd, the people of Noah, the cities that were razed. The final movement (verses 55–62) is a crescendo of short, hammering questions — Which of your Lord's boons do you dispute? Do you marvel at this discourse? Do you laugh and not weep? — closing with the chapter's last command and the verse at which the whole congregation of Makkan history has bowed: *So prostrate unto God and worship.*

**Why This Sūrah Speaks Directly to the Modern Reader**

Three features give al-Najm an unusual address to every age. First, it defends a man against the accusation of self-invention — and therefore defends, by extension, the very possibility of revelation against every age that prefers its own conjecture to received truth. Second, it dissects the anatomy of idolatry at its roots — not merely the worship of stone, but the deeper habit of *following conjecture* (al-ẓann) and *what the soul desires* (mā tahwā al-anfus) — a diagnosis the modern world, saturated with customized beliefs and manufactured certainties, can read like a mirror. Third, it stakes everything on one moral axiom: *man shall have naught but that for which he endeavored* (53:39), a principle that dignifies human effort, forbids fatalism, and makes the accounting of the Hereafter not a threat bolted onto life but the meaning of life itself. In sixty-two verses, the sūrah moves from the setting of a star to the prostration of a human forehead; the commentary that follows walks that road verse by verse, as the tradition demands — each verse given its own complete treatment, its own language opened, its own witnesses gathered, its own logic traced, and its own light cast forward upon the life of the reader.

**How to Read What Follows**

The commentary below treats each of the sixty-two verses as an independent discourse, in strict verse-by-verse order. Every verse is quoted individually from the sūrah's text, then expanded through the Arabic language, the classical exegetes — al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Baghawī, al-Bayḍāwī, al-Rāzī, al-Zamakhsharī, and the reports of Ibn ʿAbbās — the authentic ḥadīth with their collections identified, the historical circumstances of revelation where these are transmitted, and the bearing of each verse upon creed, psychology, society, and daily practice. Where the source commentary treats verses in pairs or small groups, they are separated here and each receives its full individual treatment. Nothing is compressed because the chapter is long; the sūrah itself commands the manner of its reading, for it is the chapter of prostration, and no one prostrates in haste.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:1

> **By the star when it sets,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Only Chapter That Opens with a Star**

This is the only sūrah of the Qur'an that begins with the word *najm*, "star," and the choice is deliberate in every direction. The chapter is named after the word, the word is the first of a chain of oaths, and the chain of oaths is the preface to the most intimate description of revelation anywhere in the Book: the verses that follow speak of a heart that did not lie about what it saw, of a being of awesome power upon the highest horizon, of two bows' length or nearer. Before any of that is said, God swears by a star. The reader is thus placed, from the very first syllable, under a vault of night sky — and the argument of the whole chapter will be that the same Power that orders the rising and the setting of that star is the Power whose word descends upon a human heart in Makkah.

**The Root of Najm: What a Star Actually Is**

The Arabic *najm* derives from the root *n-j-m*, whose core meaning is *to appear, to emerge, to come forth*. The noun *najm* therefore names a thing that *appears* — and the plural *nujūm* is used in the Qur'an both for the physical stars and, in one striking verse, for something that appears in installments. From the same root comes the verb *tanajjamā*, to divine by the stars, and the noun *najwā*, the private whisper of two people conferring apart from a crowd — the speech that *emerges* into secret. Al-Iṣfahānī, the great lexicographer of Qur'anic vocabulary, notes that the root carries the sense of something appearing after absence, as in the proverbial *bāraka Allāhu fī najmikum* — a blessing invoked upon the *star* of a traveler, meaning his timely arrival. The word itself, before any interpretation, already teaches: a star is defined by the fact that it *appears* from concealment. It is no accident that the Qur'an will use this word both for the lights of heaven and for the descent of revelation, which likewise *appears* from a realm the eye cannot reach.

**The First Reading: The Setting of the Pleiades**

The earliest layer of interpretation, recorded from Ibn ʿAbbās and transmitted through al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī, and Ibn Kathīr, holds that *al-najm* refers to the Pleiades — *al-Thurayyā* — the tight cluster of stars that dominated Arabian sky-lore and agrarian time-keeping; the Bedouin read the seasons, the rains, and the calendar of pasturage upon her rising and setting. On this reading the oath means: *by the star at the time of its setting*. Why would God swear upon the *setting* rather than the rising? The commentators answer with a psychological insight: any observer can admire a rising star, but only the patient, the attentive, and the honest keep vigil to see it set. Rising advertises itself; setting must be awaited. Moreover, the setting of a star in the pre-dawn — *ḥubūb al-ṣubḥ* — was the signal by which travelers and shepherds ordered their first movements of the day. God thereby swears by the precise celestial event that ordered the beginning of every Arabian day, the quiet clock of the whole society He is addressing. An oath upon a clock everyone obeys but no one can alter is an oath upon order itself.

**The Second Reading: The Stars Extinct on the Day of Judgment**

A second reading, adopted by al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭūsī, and others, understands the setting eschatologically: the verse swears by the stars at the moment of their ultimate disappearance, the cosmic collapse of the Last Day. This reading is anchored by the Qur'an's own future tense about these very lights:

> *"And when the stars fade away"* (81:2)

> *"So when the stars are effaced"* (77:8)

and again:

> *"And when the stars are scattered"* (82:2)

On this interpretation, the oath looks past every nightly setting to the final one — and the choice becomes thunderous. God swears, at the opening of a chapter that will end with the command to prostrate and the promise of judgment, by the very moment when the strongest lights in creation go out. The reader is told: the stars you consider eternal are on lease; even they will bow to the command that governs your death and your resurrection. The commentators observe the rhetorical force: what human being swears by the extinguishing of his own most brilliant possession? Only the Owner of all brilliance can make that oath, and only an oath of that magnitude could carry the weight of what follows in verse 18 — *he saw the greatest of the signs of his Lord.*

**The Third Reading: The Revelation That Descends Star by Star**

A third interpretation, recorded from the earliest commentators and noted by al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭūsī, and al-Biqāʿī, takes *najm* as a figure for the Qur'an itself, descending upon the Prophet *in installments* — *nujūman*, star by star, piece by shining piece — as the physical star emerges piece by piece from the horizon's edge. This reading finds its direct parallel in the later sūrah:

> *"And I swear by the places of the stars — and it is a great oath, did you but know"* (56:75–76)

where the oath by the *stations of the stars* is explained, by the near-unanimous testimony of the commentators, as an oath by the descending portions of revelation alighting upon the Prophet's heart. The logic of the metaphor is exact: just as a star travels its assigned arc and sets precisely where its path concludes, so revelation descended verse by verse upon assigned occasions — a ruling here, a consolation there, a rebuke somewhere else — never early, never late, never displaced. The opponents of the Prophet claimed the Qur'an was a jumble of invented tales; the oath answers that it descended with the punctuality of the heavens. Anyone who has watched a real star rise, hold its station, and vanish at its appointed second understands the claim instantly: the revelation has orbits, and orbits have an Owner.

**Why God Swears by His Creation**

The Qur'an swears by created things repeatedly — by the fig and the olive (95:1), by the dawn and the ten nights (89:1–2), by the sun and its brightness and the moon that follows it (91:1–2) — and the scholars, following Ibn Taymiyyah's discussion of the divine oaths, explain the principle: God swears by the great and visible signs of His own power precisely because they are *common evidences*, things no one can deny and no one can manufacture. Human courts demand witnesses; the divine courtroom produces its witnesses from the sky. Furthermore, every such oath instructs the listener to *look*: to pause before the object sworn by and read it as testimony. When God swears by the setting star, the believer's relationship to the night sky changes permanently — the star is no longer decoration, omen, or shepherd's tool, but a standing witness whose daily speech is: *the order above you is real, and the One who set it speaks.*

**The Star as the Destroyer of Star-Worship**

A subtler purpose lies beneath the oath, and it emerges fully only at verse 49 — *it is He Who is the Lord of Sirius* — where the chapter returns to the star it began with. The pre-Islamic Arabs who would later worship Sirius, and other peoples who worshiped the luminaries, gave stars the reverence owed only to the divine. The Qur'an's opening oath performs a double act: it honors the star as a magnificent sign, and it simultaneously demotes it to the rank of *witness* rather than *lord*. A witness can be sworn by; a lord cannot. By swearing *by* the star rather than speaking *of* it as divine, the verse quietly teaches the correct theology of the heavens before the chapter argues it explicitly. Abraham's argument in the Qur'an follows the same arc: he saw a star and said to his people, *"This is my Lord"* — and then, *"when it set, he said: I love not those that set"* (6:76). Al-Najm's first verse is the whole argument compressed into one breath: the star sets; only the One who commands its setting deserves worship.

**The Lesson for Every Reader: Trust the Small Evidences**

There is a practical discipline hidden in the oath. The star is not the largest of God's signs — the sun and moon dwarf it — yet God chooses it for the opening of a chapter about the greatest of all claims: that the Creator speaks to a human being. The lesson is that certainty is built from the accumulation of ordinary, public, repeatable evidences, not from spectacular demonstrations alone. The believer is trained to reason as the verse reasons: from the verifiable to the unverifiable, from the star everyone sees to the revelation one heart received. In modern terms, the man who cannot trust the daily sunrise will never trust the testimony of history; the soul that learns to read small signs faithfully becomes capable of weighing great ones. And the oath carries a warning as well: the same stars that testify for the order of God will one day be *effaced* — every earthly evidence has an expiry date, and only the Speaker behind the evidence is eternal. The chapter thus begins by pointing every eye upward, and will end, sixty-one verses later, by pressing every forehead to the ground. Between the two postures — gazing and prostrating — lies the whole argument of Sūrah al-Najm.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:2

> **your companion has neither strayed nor erred;**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Word "Companion" — A Defense Built from Friendship**

The verse names the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ not with titles — not "the Messenger," not "the chosen one" — but with the intimate, unglamorous word *ṣāḥibukum*: *your companion*. The root *ṣ-ḥ-b* denotes accompaniment, the sharing of a road, a seat, a decade of ordinary life. The Quraysh to whom this verse descended had known this man for forty years as a fellow townsman: he had walked their streets as an orphan, managed Khadījah's caravans, carried stones in the rebuilding of the Kaʿbah, mediated disputes, and earned the surname *al-Amīn*, the trustworthy. Al-Rāzī observes the rhetorical genius of the choice: the defense of the Prophet is not launched from heaven but from earth — from the accumulated public record of his character. A man's claims about heaven can be questioned; his forty years of observable conduct cannot be rewritten. God begins the acquittal with the one piece of evidence His opponents themselves had supplied. The Qur'an uses this same word in its own testimony elsewhere: *"Say: I admonish you unto one thing only: that you stand for God, two by two and one by one; then reflect — there is no madness in your companion; he is naught but a warner unto you before a terrible chastisement"* (34:46).

**The Double Negation: Two Verbs, Two Complete Acquittals**

The verse contains two negations, and the scholars of language heard them as two distinct acquittals rather than one repeated. First, *mā ḍalla* — *he has not strayed* — from the root *ḍ-w-l*, to wander from the way, the word the Qur'an uses for the lost tribe of the Fātiḥah, *ḍāllīn*. Al-Rāzī and others note that *ḍalāl* is the precise opposite of *hudā*, guidance: it names the absence of the path itself. Second, *wa mā ghawā* — *he has not erred* — from *gh-y-w*, to be deceived into wrong action, the root behind *aghwāʾ*, the seducers of hell (7:146). The distinction, carried by the commentators from al-Farrāʾ to al-Ṭabarī's listing of the early views and codified by al-Rāzī and Ibn ʿĀshūr, is that *ḍalāl* concerns direction and *ghayy* concerns destination: the one who *strays* has lost the road; the one who *errs* believes he is on the right road while hurtling toward a cliff. Some exegetes, including those cited by al-Qurṭubī, define *ghayy* more sharply as the error born of a corrupt creed — the *ignorance that results from adhering to a false religion* — so that the two negations together cover the whole anatomy of being wrong: wrong about the way, and wrong about the goal. The verse thus acquits the Prophet at both levels at once: his *religion* is true, and his *practice* of it is true. A religion can be true and its follower incompetent; a practice can be sincere and its creed rotten. Both defects are excluded by a single, double-edged negation.

**The Order of the Negations Is Itself an Argument**

The commentators noticed that the negation of *straying* is placed before the negation of *erring*, and asked why the order cannot be reversed. The answer follows the natural history of a human being's going wrong: one first loses guidance (direction) and then, proceeding confidently in the dark, falls into error (destination). The verse denies both in their natural sequence — no lost beginning, therefore no corrupt conclusion. There is also an implicit refutation of the two opposing accusations the Quraysh leveled at the Prophet ﷺ. Some called him *majnūn*, mad, deranged — that is, *ḍāll*, cut off from sound direction; others called him a *sāḥir*, a sorcerer composing deliberate deceptions — that is, *ghāwī*, knowingly misleading. The verse replies in the same order: he has not *strayed* (he is not mad; his direction is from God), and he has not *erred* (he is not a deceiver; his destination is the truth). One short line dismantles both charges, each in its place.

**Apropos the Man, Not Merely the Message**

It is worth pausing on the fact that the verse defends *the man* — his person, his sanity, his moral standing — and not only the text. In every prophetic mission the messenger is attacked along two tracks: discredit the man, and the message falls with him. The Quraysh understood this perfectly, which is why the Qur'an records their tactics in detail: *"Nay, they have marveled that a warner from among themselves came to them, and the disbelievers say: This is a sorcerer, a liar"* (38:4). God's defense of His Prophet is therefore repeated across the Book like a wall built brick by brick: *"We know indeed that what they say grieves thee; yet it is not thee they belie — but it is the signs of God that the evildoers deny"* (6:33). Al-Najm's second verse is the summit of that wall: the complete personal acquittal — no straying, no error — placed at the very doorway of a chapter whose whole purpose is to authenticate what he would subsequently transmit. A seal must first be proven unbroken; the verse proves the seal.

**Why This Defense Matters for Every Messenger's Followers**

The defense of the Prophet's *ṣidq* (truthfulness) is not a mere historical courtesy; it is the foundation on which every later Muslim's confidence in the Qur'an and the Sunnah rests. Ibn Taymiyyah stresses in his discussions of prophecy that the credentials of a prophet are twofold: the message must be internally credible, and the messenger must be externally verified — by his known life, by his consistency, by the absence of self-interest. The Quraysh had watched Muhammad ﷺ for four decades; had he ever lied to them in trade? Had he ever shown madness, ambition, or caprice? Their own consciences — and, the sīrah records, even their most bitter enemies like Abū Jahl admitted it privately — testified to his integrity. The verse therefore teaches a permanent method of evaluation: when weighing any claim to guidance, first weigh the claimant. The modern reader, bombarded by self-appointed gurus, is trained by this verse to ask the companion-question before the content-question: *who is this man when he thinks no one is recording?* The Prophet's own companions answered that question with their lives; the Qur'an answers it in half a line.

**The Companion of God: The Highest Companionship**

The scholars of sīrah add a final note of tenderness. The word *ṣāḥib* in the Qur'an almost always marks closeness in hardship: Moses' *companion* was his brother Aaron; Abraham's *companion* in the parable of the two gardens — the disputer (18:32–44) — and the two *companions* of the Prophet in the cave (9:40, where God says to His Prophet of Abū Bakr, *"grieve not; God is with us"*). To have God Himself open a sūrah by calling Muhammad ﷺ *your companion* is to have the Lord of the universe invoke the warmest category of human relationship on His Prophet's behalf. Al-Ālūsī reflects that the Qur'an here models for every believer how to defend the honor of the righteous: not with abstract theology, but with the concrete testimony of shared life. And for the believer, the address becomes personal: the man called "your companion" by God is also offered to you as your lifelong companion — his way, his prayer, his character — so that the word of the verse extends across the centuries: *your companion*, the one who walks with you, has neither strayed nor erred; walk where he walked, and you cannot be lost.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:3

> **nor does he speak out of caprice.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Acquittal: The Mouth Itself Is Sealed**

The first two negations acquitted the Prophet's direction and destination; this third acquittal reaches further — into his very *speech*. *Wa mā yanṭiqu ʿani'l-hawā*: *he does not speak from caprice*. The verb *yanṭiqu* shares its root (*n-ṭ-q*) with *al-Ṭāriq* ("that which knocks by night," 86:1) and with *manṭiq*, articulate speech — it names the emission of sound from the inner man, the point where the hidden heart becomes audible. Al-Zamakhsharī notes the precision: God does not merely say the Prophet does not *invent* falsehood; He says the mouth of the Prophet does not produce falsehood as its *source*. The verse therefore covers every word the Prophet spoke, and the commentators, led by Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī, draw the two conclusions the verse sustains: first, the Qur'an itself is not the Prophet's own composition — it is revelation; second, even his ordinary, non-Qur'anic speech stands under a divine guardianship — his judgments, counsels, and commands are guided and preserved from whim. This double scope is the seed of the entire Sunnah's authority: the community that heard this verse understood that both what he *recited* and how he *lived* were secured against caprice.

**Hawā: The Word That Names the Soul's Downhill**

The key term is *hawā*, "caprice," from the root *h-w-y*, whose literal image is *falling* — the hollow into which water drains, the empty belly of a valley (*hāwiya*), and hence the abyss (*al-hāwiyah*) that is the name of hell's lowest pit (101:9). Caprice, in the Qur'anic vocabulary, is not mere whim; it is *the soul sliding downhill*, desire moving along the path of least resistance exactly as water finds its drain. This is why the Qur'an sets *hawā* and *hudā* (guidance) as cosmic opposites, on opposite slopes of the same mountain. The contrast is drawn with terrifying clarity in one of the Qur'an's most sobering portraits:

> *"Have you seen him who takes his caprice for his god? God has led him astray knowingly, and sealed his hearing and his heart, and set a veil upon his sight."* (45:23)

and again:

> *"Who is further astray than him who follows his caprice without guidance from God?"* (28:50)

The commentators add that the verse's grammar — *yanṭiqu ʿan* — uses the preposition of *motion away from*: he does not speak *away from* his own whim into the realm of mere opinion; rather his speech never originates there at all. The Prophet himself enacted this verse in a moment of hope that a chief might accept Islam, hoping inwardly they would all believe — and the rebuke descended: *"that you would kill yourself with grief because they believe not"* (26:3). Even his *longing* was corrected by revelation, so that not only his tongue but his desires were discipled. Al-Rāzī draws the conclusion: the Prophet's speech is a third thing between the speech of men and the speech of God — human in voice, divine in origin and protection.

**The Logical Necessity of This Verse**

The scholars observed that this third acquittal is the *necessary conclusion* of the first two, and the verse sequence in al-Najm follows the logic of a proof. If the Prophet has neither strayed (no false direction) nor erred (no false destination), then the question remains: *what produces his speech?* Every human utterance has a source, and the sources are essentially two: truth received, or caprice produced. Having denied error, the verse must name the true source — which is exactly what verse 4 does: *"It is naught but revelation revealed."* Verses 2–4 thus form a tight logical chain: (1) the man is not lost; (2) his religion is not mistaken; (3) his speech is not self-generated; therefore (4) his speech is revelation. Al-Biqāʿī notes that the three negations also answer the three standard accusations made against every prophet in history — madness (no straying), error (no misguidance), and authorship (no caprice) — so that the Makkan audience hears, in three half-verses, the complete dismantling of their own recorded objections: *"We know indeed that they say: It is only a human being who teaches him; the tongue of him at whom they hint is foreign, while this is a clear Arabic tongue"* (16:103).

**Caprice in the Balance: The Psychology of the Soul's Slope**

The Qur'an's choice of *hawā* rather than a word for "lying" teaches a psychology far deeper than the accusation of fraud. The Quraysh were not primarily accusing the Prophet of conscious forgery; they were, in their own terms, suggesting that *everyone speaks from somewhere* — from interest, from grief, from ambition — and that his speech too must flow downhill from some human source. The verse answers: not this man. And in answering, it draws a map of the human soul that every person since can verify in themselves. The scholars of the inward sciences, following al-Ghazālī and Ibn al-Qayyim, describe caprice as the default channel of the untrained soul: opinion follows feeling, feeling follows appetite, and appetite follows the visible world. The disciple who has watched his own opinions bend toward his own comfort understands *hawā* experientially — it is the inner slideshow that always argues for the self. This is why the Prophet's freedom from caprice is a miracle in kind, not only in degree: the one human mouth in which the downhill drain was sealed. When ʿĀʾishah, may God be pleased with her, was asked about the Prophet's character, she answered: *"His character was the Qur'an"* (transmitted by Muslim in the Ṣaḥīḥ, 746) — the Book and the man flowed from the same sealed source.

**The Sunnah Stands on This Verse**

Al-Shāfiʿī built his entire jurisprudence upon the principle contained here: the Prophet's speech and practice are themselves revelation, binding because guarded. The Qur'an commands elsewhere: *"Whatever the Messenger gives you, take it; and whatever he forbids you, abstain from it"* (59:7) — a command that would be unintelligible if the Prophet's speech were subject to whim. Ibn Kathīr cites the consensus: obedience to the Prophet in what he commands is obedience to God, because God has removed *hawā* from his tongue as He removed error from his heart. Al-Ṭabarī records the early interpretation that the negation includes everything the Prophet uttered by way of religion — so that his believers across the centuries, reciting this verse, are receiving a guarantee whose practical reach is the hadith literature itself: every authenticated report is speech from a mouth that God Himself declared incapable of speaking from caprice. Few verses carry such enormous juridical and spiritual freight in so few words.

**A Mirror for Every Speaker**

For every human being who will ever open his mouth, the verse sets a standard and a mirror. It does not demand that we be incapable of caprice — only the Prophet ﷺ is — but it identifies caprice as the *enemy of true speech*, the thing to be hunted out of one's words. Ibn Taymiyyah counseled his students to distinguish within themselves three speakers: the tongue of revelation (whatever agrees with God's word), the tongue of honest reason (whatever humbly seeks truth), and the tongue of caprice (whatever merely defends the self). The discipline of the believer is to know which speaker is currently talking. The modern world — with its algorithms of desire, its platforms engineered to feed *hawā* precisely because desirous speech generates engagement — has industrialized the soul's downhill slide; the Qur'an's third acquittal stands against all of it as the portrait of what human speech looks like when the slope is closed and only the truth can pass. And it tells the believer where such speech begins: not in cleverness, but in the heart's surrender — *"It is naught but revelation revealed"* — which is the next verse, and the true source.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:4

> **It is naught but a revelation revealed,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Three Negations to One Affirmation**

Three verses have said what the Prophet's speech is *not* — not straying, not error, not caprice — and this fourth verse finally says what it *is*: *in huwa illā waḥyun yūḥā* — literally, *"it is not but a revelation being revealed."* The commentators pause over the architecture of the Arabic. The sentence is framed with the double restrictive device of Arabic rhetoric — the negative particle *illā* ("except," "only") bound to the pronoun *huwa* ("it") — so that the exclusion is absolute: speech like this can have one source and one source only. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that the construction has the force of a oath-complete declaration: nothing else is even possible for speech of this kind. The Quraysh had demanded the Prophet's *source*; the verse now names it in two words, and the whole remainder of the chapter's first section (verses 5–18) will simply *describe the source*: who taught him, how, from where, and what the Prophet saw.

**The Rhythm of Emphasis: Revelation Revealed**

The phrase *waḥyun yūḥā* is an alliterative pairing — *waḥy* (revelation) from *wa-yḥ*, and *yūḥā* ("is revealed") from the same root — and the tradition of commentary reads the doubling in two complementary ways. Al-Ālūsī, following the earlier exegetes, explains first that the emphasis distinguishes *revelation* from mere *inspiration* (*ilhām*): God inspires intuitions, guidance, and knowledge into the hearts of the righteous generally, but this is not that; this is *waḥy* in its full, technical, apostolic sense — divine speech committed to a messenger for transmission, protected and complete. Second, the grammatical form *yūḥā* is in the *continuous* passive — "a revelation being revealed" — which al-Ālūsī reads as indicating that revelation was renewed to the Prophet moment by moment, verse by verse, season by season, throughout twenty-three years: not one book dropped at once, but a living link, alive at every hour of the Prophet's mission. The two readings together produce the doctrine in its fullness: the Qur'an is divine in source (not inspiration), and immediate in delivery (not a memory of a past event but a present-tense receiving). When the Prophet said of the Qur'an, *"Jibrīl used to review the Qur'an with me once every year, and in the year of his death he reviewed it with me twice"* (al-Bukhārī 4998), he was describing this continuous channel still open at the end of his life.

**The Qur'an's Own Definition of Its Transmission**

The verse is the Qur'an's own clearest self-declaration of its transmission chain, and it must be read alongside its sister verses, each naming one link. God speaks of *revelation* as the only channel by which divine speech reaches men:

> *"It is not for any human being that God should speak unto him, save by revelation, or from behind a veil, or by sending a messenger, revealing by His leave whatsoever He wills."* (42:51)

and the Qur'an names the Messenger and the heart as the two final links:

> *"The Trustworthy Spirit descended with it upon thy heart, that thou mayest be of the warners, in a clear Arabic tongue."* (26:193–195)

> *"And thus have We revealed unto thee a Spirit of Our command; thou knewest not what the Book was, nor what was faith — but We made it a light by which We guide whomsoever We will of Our servants."* (42:52)

Al-Ṭabarī gathers these passages to define the doctrine of *waḥy* precisely: it is God's causing His knowledge to reach the prophet immediately — sometimes like the ringing of a bell, hardest upon the Prophet, who would grip his companion's hand and sweat on a cold day; sometimes the angel appearing as a man who spoke and left (the ḥadīth of ʿUmar: al-Bukhārī 49; Muslim 2333). Al-Najm 53:4 is the doctrinal cornerstone of all of it: *the words are not the Prophet's; the source is beyond the world*.

**Why Revelation and Not Philosophy: The Logic of the Claim**

The scholars long reflected on why God answered the accusation of invention with the doctrine of revelation rather than with philosophical argument. The answer is that revelation is not one theory among alternatives; it is the alternative to the entire category of human production. A philosopher's book can be improved, corrected, outgrown; a revelation claims finality and demands surrender — *"Do they not consider the Qur'an? Had it been from other than God, they would have found in it much discrepancy"* (4:82). Ibn Taymiyyah observed that every false religion bears the fingerprints of its human origin: its laws flatter the powerful, its images flatter the imagination, its loopholes flatter the appetite. The Qur'an challenges every such fingerprint: *"Say: If mankind and the jinn gathered to produce the like of this Qur'an, they could not produce the like of it, even were they to help one another"* (17:88). The verse in al-Najm places the whole burden of proof where it belongs: if the speech is not from the Prophet's caprice, the opponents must produce an alternative source — and fourteen centuries of attempting to do so have produced, at most, the rephrasing of their original conjecture.

**The Verse That Founded the Science of Ḥadīth**

The scholars of ḥadīth and law drew from this verse the principle that made their entire discipline possible: the Prophet's religious speech is guarded, and therefore *recordable, collectible, verifiable, and binding*. Al-Shāfiʿī argued in his Risālah that the command *"Whatever the Messenger gives you, take it"* (59:7) joined to this verse's guarantee of non-caprice establishes the Sunnah as revelation in a second register. This is why Muslim scholarship never treated the Prophet's statements as the "memoirs of a wise man" to be weighed against the Qur'an as an equal: the Qur'an itself declares the channel sealed. At the same time, the community learned a nuance from the scholars: the guarantee covers the Prophet's *religious* speech and preserves the *transmission* through verification — hence the Muslims' unprecedented discipline of chains (*isnād*) and criticism (*jarḥ wa taʿdīl*), described by the scholars as the ummah's unique credential, *"a science the likes of which no other nation possesses."* The verse guards the source; the ummah's labor guarded the record; the two together are why a Muslim in Jakarta can today know, within a rigorous science of authentication, what a man said in seventh-century Madinah.

**The Believer's Personal Claim on This Verse**

For the individual believer, this verse changes the experience of recitation. The Book in one's hands is not a historical document about a man who reported experiences; it is *a revelation being revealed* — the same speech that descended upon a heart in the cave of Ḥirāʾ, riding on the tongue of a being of awesome power, arriving at the reader's eye and ear in the present. The scholars of recitation loved to note that the Qur'an names itself with words of continuous descent: *tanzīl* — "a sending-down" — appears in verbal and noun forms literally hundreds of times, and one verse compresses the whole geography of the Book's journey into one breath: *"[This is] a Book whose verses are made firm, then expounded in detail, from One Wise, Aware: that you worship none but God"* (11:1–2). When the Muslim opens the muṣḥaf, al-Najm 53:4 stands as the certificate of authenticity for the page: not strayed, not erred, not caprice — *a revelation, revealed.*

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:5

> **taught him by one of awesome power.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Teacher Named at Last**

After three negations and one affirmation — not strayed, not erred, not caprice, but revelation — the verse now supplies what every opponent's question requires: *who taught him?* ʿAllamahu shadīd al-quwā — *"taught him by one of awesome power."* The verb *ʿallama* is the causative of *knowing*: not a whisper of influence, not a vague enlightenment, but *teaching* — the deliberate transmission of content from a teacher to a student, the very activity by which the Quraysh themselves had learned every art they possessed. Al-Qurṭubī notes the force: the Qur'an is here compared, in its mode of arrival, to the ordinary experience of all learning — except that the teacher is not a man. The verse thereby answers the specific accusation recorded in Sūrah al-Naḥl: *"It is only a human being who teaches him"* (16:103). Very well, the Qur'an replies: he *was* taught — but by whom?

**The Majority Reading: Gabriel, the Mighty Teacher**

The near-universal reading of the earliest exegetes — recorded from Ibn ʿAbbās and transmitted through al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī, and Ibn Kathīr — identifies the teacher of awesome power as the Archangel Gabriel (Jibrīl), upon whom be peace. The identification is confirmed by the Qur'an itself, which names Gabriel as the carrier of revelation and describes him with the vocabulary of might:

> *"The Trustworthy Spirit descended with it upon thy heart"* (26:193)

> *"Say: Whoso is an enemy to Gabriel — then truly he sent it down upon thy heart by God's leave, confirming that which was before it, and a guidance and glad tidings for the believers. Whoso is an enemy to God and His angels and His messengers and to Gabriel and Michael — then God is the enemy of the disbelievers."* (2:97–98)

The title *shadīd al-quwā* — "mighty in power" or "of awesome strength" — belongs to the Gabriel of the Qur'an's own descriptions: the being who *struck* the deviant city with his wing, who wrestled with nations' worth of power at God's command. Al-Ṭabarī cites the early view that God gave Gabriel the strength of *some thousands* of angels for his missions; Ibn Kathīr recalls the Prophet's description of Gabriel in his true form filling the horizon. When such a being stood before a man in the cave of Ḥirāʾ and said *"Recite!"* — squeezing him until he could bear no more, as the Prophet related in the famous first-revelation narration (al-Bukhārī 3) — the teaching was attended by power the like of which no academy on earth has ever seen. The mode of the teaching is part of the teaching: truth was not delivered as an option but as a commission.

**The Minority Reading: God Himself as Teacher**

A minority of the commentators, noted by al-Qurṭubī and others, read *shadīd al-quwā* as a reference to God Himself — so that the sense is: *"God, of awesome power, taught him."* They support this with verses that attribute the teaching directly to the Lord: *"And thus have We revealed unto thee a Spirit of Our command"* (42:52) and *"The Most Merciful taught the Qur'an"* (55:1–2). The two readings harmonize in the doctrine they share: the ultimate teacher is God; Gabriel is the appointed carrier, as a riverbed is the appointed channel of water. Al-Rāzī reconciles them beautifully: in the chain of transmission the teacher is Gabriel; in the chain of authority the teacher is God; and scripture itself uses both locutions, for the Qur'an says both *"Gabriel sent it down upon thy heart by God's leave"* (2:97) and *"It is naught but revelation revealed."* The later mystics added a third echo: the soul of every believer is also taught by this same God, mediately and immediately — but the Prophet's teaching alone came with the office of transmission, sealed and complete.

**Why "Awesome Power" Attends the Teaching**

The scholars asked why the verse describes the teacher by his *power* rather than by his knowledge, purity, or rank. Ibn ʿĀshūr answers that power is precisely the attribute that excludes the last possible human explanation: no man could compel, produce, or fabricate what this teacher delivered, and no man could have overpowered the Prophet's consent — the teaching was authoritative in the fullest sense, attended by overwhelming might that made resistance and fabrication alike impossible. There is also a pastoral logic: the Prophet ﷺ was a human being standing before a cosmic being; the verse assures him (and every reader) that the one who teaches is neither fragile nor ambiguous but *shadīd al-quwā* — no deception, no midway spirit, no jinn's whisper, could pose as this teacher. The Prophet himself later warned against the false claimants of revelation: *"Whoever claims a dream he did not see will be made to tie a knot between two barleycorns on the Day of Resurrection"* (al-Bukhārī 6995; Muslim 1679) — and the ḥadīth literature preserves the Prophet's clear distinction: *"True dreams are one of the forty-six parts of prophethood"*, but they are not revelation, and the seer of them is not its carrier. The teacher of al-Najm was *one* teacher, named by power, and every generation's false teachers are measured against him and found weightless.

**The Ethics of Being Taught**

The word *taught* dignifies the Prophet as a *student* — and places him in the posture every believer must occupy. The commentators draw from this verse the Prophet's own lifelong identity as *the student of heaven*: he never claimed to originate knowledge; he repeated what was taught. His companions preserved the humility of the posture in his practice — he sat with his companions as one of them, corrected his own mistakes publicly, said *"I am only a human being; you bring me your disputes and perhaps one of you is more eloquent in his proof than the other, and I judge in his favor accordingly; whoever I grant anything of his brother's right, let him not take it, for I am only granting him a brand of Fire"* (al-Bukhārī 2680; Muslim 1713). Here is the paradox of the verse: the most authoritative teaching humanity ever received was delivered through the most teachable man humanity ever produced. The believer draws the lesson directly: revelation flows toward the taught, not the proud; the Qur'an says of itself that it is *"an admonition for him who has a heart, or gives ear while he is a witness"* (50:37) — and the first witness to have given ear was the Prophet, taught by one of awesome power.

**Power and Mercy in One Teacher**

Al-Ālūsī closes the commentary on this verse with a reflection the tradition has cherished: the teacher is described as *mighty in power*, yet the content of what he taught was a Book whose opening address is *"In the Name of God, the Compassionate, the Merciful."* Power guards the channel; mercy fills the content. Had the teacher been mighty but not commissioned by mercy, the teaching would have crushed the student — as indeed the first encounter nearly did, the Prophet returning to Khadījah trembling, saying *"Cover me, cover me!"* (al-Bukhārī 3). The strength of the teacher was exactly calibrated to the task: enough to ensure fidelity, never so much as to coerce the heart of the hearers. This is the Qur'anic balance of *qudrah* and *raḥmah* — and the next verse will display the teacher upon the horizon, where the spectacle of his stature will become, for the Prophet, the first vision of his true form.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:6

> **Possessed of vigor, he stood upright**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Teacher Displayed in His Full Stature**

The verse continues the description of the teacher with a second attribute and a first image: *dhū mirratin fa-stawā* — *"possessed of vigor (or beauty of stature), he stood upright."* Al-Ṭabarī records that *mirrah*, though it denotes strength, is also used for *ḥusn al-khalq* — beauty and nobility of bodily form — so that al-Ṭūsī, Ibn Kathīr, and al-Zamakhsharī all render the phrase: *"possessed of perfect strength and beautiful stature."* The picture is deliberate: this is not a description of mere muscular force but of *composed, complete perfection of form* — the way one describes a figure whose every proportion is right. The commentators then add the decisive fact of sacred history: the Qur'anic tradition holds that Gabriel showed his true form to the Prophet ﷺ only twice in the whole mission, and both times are tied to this passage — once at the first revelation, when the angel appeared upon the horizon, and once during the Ascension at the lote tree (Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī). This verse and the two that follow are the Qur'an's compressed record of those two appearances.

**Six Hundred Wings on the Horizon: The Prophetic Testimony**

The weight behind this verse is the Prophet's own testimony, preserved in the two Ṣaḥīḥs. Ibn Masʿūd relates:

> *"The Messenger of God ﷺ saw Gabriel in the form he was created, having six hundred wings."* (al-Bukhārī 3232; Muslim 174)

And al-Bukhārī transmits the detail of the first revelation from ʿĀʾishah's narration of what the Prophet said of that day:

> *"...then he appeared to me, and he was upon the horizon; then he drew near and came close."* (al-Bukhārī 4855)

Ibn Kathīr notes that the accounts describe the horizon itself darkened and filled by the winged form — a being whose wingspan covered the east while the Prophet stood gazing. The scholars make a careful distinction here, taught by the tradition: Gabriel's *ordinary* form when he came for teaching was human — a man in white garments with intensely black hair, whom the companions sometimes saw and spoke with, most famously in the hadith of Gabriel (al-Bukhārī 50; Muslim 8), where he questioned the Prophet before the assembled Muslims about Islam, faith, excellence (iḥsān), and the Hour, and then vanished — and the Prophet said: *"That was Gabriel, who came to teach you your religion."* What al-Najm 53:6 records is the *other* register: the created form, the true angelic substance, shown only twice. Al-Rāzī reflects on the mercy in the arrangement: had Gabriel always appeared in his true form, the Prophet could never have sat with him, debated with him, or received the Qur'an gently over twenty-three years; the two unveilings served specific purposes of witness and honor.

**The Meaning of Istawā: The Posture of the Teacher**

The verb *stawā* — he *stood upright, composed himself, assumed his posture* — from the root *s-w-y*, to be level, complete, well-proportioned — is read by the commentators in close relation to the teaching of verse 5. Al-Ālūsī explains that *istawā* describes the manner in which Gabriel *conducted the teaching*: not in haste or agitation, but in perfect composure, as a master seated before his student delivers the lesson without a tremor — the revelation descending with the calm of the divine order itself. Others, following the horizon narration, read it as the angel's *assuming his stance upon the horizon* when the Prophet asked to see him in his true nature. And the third reading — which belongs to those who take the whole passage as the vision of the Lord during the Ascension — understands *stawā* of the manner in which God disclosed His Light to the Prophet. The three readings are not rivals so much as three lenses on one theme: *composure*. Revelation came to the world not as chaos but as order; the teacher stood as a mountain stands — and the Book he delivered would command the same composure in prayer, in trade, in war, and in the face of death.

**Why the Vision Was Shown Twice**

The tradition preserved by Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī asks why Gabriel displayed his true form to the Prophet only twice — at Ḥirāʾ and at the lote tree — and answers with the two functions such a display serves. The first display, at the inauguration of the mission, served as the Prophet's personal, unshareable authentication: whatever doubts a human being might carry about unseen callers were dissolved for him at the origin by an overwhelming sensory event — the horizon filled, the command thundered, the whole sky testified. The second display, at the summit of the Ascension, served the community: it marked and sealed the farthest point of the Prophet's journey, so that the mission would end, as it began, with an unveiled sight of the world beyond the world. Al-Ālūsī adds the psychological wisdom: such visions were rationed precisely because faith in the unseen is *meant* to operate without constant spectacle — *"Sight comprehends Him not"* (6:103) is the rule of ordinary religious life; the two great visions were exceptions by which the rule's messenger was certified, not the rule itself abolished. The Muslim therefore does not seek visions; he seeks the Book that the vision guaranteed.

**Strength of Form, Strength of Content**

There is a quiet correspondence the scholars loved to note: the teacher's attribute — *vigor, beauty of stature* — becomes the attribute of his teaching. The Qur'an that this being delivered is described by the Prophet as strong and beautiful: he said, *"The best of speech is the speech of God"* (al-Bukhārī), and described certain sūrahs as the *glory* of the Qur'an. The believing reader who feels the majesty of the Book's language is feeling, at a distance of centuries, the composure of the being who stood upright to teach it. Al-Rāzī draws the ethical conclusion for every teacher of truth: authority in teaching comes not from volume, aggression, or performance, but from *composed possession* of what one teaches — the angel taught as one who stood, not one who scrambled; so the scholar, the parent, the preacher who would teach religion must first stand within it, inwardly settled, before the words leave the mouth.

**The Horizon as Boundary Between Worlds**

The image of the figure standing on the horizon carries symbolic weight the commentators do not miss: the horizon is the line where the visible world ends and the invisible begins — the nearest thing to a *door* between human perception and the realm of the unseen. When Gabriel stood upon the horizon, the Qur'an's message to every reader is that revelation came *from across that line*: not from the dreams of a sleeping man, not from the speculations of a monk in a cave, but from the border of another order of being, carried by its appointed and magnificent inhabitant. The next verse will fix the location precisely — *the highest horizon* — and the two verses after that will record the crossing: the drawing near, and the distance of two bows. The chapter is walking the reader, step by step, along the route by which the Word of God entered history.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:7

> **when he was upon the highest horizon.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Location of the Encounter: The Highest Horizon**

The verse fixes the geography of the vision: *wa huwa bi'l-ufuqi al-aʿlā* — *"while he was upon the highest horizon."* Three readings stand in the classical commentaries, all converging on the same truth. First, al-Qurṭubī transmits from Ibn ʿAbbās (the reading he shares with al-Jalālayn) that this is Gabriel's appearance at the *eastern horizon* — the point of sunrise — after the Prophet asked to see him in his true form, when the great angel stood filling the sky where day begins. Second, al-Ṭabarī records the reading that the location is the *highest heaven* — the angel appearing in the loftiest of the seven heavens, the place nearest the divine Throne. Third, the reading of those who take the passage as the Ascension: it was *the Prophet* himself who came upon the highest horizon during the Night Journey and Ascension, reaching the boundary-point beyond which none of creation's ordinary occupants may pass. The Arabic *ufuq* denotes the circular rim where sky meets earth — the visual boundary of the whole visible world — and the adjective *al-aʿlā* raises that boundary to its ultimate height. Whether the stand was upon earth's eastern rim or heaven's seventh, the meaning is constant: the encounter occurred at the *utmost edge of visibility*, the farthest point the eye of any creature reaches before sight itself gives out.

**The Qur'an's Own Cross-Reference: The Clear Horizon**

This verse is the anchor of a small constellation of Qur'anic texts that record the same event from adjacent angles. In Sūrah al-Takwīr, the same vision is described with an emphasis on its *clarity*:

> *"No indeed — by the moon, and the night as it recedes, and the dawn as it breathes: this is naught but the speech of a noble messenger, possessed of power, established in the presence of the Lord of the Throne, obeyed there, and trustworthy. And your companion is not mad; and truly he saw him upon the clear horizon."* (81:15–22)

The passage in al-Takwīr is almost a legal deposition for the same case: a *noble messenger* (Gabriel), *powerful*, *trusted* by the Lord of the Throne — *seen upon the clear horizon* by *your companion* who is *not mad*. Al-Rāzī notes that the two sūrahs mutually interpret: al-Najm says *highest* horizon (the location), al-Takwīr says *clear* horizon (the certainty); together they testify that the vision was both at the uttermost boundary and unmistakably seen. The repetition across two sūrahs of such particular, unlovely details — a horizon, a standing figure, a not-mad companion — carries the ring of testimony rather than legend: myths decorate; witnesses report coordinates.

**Why the Horizon and Not the Interior**

The scholars asked a beautiful question: why did God stage the first great vision at the horizon — the sky's rim — rather than in some interior space, a valley or the Kaʿbah's court? Ibn ʿĀshūr answers that the horizon is the *limit* of the human visual field: by placing the teacher at the farthest reachable point, the vision dramatized the truth that its source lay *beyond* the reach of every creature's perception, and that the teacher was *coming toward* the human world from outside it. Had Gabriel appeared inside the Prophet's room, the vision might have been read as an inward figment — a dream or a phantasm. On the horizon, before the open sky, in the physical direction from which light itself enters the world, the event belonged to the public geometry of creation: any who had been present would have seen it. The scholars add the theological point: revelation descends *upon* the world from *beyond* it, and the staging of the first vision taught this cosmology in a single image — the boundary was crossed, from outside in.

**The Highest Horizon in Every Life**

The commentators extend the image into the moral life with a reflection that has become a staple of devotional literature: every human being has a horizon — the limit of what he can see, know, and control — and humility consists in knowing where one's horizon lies. The modern world, with its telescopes and satellites, has pushed back the *physical* horizon beyond anything the Arabs of Makkah imagined, yet the *cognitive* horizon stands exactly where it always stood: we see effects, not first causes; surfaces, not essences; the near, not the hereafter. The verse teaches the believer to expect the decisive encounters of truth at that boundary — the place where self-sufficiency ends. It is precisely when a human being reaches the rim of his own powers — in the operating theater at 3 a.m., at the graveside, at the edge of the sea of his own ignorance — that the question of what lies beyond becomes inescapable. Al-Najm's horizon says: beyond that rim, Someone stands, and He is not silent.

**From the Highest Horizon to the Highest Teaching**

There is also a lesson in sequence. The vision was placed *above* the Prophet's eye-line — he looked *up*. The commentators observe that the Qur'an's imagery consistently associates divine address with elevation: the Throne, the heavens, the *Tāʾ Hā* of God's call to Moses from the *valley of Ṭuwā*, the mountain that *crumbles* at its Lord's manifestation (7:143). Human beings learn their smallness by looking up; the megalomaniac looks only level or down. The Prophet, the most honored of creatures, spent his mission looking up — in prayer, in vigil on the mountain, in the Ascension itself — and the community that followed him learned to build its sanctuaries with domes and minarets, architecture that physically instructs the neck. The verse that describes a figure standing upon the highest horizon thus encodes the posture of the whole religion: *lift what is below you toward what is above you*, and measure every claim on earth against the Standard that stands at the rim of sight.

**A Word on the Two Schools at Their Point of Agreement**

It should be said plainly, as the scholars from al-Ṭabarī to Ibn Kathīr say it: whether one reads verses 6–9 as describing Gabriel in his true form upon the horizon, or as describing the Prophet's ascent and the vision vouchsafed to him near his Lord, the doctrinal content on which Islam stands is identical and untouched by the difference — the message is from beyond the visible world; the carrier is mighty and commissioned; the recipient is truthful and unharmed in mind; and what was seen was real, witnessed by the most trustworthy eye in creation. The schools debated *who stood* on the horizon; no Muslim school ever doubted *that the vision stood* — and the next verses carry the reader from the horizon's rim into the nearness that lies beyond it.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:8

> **Then he drew nigh and came close,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Two Verbs of Approach**

The vision moves: *thumma tazalla fa-tazāllā* — *then he drew near, and came close.* The Arabic is a grammatical jewel that the commentators unstop with care. The verb *dunū* (to draw near) appears first in a form (*tazalla*) read by many grammarians as one verbal root doubled through the Arabic eighth form, and the second occurrence adds the doubled form *tazāllā*, whose *fāʿʿala* pattern in Arabic idiom denotes *intensive, mutual, or participatory action*. Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī observe that the doubling of nearness in a single verse — drawing near, then *surrendering* to nearness — teaches that the approach happened in stages, each more intimate than the last: there is a nearness of presence, and then a nearness of communion; first the distance shrinks, then the shrinking itself becomes the state. Al-Qurṭubī records the earliest readings: *he* — Gabriel — drew near to the Prophet; or *He* — God — drew near to the Prophet; and al-Ṭūsī adds the mystical reading that became a treasure of the tradition: *"Then he [the servant] drew near, and He [God] came close"* — the servant's approach answered and outmatched by the Lord's.

**Gabriel's Nearness to the Prophet**

On the majority reading, the verse describes Gabriel leaving his stance upon the horizon and closing the distance to the Prophet ﷺ. The sīrah tradition preserves the sensory register of such approaches in the reports about ordinary revelations: ʿĀʾishah testifies that revelation would come to the Prophet *"in the coldest day and sweat would pour from his brow"* (al-Bukhārī 2), and Zayd ibn Thābit, whose thigh the Prophet's knee pressed as revelation descended, said: *"By God, my thigh bore such weight from the leg of the Messenger of God ﷺ that I feared it would be crushed"* (recorded by Abū Dāwūd 3647). If the *ordinary* descents pressed so heavily upon those who merely stood adjacent, what must the approach of the great angel in his six-hundred-winged true form have been — not the burden of a man's leg, but the nearness of a being of awesome power? Al-Ṭabarī notes that the verse's composure — two calm verbs, no tumult — itself instructs: the mightiest approach in creation is described with the restraint of perfect order, for the realm it came from is a realm of order, and terror is not its native register; majesty is.

**The Divine Nearness: God's Coming Close**

The reading that assigns the nearness to God Himself — *He drew near* — is anchored by the Qur'an's own repeated doctrine of divine closeness, which the commentators marshal here:

> *"And when My servants ask thee concerning Me — truly I am near: I answer the call of the caller when he calls upon Me."* (2:186)

> *"We are nearer to him than the jugular vein."* (50:16)

and in the ascent of worship:

> *"So remember Me; I will remember thee."* (2:152)

Al-Ṭūsī and the mystic exegetes after him read the verse as the Qur'anic icon of *spiritual wayfaring*: all nearness to God passes through two movements — the creature's drawing near (*tazalla*) by worship, and God's coming close (*tazāllā*) by grace — and the second always exceeds the first, for the creature crawls while God surpasses. The famous ḥadīth qudsī, which al-Qurṭubī cites explicitly in commenting on this very verse, gives the doctrine its full form. Al-Bukhārī records it from Abū Hurayrah:

> *"God says: I am as My servant thinks of Me, and I am with him when he remembers Me. If he remembers Me in himself, I remember him in Myself; and if he remembers Me in an assembly, I remember him in an assembly better than theirs. And if he draws near to Me a hand's breadth, I draw near to him an arm's length; and if he draws near to Me an arm's length, I draw near to him a fathom's length; and if he comes to Me walking, I come to him running."* (al-Bukhārī 7405; Muslim 2675)

Here is verse 8's grammar written into the life of every believer: *he drew near — and He came close*, disproportionately, generously, always ahead of the creature's motion.

**The Obligatory and the Supererogatory: The Two Stages of Approach**

Al-Qurṭubī transmits, in the same commentary, the continuation of that ḥadīth qudsī which maps the two verbs of the verse onto the two tiers of religious life:

> *"...My servant draws not near to Me with anything more beloved to Me than what I have made obligatory upon him; and My servant continues to draw near to Me with supererogatory works until I love him."* (al-Bukhārī 6502)

The scholars of the inward sciences built an entire pedagogy on this pairing. The *first* nearness — the obligatory — is the channel: prayer, fasting, zakāh, honesty; no one skips the channel and reaches the shore. The *second* nearness — the voluntary — is the widening: the extra prayer at night, the charity beyond the tithe, the forgiveness beyond the duty, by which the channel becomes a river. And the fruit of the widening is stated in the ḥadīth's final clause, which the mystics called the verse's deepest echo:

> *"...and when I love him, I am his hearing with which he hears, and his sight with which he sees, and his hand with which he strikes, and his foot with which he walks."* (al-Bukhārī 6502)

Ibn Taymiyyah warns the reader to take this by its sound meaning — God's making the servant's organs *succeed only in obedience* and His preserving and guiding them — not by any confusion of Creator and creature. But even guarded thus, the teaching lands: nearness to God is a real ascent with real stages, and the Verse of the Nearness (as al-Najm 53:8 came to be called among the devout) is its map.

**Why Nearness, and Not a Throne Room, Is the Doctrine**

The scholars observed a striking fact about this verse and its companion (verse 9): the Qur'an describes the *drawing near* — nearness as motion, as relationship — but records no image of *arrival* as absorption. There is no melting of the servant into the Lord, no pantheistic vanishing at the end of the approach. Ibn Taymiyyah made this the centerpiece of his critique of the extremes on both sides: the verse *guarantees real nearness* (against those who reduce God to a distant abstraction) and *preserves real distinction* (against those who dissolve the servant into God). The distance closes to two bows' length — and two is still two; the bows never merge into one. The believer lives his whole spiritual life inside that mathematics: forever approaching, forever received, never erased. A devotion that ends the self is not the devotion of al-Najm; the devotion of al-Najm ends with a *servant*, called by name — *"His servant"* (53:10) — standing nearer than any creature has ever stood, and still a servant.

**The Practical Reading: When Distance Begins to Close**

For the ordinary Muslim, the verse offers a diagnostic and a hope. The diagnostic: distance from God is never primarily spatial or emotional; it is a *failure of approach* — worship neglected, sin embraced, the Qur'an unopened — and distance that results from our own stillness cannot be blamed on His silence, for He has sworn the ratio: *come walking, I come running.* The hope: the verse shows God's motion *always exceeding* ours — a hand's breadth answered by an arm's length. The scholars counsel the struggler who feels far away: do not measure the relationship by feeling; measure it by approach. The servant who crawls toward a Lord who runs is not far — he is, in the language of this verse, mid-verse: *thumma tazalla* — he has drawn near; the *fa-tazāllā*, the Lord's close approach, is already in motion toward him, as it moved for the Prophet beneath the highest horizon, and will carry the reader — the next verse measures it — to two bows' length, or nearer.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:9

> **till he was within two bows' length or nearer.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Most Measured Distance in the Qur'an**

The verse of approach closes with a measurement: *fa-kāna qāba qawsayni aw adnā* — *"so he was at two bows' length, or nearer."* The *qāb* (plural *aqbā*) was a standard Arabian unit — the span between a drawn bow and the string, or between the two ends of a bent bow — familiar to every archer, every carpenter stretching a cord, every desert dweller who measured with what he carried. Al-Rāghib al-Iṣfahānī explains that in Arab idiom *qāba qawsin* denotes any small, near distance — the span of a hand's reach when two people stand almost touching. The commentators marvel at the verse's combination: the immeasurably exalted realm of the divine presence is described with the humble vocabulary of the bazaar and the battlefield. Ibn Kathīr notes the mercy in this: God condescends to human units of measure because He is addressing human beings, whose minds can receive the transcendent only through the familiar — and by choosing a *bow's length*, the Qur'an even embeds an image of the sacred in the instrument of the hunt: the curve, the tension, the readiness.

**Of Whom Is the Nearness Spoken? The Three Schools**

The classical commentaries preserve three structured readings, and honesty requires naming all three. First — the majority, from al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī — the Prophet drew within two bows' length *of his Lord*: this is the reading of the ḥadīth of Ibn ʿAbbās, who insisted, against the objectors, *"He saw his Lord"* (recorded by Muslim in his Ṣaḥīḥ, 162–163, where Ibn Masʿūd dissents), and of the reports that the Prophet came near his Lord at the lote tree. Second — also transmitted by al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī — the Prophet stood two bows' length *from Gabriel*: the nearness is between the two participants of the revelation, the teacher and the taught, at the moment of highest transmission. Third — a minority reading noted by al-Qurṭubī — the verse describes *Gabriel's own nearness to God*: the angel drew close to his Lord before descending with the trust. Al-Rāzī reconciles: whether Gabriel came near to God and then to the Prophet, or the Prophet came near to God, the verse registers one fact beyond dispute — *the utmost creaturely nearness ever granted* occurred on this occasion, and the Qur'an chose to print the moment's measurement into eternal revelation.

**"Or Nearer": The Grammar of Concealment**

The verse's final phrase — *aw adnā*, "or nearer" — has occupied the scholars more than any other two words in the sūrah. Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī observe that Scripture stops at the boundary of the expressible: to say "or nearer" is to certify that the distance closed *further* — while refusing to print how far, for what lies *nearer than near* belongs to the secret of the Presence that created things cannot enclose. The great commentators see in this elision a deliberate doctrine: revelation measures what benefits us and veils what would consume us. The Prophet himself, asked *where* his Lord was, answered with the celebrated words Muslim records from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Shibīb — a testimony the exegetes cite precisely at this verse:

> *"[He is] Light — how could I see Him?"* (Muslim 167)

and the ḥadīth of the veil completes the grammar of concealment:

> *"His veil is Light. Were He to remove it, the splendors of His Face would burn all that His sight reaches of creation."* (Muslim 179)

"Or nearer" is thus Scripture's own etiquette before infinity: the distance is real, the closing is real, the veil is real — and behind the veil is a Reality no bow-span of speech can compass. Ibn Taymiyyah draws the creedal conclusion: the verse affirms nearness, elevation, and encounter in their real sense — while sealing, in the same breath, the transcendence that no measurement crosses.

**The Two Bows as the Arc of All Existence**

The mystic commentators — al-Ṭūsī, and after him the school of Ibn ʿArabī that al-Ālūsī transmits — offer the reading that has made this verse a symbol beyond its occasion: the two bows may be read as *two arcs of a single circle*. One bow is the arc of *descent* — creation proceeding from God, from the Throne through the heavens through the elements to the dust of Adam. The other bow is the arc of *return* — creation ascending back to God through obedience, purification, death, and resurrection. Where the two arcs meet — at their joined tips — the circle of existence is complete; and the verse places the Prophet at that meeting point: the man in whom the descent of revelation and the ascent of nearness close their circle. Al-Rāzī, no mystic but a rationalist, finds in the image the same lesson in theological dress: the revelation descends to earth by the same power by which the servant ascends to heaven; the road down and the road up are one road, and its name is the command of God. The believing reader holds both readings together: the verse is about a historical night when the Prophet was drawn near — and about the shape of every created life, which begins in descent and is completed only in return.

**The Prophet's Own Account of What the Nearness Brought**

The Ṣaḥīḥ collections preserve the Prophet's narration of the Ascension night, and its content at this point is astonishing in its practicality. Anas ibn Mālik relates the long hadith of the fifty prayers: the Prophet was brought near — *"until I was at two bows' length or nearer"* is how Ibn Kathīr summarizes the tradition's wording — and *fifty prayers* were made obligatory upon his ummah; on his descent, Moses counseled him to return and seek reduction, and he went back and forth between his Lord and Moses until the burden was eased:

> *"The obligation of prayer was decreed upon my ummah fifty prayers; I returned with them to Moses, and he said: Return to thy Lord, for thy ummah will not bear it... until they became five prayers, and the reward of fifty; and it was said: The word has been decreed."* (al-Bukhārī 349; Muslim 162–164)

The scholars never tire of the irony's beauty: the greatest nearness ever granted produced, for mankind, not an esoteric secret but the *five daily prayers* — the most public, most repeated, most bodily act of the religion. The measure of proximity is a timetable of return: five bridges a day by which every human being can cross toward the nearness the Prophet was shown.

**Nearer Than Near: The Verse for Every Worshipper**

The tradition closes its reading of this verse with the consolation it plants for every worshipper after the Prophet. If within two bows' length "or nearer" was granted to the best of men on the best of nights, then every lesser nearness is real in kind: the prostration of the obscure believer in a small room is a motion along the same road. Al-Ghazālī counsels the worshipper to remember, in the very moment of prostration, that bodily lowliness is the vehicle of spiritual elevation — the two bows are drawn, as it were, in miniature at every prayer: the forehead lowered, the heart raised. And Ibn al-Qayyim notes the perfect fit of the verse's content to its position: the Qur'an placed the doctrine of nearness at the *center* of the chapter that authenticates revelation, so that the reader would know that the Book's authority and the Book's author share one address — nearer than near, behind a veil of light.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:10

> **Then He revealed to His servant what He revealed.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Servant Is Named**

At the climax of nearness, the Qur'an names the recipient — *ʿabdihi*, *"His servant."* The commentators stand in reverence before this word choice. Of all the titles the Prophet ﷺ carries — Messenger, Warner, Bearer of Glad Tidings, the Illiterate Prophet, the Mercy to the worlds — the Qur'an chooses, at the very point of the utmost honor ever granted a creature, to call him *slave and servant*.
