Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī note that the Prophet himself loved this identification: he said of the Ascension, *"I was taken by night to Bayt al-Maqdis, and God revealed to His servant what He revealed"* is how he opened the narration in some of its transmissions; and the opening of Sūrah al-Isrāʾ does the same: *"Glory to Him who took His servant by night"* (17:1). The lesson the scholars draw is the constitution of all honor in Islam: *the highest rank belongs to the fullest servanthood*. The Prophet is most near precisely as he is most submitted; his title at the summit is not "the one who became divine" but "the one who remained, to the uttermost, a servant." Every Muslim who prays five times a day repeats the same ladder in miniature: *iyyāka naʿbudu* — "Thee alone we worship" — servanthood as the road to nearness.

**The Three Grammatical Readings of "He Revealed"**

Al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, and al-Ṭabarī's student Ibn al-ʿArabī al-Mālikī (whom the Study Quran cites as Ṭb) lay out three disciplined readings of the clause, and each contributes to the doctrine of revelation. First: *He* — God — revealed *to His servant* — the Prophet — *what He revealed*, Gabriel being the medium unmentioned: the plainest reading, and that of the majority. Second: the verse may be read as Gabriel's act — *then he revealed to His servant what He [God] had revealed [to him]*: the chain is made explicit, God to Gabriel to Prophet, exactly as 2:97 states: *"Gabriel — he it is who sent it down upon thy heart by God's leave."* Third — the reading al-Qurṭubī notes: *God revealed to His servant Gabriel what Gabriel then transmitted*: "His servant" being Gabriel, the greatest of the angelic servants. Al-Rāzī comments that the ambiguity is a feature: by leaving the pronoun's referent open, the verse honors every link of the chain at once — the Sender, the carrier, and the recipient — while identifying none of them as the *source*; source belongs to God alone. The doctrine of *tanzīl* (sending-down) is thereby complete in one clause: divine in origin, angelic in conveyance, prophetic in reception, universal in delivery.

**The Veiled Content: "What He Revealed"**

The most remarked feature of the verse is its reticence: the object of revelation is not named. *What* He revealed? The commentators, following al-Ṭabarī's citation of the early reports, list the candidates the tradition has transmitted — and their very plurality instructs. Some early scholars said what was revealed at this nearness was the *obligation of the fifty prayers*, per the Ascension narration of al-Bukhārī 349 and Muslim 164, in which the command of prayer is given at the climax of the ascent. Some said it was the *command of the ummah's way* — the ḥadīth of Muslim 162 in which the Prophet is ordered to enjoin upon his community the worship of God alone and the abandonment of shirk. Some said it was the *tidings of the ummah's leaders* or the *secrets of the Hereafter*; others said simply: what none but he was given. Al-Rāzī and Ibn Kathīr then observe the deeper reasons for the veil, transmitted from the early generations:

> *"What is revealed is not mentioned by name out of reverence for its exalted status."* (the reasoning recorded by al-Jalālayn and Ibn al-ʿArabī al-Mālikī)

The Qur'an itself elsewhere practices the same eloquent silence at moments of supreme encounter — Moses is not told *what* he will be taught at the burning bush; the Night of Decree is not dated. Revelation is deliberately modest at its own summits, and the modesty is a lesson in *adab* — the etiquette of the sacred — for a community that would otherwise trade its holiest coordinates in gossip.

**Reverence, Not Censorship: The Character of the Veil**

Ibn Taymiyyah is careful to distinguish this reverent concealment from mere incompleteness: the Qur'an is *complete* — *"This day I have perfected for you your religion"* (5:3) — but completion does not require the publication of every glory. What the ummah needs for guidance is printed; what would profit only curiosity or invite corruption is veiled. The Prophet himself practiced the discipline: asked about things he had been shown of the Garden and the Fire, he would say, *"I have seen such-and-such, and I would tell you — but glad tidings suffice you"*; and he forbade his community to exhaust such matters: *"Leave me with what I have left you, for those before you were destroyed only by their much questioning and their disputing with their prophets"* (al-Bukhārī 7288; Muslim 1337). Al-Najm 53:10 therefore models the believer's whole relationship to the unseen: certainty about *that* it happened; humility about *what* it contained beyond need; reverence in the space between.

**"Then": The Order of the Ascent's Gifts**

The commentators note the word *thumma* — *then* — which sequences this revelation *after* the nearness of the two bows. Al-Ālūsī draws the spiritual order: nearness precedes disclosure; the gift follows the approach. This is a law of the spiritual life the scholars repeat: knowledge is given in proportion to nearness, not curiosity. The Prophet was not handed information at arm's length; he was drawn near *first*, and the disclosure entered a heart already in the divine presence — which is why what he received could bear the weight of guidance for humanity. The believer reorders his own seeking accordingly: the student who wants knowledge must first seek the Nearness from which knowledge descends — prayer before notes, purity before research — for God gives the river only to those who stand by its bank.

**The Servant's Mission and the Servant's Reader**

The verse ends the first great movement of the sūrah's ascent with a turning outward: what was revealed *to* the servant was revealed *for* the servants — the Qur'an itself, the prayers, the way. The Prophet returned from two bows' length carrying a public inheritance; his nearness became the community's charter. Al-Rāzī observes the perfect justice of the arrangement: the one who served most fully was nearest, and the nearness he was given he immediately spent upon mankind. Here the reader finds his own vocation measured: whatever nearness God grants any soul — a moment of prayer answered, a verse that opens, a sin forgiven in secret — the measure of its reality is what it releases into service. The Prophet's two bows' length produced fourteen centuries of prayer; what will our small nearness produce? The verse does not ask; it implies. And the next verse seals the man himself: *the heart lied not in what it saw* — for what he carried back, he carried truthfully, to the last letter.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:11

> **The heart lied not in what it saw.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Witness Is the Heart**

The verse names the organ of the great vision: *mā kadhaba al-fuʾādu mā raʾā* — *"the heart lied not in what it saw."* The word *fuʾād* — here rendered heart — is, in the precision of Arabic anatomy of the soul, the *inward core* of the *qalb*: the scholars distinguish *qalb* (the heart as the seat of turning and comprehension), *fuʾād* (its burning center, from *fāʾida*, to burn — the furnace of perception and feeling), and *lubb* (the kernel of pure intellect). Al-Rāghib al-Iṣfahānī notes that *fuʾād* is chosen in the Qur'an precisely when perception is *intense* — the father whose eyes whitened with grief is said to have a *burning* fuʾād (12:84); and here, the most intense perception ever granted a creature is attributed to a fuʾād that *did not lie*. The grammarians add the force of the double *mā*: the negation is total and the object is repeated — what it saw, *that very thing*, the heart did not falsify. This is not the language of dreams and guesswork; it is the language of courtroom attestation applied to the innermost perceptive faculty of the best of men.

**The Great Debate: Did the Prophet See His Lord?**

No verse of the Qur'an has been debated more earnestly in the houses of ḥadīth than this one, and the debate is itself a treasure of the tradition, recorded with names and arguments in Muslim's Ṣaḥīḥ (162–177). Two Companions stood opposed. Ibn ʿAbbās held that the Prophet *saw his Lord* — citing this very sūrah: *"And indeed he saw him another time, at the lote tree of the boundary — the heart lied not in what it saw"* (53:13–14, 11). Ibn Masʿūd held that the Prophet saw *Gabriel* — six hundred wings and all — but not his Lord. ʿĀʾishah was asked by Masrūq whether Muhammad saw his Lord, and Muslim records her startled answer:

> *"My hair stood on end at what you said! Have you not heard: 'Sight comprehends Him not, but He comprehends all sight' (6:103) and: 'It is not for any human being that God should speak unto him, save by revelation, or from behind a veil' (42:51)? No one among His servants shall see Him until he dies."* (Muslim 169)

And Muslim preserves the report in which the Prophet himself, asked *had he seen his Lord*, answered:

> *"[He is] Light — how could I see Him?"* (Muslim 167)

The scholars from al-Ṭabarī to Ibn Taymiyyah weighed the reports, and the sound position that carried the majority — adopted by al-Nawawī, Ibn Ḥajar, and the ḥadīth masters generally, and defended in depth by Ibn Taymiyyah in his creeds and fatwas — is this: *the Prophet saw his Lord in the fullest real sense that a creature can see Him, but not with the encompassment of the eye upon the Essence.* Ibn Taymiyyah's formulation: the seeing was real and with the heart (and by the soundest reading, with the eye that saw what the heart saw), but its object was a *self-disclosure* — a *tajallī* — suited to the creature's bearing; for God is not seen *as created things are seen*, circumscribed by direction and distance. Al-Rāzī's summary: the Companions differed over the *mode*; none differed over the *reality* of what the Prophet beheld.

**The Two Reports That Guard Both Truths**

The ḥadīth corpus hands down the two formulas the ummah has balanced ever since. On one side, the Prophet's own words preserved by Muslim:

> *"His veil is Light. Were He to remove it, the splendors of His Face would burn all that His sight reaches of creation."* (Muslim 179)

This teaches transcendence: between eyes and Essence stands a veil of light, and the Essence behind it would consume every gaze. On the other side, the report the exegetes cite for those who affirm the vision:

> *"The light of my vision was placed in my heart; then my heart gazed upon Him."* (a report transmitted in the commentaries of al-Ālūsī, Ibn Kathīr, and al-Qurṭubī)

This teaches reality: the vision was not abolished but *transposed* — the eye of the heart saw what the constitution of the eye cannot. The scholars' synthesis, which Ibn Taymiyyah states most fully: the Prophet saw the highest of God's self-disclosures — real, with heart and eye together, as Ibn ʿAbbās held — yet he did not and could not compass the Essence Itself, as ʿĀʾishah and Ibn Masʿūd insisted. Both parties spoke truth and differed in wording; the ummah's safest creed repeats both truths in both words.

**Why the Heart and Not the Eye Is the Issue**

The verse's choice to certify the *heart* rather than the eye answers the deepest accusation against all religion: that its experiences are projections. The Quraysh had already begun the argument that would be repeated by every skeptic after them — a man in a cave, a vision, a book; who can verify an inner experience? The verse answers with an anatomy of truthfulness: the heart — the very faculty a skeptic would suspect — is declared incapable of falsification *in this man*, because in him the heart had been purified of the causes that make hearts lie. What makes a heart report falsely? Fear that distorts, desire that embellishes, ignorance that fills gaps with imagination, self-interest that edits. The Qur'an had already certified this heart's cleansing: *"...that thou mayest warn a people whose fathers were not warned"* and, explicitly, *"We revealed upon thee... " and "the Trustworthy Spirit descended with it upon thy heart"* (26:193). Al-Ghazālī, in his science of the heart's diseases, explains that only the sick heart hallucinates its desires; the *sound* heart (*qalb salīm*) perceives what is set before it as a mirror perceives a face. The verse thus attests not merely an event but a *calibrated instrument*: this heart had been polished until what it saw, it reported exactly.

**The Certainty That Stands Behind the Book**

For every Muslim who has ever wondered how the Qur'an can be trusted across fourteen centuries, this verse is the answer's keystone. The Book in a believer's hands passed from Gabriel to a heart that did not lie, from that heart to a tongue the Qur'an itself certified (*"nor does he speak out of caprice,"* v. 3), from that tongue to companions who were obsessed with exact transmission, from them through generations of named, graded, scrutinized transmitters — the isnād system the Muslims built *because* verses like this commanded them to treat the chain as sacred. Al-Bukhārī opens his Ṣaḥīḥ with the chapter on how revelation began precisely so that his collection would stand on this foundation: the first link of the chain is a heart certified by God Himself. The scholars express it with the maxim: *the credibility of the whole is the credibility of its first link* — and the first link here is not a legend, not a community's retrojection, but the personal, unshareable, God-attested truthfulness of one heart: *the heart lied not in what it saw.*

**What It Means That the Heart Sees at All**

There remains the verse's quiet gift to every human being: the Qur'an here confirms, in the strongest possible terms, that *the heart sees*. Modern people are taught to locate all perception in the brain and all value in the senses; the Qur'an insists that beneath the senses operates a deeper perceiver, capable of beholding realities the retina cannot register — and that this perceiver can be *trusted or corrupted*, cleaned or stained. The Prophet said: *"Beware, there is a piece of flesh in the body: if it is sound, the whole body is sound; if it is corrupt, the whole body is corrupt — truly it is the heart"* (al-Bukhārī 52; Muslim 1599). The believer's lifelong labor — prayer, repentance, remembrance, honesty — is the maintenance of this inner eye. And the incentive to that labor stands at the head of this chapter: there exists a seeing that does not lie; it can be cultivated; and the first man to see with it perfectly has left us the road he traveled — the road that ends, for those who walk it, at a nearer seeing still: *"That day faces shall be radiant, gazing upon their Lord"* (75:22–23).

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:12

> **Do you then dispute with him as to what he saw?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse Turns to the Jury**

The first eleven verses established the facts: the messenger did not stray, did not err, did not speak from caprice; revelation descended upon him through a teacher of awesome power who stood upon the highest horizon, drew near, and was revealed to within two bows' length; the heart that received it all did not lie. Now, at verse 12, the courtroom turns to the observers: *a-fa-tumārūnahu ʿalā mā yarā* — *"do you then dispute with him over what he sees?"* The verb *tumārūna* (root *m-r-y*, the same root as *murūr* of doubt and the river Murrayn) denotes not polite inquiry but *contentious opposition* — the wrangling of a party determined not to be persuaded. Al-Jalālayn and Ibn Kathīr identify the addressees: those who denied the Prophet's vision of Gabriel — including the Quraysh who heard of the six-hundred-winged form and called him a liar — and those who denied the Night Journey and Ascension outright. Al-Ālūsī hears in the verb the full register of resistance: dispute, opposition, the refusal that masquerades as doubt. The verse's rhetorical structure is devastating in its brevity: *you* — who saw nothing, heard nothing, and were absent from every moment of it — will *dispute* with *him* — the sole eyewitness, certified by the verse before? On what evidence? The question expects no answer, because none exists.

**The Historical Dispute: Makkan Ridicule of the Night Journey**

The most famous denial this verse confronts is recorded in the Ṣaḥīḥ of al-Bukhārī. Abū Hurayrah relates:

> *"The disbelievers of Quraysh came to the Messenger of God ﷺ and disputed with him about the Night Journey. They said: Tell us of Bayt al-Maqdis — how are its gates, its buildings, its surroundings? And God raised Bayt al-Maqdis before him, and he kept looking at it and describing it to them, answering everything they asked. Then they said: Describe our caravans. He described the caravan and its outcome; and I was among them, and I remember when he said: On such-and-such a ridge, they lost a camel which they were seeking — and it was so."* (al-Bukhārī 3886)

Ibn Kathīr and the sīrah historians add the detail of Abū Jahl's mockery: when news of the claim spread, he announced to Quraysh's assembly, *"Muhammad claims he prayed last night in Bayt al-Maqdis! Who will go and ask him?"* — expecting the details to trap him, and being trapped instead. And the Quraysh fetched their best interrogator, the returned traveler Abū Bakr ibn Abī Quḥāfah, expecting him to denounce the claim — whereupon Abū Bakr answered with the sentence that earned him the name al-Ṣiddīq, *the Verifier*: *"If he said it, then it is true. I believe him in what is more amazing than this: the news from heaven in the morning and the evening"* (recorded in the sīrah literature and by al-Bayhaqī). The verse 53:12 stands behind that scene as its eternal transcript: the disputer had only his own blindness; the verifier had the Prophet's twenty-year record.

**The Logic of the Verse: Evidence, Not Volume**

The commentators note the verse's implicit syllogism, which al-Rāzī renders with juristic clarity: a report is only as doubtful as its reporter is unreliable; the reporter has been established as *incapable* of straying, error, and caprice; therefore disputing his report is not skepticism but *insolence*. There is a moral anatomy here the scholars develop: doubt in revelation is almost never an evidentiary problem; it is a *submission* problem. The Quraysh did not lack data — they had the Prophet's known honesty, the Qur'an's inimitability, the accuracy of his predictions, the transformation of his community; what they lacked was the willingness to obey what acceptance would cost them: their idols, their ancestral prestige, their markets. Hence the Qur'an names their doubt precisely: *"They say: We hear, and we hear; had they listened and submitted"* — the verb *yasmaʿūna* repeated for the deafness of will (8:21). Ibn Taymiyyah's aphorism, built on verses of this kind: *the obstacle between most men and truth is not the intellect but the heart's refusal of its consequences.* Verse 12 exposes the refusers by the very word used of them: they are not seekers who failed to find; they are disputants who never intended to be answered.

**When the Witnesses Are Few: The Sociology of Unseen Claims**

There is a modern temptation the verse disarms in advance: *such claims are unfalsifiable because they had no witnesses.* The scholars answer with the structure of all revelation: prophecy is by its nature an office of *singular* reception — no scripture in the history of religion was delivered before parliaments. But singular reception does not mean *untestable* transmission: the Prophet's claims came with public, checkable consequences — the Qur'an's language, the transformation of Yathrib, the predicted victories, the detailed accuracy about Bayt al-Maqdis and the caravan. Abū Bakr's reasoning is the model of rational faith: the Prophet had been right in everything verifiable; the rational move is to extend trust to the unverifiable, not to suspend trust retroactively. Every human being practices this daily — no one has seen his own great-grandparents, yet no one doubts his own existence; testimony chains are the currency of all knowledge. The Qur'an's point in verse 12 is that the chain behind *this* testimony is more certified than any other in human history — certified by God in the verses immediately preceding.

**The Believer's Own Courtroom**

The verse also equips the believer for his own inner disputes. Every soul hears reports from its own unseen — the whisper that prayer is useless, that the Hereafter is remote, that this one life is all. Those whispers are, in the grammar of this verse, *tumār* — disputes lodged against testimony. The scholars counsel meeting them with the same procedure the verse models: examine the reporter (is the whisperer — the caprice of verse 3, the conjecture of verse 23 — trustworthy? has it ever been right?), then weigh the credentials of revelation (does its account of the world hold at every verifiable point?), then rest. Al-Ghazālī named this the *alphabet of certainty*: the believer who has settled the credentials of the Reporter lives in peace about the reports. And the verse's addressee-history gives the final comfort: the disputes of the Quraysh are over; their city, their idols, and their objections are dust; the testimony they disputed is recited this evening in every timezone of the earth. History has already graded the exam: the disputer of verse 12 has a poor record, and the heart of verse 11 an unbroken one.

**The Sequence of the Chapter: Dispute Answered by a Second Vision**

The verse is not the end of the argument; it is its hinge. Having exposed the disputers, the Qur'an immediately multiplies the evidence: *And indeed he saw him another time* (v. 13) — a second sighting, at the lote tree of the boundary, with the Garden of refuge beside it. The rhetorical principle, which jurists call *tawātur* (successive, independent corroboration), is being built into the chapter's own architecture: one vision might be questioned by the obstinate; two visions, at two times, in two modes, corroborated by the Prophet's unblemished record and God's own testimony — this the Qur'an leaves without excuse. The next verses will walk the reader to the lote tree; the disputers are invited to come along, and to notice, on the way, how much evidence accumulates at every step.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:13

> **And indeed he saw him another time,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Second Witness Event**

The verse testifies to a repetition: *wa laqad raʾāhu nazlatan ukhrā* — *"and indeed he saw him another time, a second descent."* The particle *laqad* opens with an oath-grade emphasis — *la-* — as though the Qur'an will not let the fact be weighed without its full weight attached. Al-Jalālayn, al-Ṭabarī, and Ibn Kathīr converge on the two occurrences: the Prophet ﷺ saw Gabriel in his true form *twice* — once at the inauguration of revelation, when the great angel appeared on the horizon at Mount Ḥirāʾ; and a second time during the Night Journey and Ascension, at the lote tree of the boundary. On the alternative reading — that of Ibn ʿAbbās and those who affirm the vision of the Lord — the verse records *two separate visions of God*: once at the rending of the highest veil, and once at the lote tree (the reading al-Ālūsī transmits from the mystic commentators). Under either reading, the verse's function is identical and cumulative: what was seen once (verses 6–12) was seen *again* — and testimony that repeats is testimony that cannot be dismissed as a single disordered moment.

**The Vocabulary of Descent: Nazlah**

The word for the occurrence is *nazlah*, from *n-z-l*, the root of *tanzīl* — the sending-down of revelation — and of *munzil*, the way-station where a caravan *descends* and alights. Al-Rāghib al-Iṣfahānī notes that *nazlah* in Arabic idiom names the *descent of a traveler to a place of stay*: the arrival, the settling, the temporary lodging. The Qur'an thus describes the great visions in the vocabulary of *visiting* — the unseen realm has way-stations, and its inhabitants *travel* to the Prophet as messengers travel between courts. The grammar of the phrase *nazlatan ukhrā* — "a descent, another one" — carries the indefinite-then-cardinal structure the Arabs use to enumerate experiences: *an occurrence, a second one*, as a witness enumerates events he can distinguish and recount separately. The scholars note the evidentiary point: hallucination and dream blur together; testimony distinguishes. The Prophet's account did not say "such things happened"; it said: once at Ḥirāʾ, and a second time at such a place, with such and such features — and the two accounts were consistent across years.

**Why Two, and Why These Two**

The commentators ask why revelation records *two* sightings and no more, and answer with the symmetry of the mission. The first vision, at Ḥirāʾ, belongs to the *beginning*: it launched the mission, overwhelmed the Prophet, and was witnessed by no one but him and his wife who found him trembling — *"Cover me, cover me!"* (al-Bukhārī 3). Its purpose was private authentication: the vessel of revelation needed to know, beyond all doubt, that the voice commanding him was not madness, not a jinn, not a monk's ghost, but the messenger of the Lord of the worlds. The second vision, at the lote tree, belongs to the *summit*: it crowned the Ascension, and its purpose was public certification of the Prophet's supreme journey — for he would return to Makkah and claim before mocking enemies that he had traveled to Jerusalem and up through the seven heavens in one night. Al-Rāzī observes the pedagogy: God did not show the Prophet a stream of wonders, which would have made faith in the unseen a matter of spectacle; He showed *two*, precisely placed, and then said to the Prophet and to all prophets' communities what He says in the Qur'an: *"We show them Our signs in the horizons and in themselves until it becomes clear to them that it is the truth"* (41:53). Spectacle authenticates the messenger; signs sustain the believer.

**The Third Reading: The Two Visions of the Lord**

For the school of Ibn ʿAbbās, the two sightings are two *tajalliyāt* — two self-disclosures of God to the Prophet, one at the *rending of the highest veil*, one at the *lote tree*. Al-Ālūsī records how the mystic commentators understood the pairing: the first disclosure certified the Prophet's heart at the mission's origin; the second, at creation's farthest boundary, certified his rank at the mission's zenith. This school reads the whole passage (53:5–18) as the most exalted biography in the Qur'an: a man drawn nearer than any creature, seeing what none other saw, and returning with a Book. Ibn Taymiyyah, while careful about the *mode* of such seeing, affirms the *reality* of the Prophet's unique nearness and vision of the greatest self-disclosure; the difference between the schools is a difference in describing one truth, not in denying or affirming it. For the believer, the practical fruit is stated by the scholars plainly: *the Prophet's authority to speak of the unseen stands on the highest possible eyewitness grade* — he is not transmitting inherited tales about heaven; he went there, in the sound tradition, and described what he saw with the precision of a returning traveler.

**The Psychology of Corroboration**

There is a lesson in the verse for every honest witness of extraordinary truth: the human mind distrusts the unrepeatable. A single overwhelming experience tends, over years, to be reclassified by its own recipient as dream or fancy — this is the ordinary erosion of memory under social pressure. The Quraysh worked this erosion deliberately: for years they offered the Prophet every exit — declare yourself a poet, a soothsayer, a madman, name your price. A fabricator erodes; a liar accommodates. The Prophet held a *second* vision in memory — different in place, mode, and circumstance — and the two held each other fixed, the way two pillars hold an arch. The scholars of ḥadīth note that this is precisely the epistemology of their science: *tawātur*, the mutual corroboration of independent channels, creates the certainty no single channel could. Al-Najm 53:13 is that principle revealed: another time — and the architecture of certainty acquired its second pillar.

**Between the Two Sightings: Twenty Years of Fidelity**

The scholars close with the observation that gives the verse its human weight: between the first sighting at Ḥirāʾ and the second at the lote tree lay years — most authorities say a decade and more — of persecution, boycott, the deaths of Khadījah and Abū Ṭālib, the stoning of Ṭāʾif, hunger, ridicule, and the unbroken daily obedience of prayer. The second vision was not granted to a man resting on the first; it was granted to a man who had *spent* the first — who had believed it wholly and obeyed it totally for years without one further unveiling. Herein the commentators find the eternal order of the spiritual life: *fidelity between unveilings is the price of the next unveiling.* The believer who waits for God to prove Himself before obeying has inverted the order; the order of al-Najm is: obey what you have seen, and what you shall see is multiplied — *"As for those who strive for Us, We will surely guide them to Our ways; truly God is with the doers of good"* (29:69).

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:14

> **at the lote tree of the boundary,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Farthest Landmark of Creation**

The verse now names the place of the second vision: *ʿinda sidrati al-muntahā* — *"by the lote tree of the boundary."* The *sidr* is the lote tree, the hardy, thorned, shade-giving tree of the Arabian and Levantine wilds — its leaves were the soap of the Arabs, its fruit their food in famine, its shade their shelter in the pilgrimage season; no tree is more *ordinary* to them, none more woven into daily life. *Al-muntahā*, from *n-h-y*, means *the end, the terminus, that beyond which there is no passing*. Al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī, and Ibn Kathīr transmit the composite classical description: the lote tree of the boundary stands in the *seventh heaven*, at the edge of the farthest sphere, beside the divine Throne; it is *of the boundary* because created existence ends there — beyond it none of creation passes, and of what lies beyond it none of creation knows. Ibn Masʿūd heard the Prophet describe it in the ḥadīth of the Ascension:

> *"...then I was taken up to the Lote Tree of the Boundary, whose fruits are like the great clay jars of al-Ḥajar and whose leaves are like the ears of elephants."* (al-Bukhārī 4856; recorded also at 3250)

And al-Bukhārī's longer narration adds what the Prophet saw beneath it:

> *"...and there were four rivers: two hidden rivers and two manifest. As for the two hidden, they are two rivers of Paradise; and as for the manifest, they are the Nile and the Euphrates."* (al-Bukhārī 349)

The Qur'an's choice of tree is thus deliberate in two directions at once: the most familiar tree on earth becomes the marker of the most unfamiliar place — creation's ordinary furniture planted at creation's absolute rim.

**Why a Tree Stands at the End of the World**

The scholars have long meditated on the divine wisdom in placing *a tree* — rooted, growing, living — at the boundary of all that is. Al-Ālūsī gathers the strands: the lote tree is the *last living thing* of the created order, the final handiwork visible to a rising traveler, planted as it were at the gate of the Uncreated. Its rootedness preaches permanence within the realm of change: everything below it passes, the tree remains until its appointed end. Its shade and fruit preach provision: even at creation's farthest edge, the signature of the Provider — food, shade, rivers — is printed on the landscape. And the commentators note a tender correspondence: the *sidrah* under whose shade the pilgrims rest at Mina, whose leaves washed the dead, whose thorns guarded the fields of the poor — this homely tree is honored in the seventh heaven, as if to say that the mundane is never far from the majestic in God's ordering: the same Lord who decorated the boundary of heaven made the wilderness of the Ḥijāz livable with the same kind of tree. Ibn Taymiyyah's principle of the divine signs — *the end of one world is the beginning of knowledge of another* — finds its image here.

**The Boundary as Doctrine: The Limits of Creaturely Knowledge**

Theologically, the lote tree teaches what may be called *the mercy of the boundary*. The Qur'an elsewhere fixes the epistemological limits of creation: *"None knows the unseen in the heavens and the earth save God"* (27:65); the angels at the lote tree, per the commentary tradition, are given only what God appoints for them; even Gabriel, the constant companion of revelation, halted there. Ibn Kathīr emphasizes that in the Ascension narration the Prophet proceeded *past* the lote tree by God's leave — a unique permission — while Gabriel stopped, saying in the famous words of the tradition: *"This is my station; if I advance one step I will be burned"* (a report the commentators transmit from the early narrations of the Ascension). Al-Rāzī draws the doctrine: every creature has a *muntahā* — a terminus of knowledge, power, and approach — and humility is the acceptance of one's terminus; rebellion is the demand to pass it. The modern project of unlimited knowledge reads this verse as a map, not a taunt: there *are* limits, and they are not an insult but an architecture — the boundary is what makes a house a house and a creature a creature.

**The Garden of Refuge Beside It**

The verse's companion (v. 15) tells what lies beside the boundary-tree: *the Garden of the refuge* — *al-jannata al-maʾwā* — which the commentators, following al-Ṭūsī and the early reports, identify with the *seventh heaven* itself or with the supreme Garden abutting it: the lodging (*maʾwā* = the place of resort) of the spirits of the prophets, the truthful, the witnesses, and the righteous, per the Qur'an's own ranking: *"Whoso obeys God and the Messenger — they are with those upon whom God has bestowed favor: the prophets, the truthful, the witnesses, and the righteous; what excellent companions they are"* (4:69). Al-Qurṭubī notes the scholars' discussion of whether this Garden is the Paradise of the Hereafter or its highest antechamber; either way, its placement is the point: *refuge stands at the boundary*. The road of creation ends at a tree; and beside the tree is a garden — so that the last word of the created realm, before the threshold of the Uncreated, is not terror but *hospitality*. The believer reads the geography as promise: at the end of the world's hardest road there is shade, rivers, and rest.

**Boundary, Journey, and the Believer's Own Horizon**

The practical fruit the commentators harvest is a discipline of *stopping where God stops*. The Qur'an models it within the same passage: what covered the tree will be described only as *that which covered* (v. 16); the gaze will be praised for neither swerving nor transgressing (v. 17) — for *transgression* here includes the eye's attempt to see beyond what was disclosed. The believer's speech about heaven must follow the same rule: say what revelation says; fall silent where it falls silent. Ibn Taymiyyah made this the method of all sound theology: *worship God by what He describes, and halt where the revelation halts* — for the demand to see beyond the veil is not courage but trespass. In an age that maps ever-farther galaxies and yet starves for meaning, the lote tree stands as the Qur'an's balanced cosmology: pursue the horizons — *"We will show them Our signs in the horizons and in themselves"* (41:53) — and know that every horizon of creation ends at a boundary which only reverence crosses, and that beyond reverence lies what no instrument will ever photograph: the presence of the Lord of the tree.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:15

> **by which lies the Garden of the refuge,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Refuge as the Final Word of the Created Realm**

The verse completes the geography of the second vision with one clause that changes its entire emotional temperature: *ʿindahā jannatu al-maʾwā* — *"by which lies the Garden of the refuge."* The lote tree of the previous verse marked the boundary — the *muntahā*, the terminus of creation. Had the Qur'an stopped there, the image of heaven's edge would be an image of a wall: the end of the road, the last landmark, the point where even Gabriel is burned back. Instead, the very next verse places a *garden* beside the boundary — *jannah*, the walled enclosure of shade and water that for the desert Arab was the whole vocabulary of joy — and names it *al-maʾwā*: the place of *return, resort, final refuge*, from *a-w-y*, to take shelter. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī record the early readings: the Garden of refuge is the seventh heaven itself (the reading of al-Ṭūsī); or it is the abode wherein the spirits of the prophets, the truthful, the witnesses, and the righteous take refuge, per the Qur'anic catalog of 4:69; or it is the highest Garden adjoining Paradise proper. The variety of the identifications teaches the same core: *beyond the last limit of the world stands not emptiness but abode.*

**The Grammar of Nearness: ʿIndahā**

The preposition is *ʿinda* — *by it, beside it, in its vicinity* — and the commentators savor its precision. The Garden is not *beyond* the boundary (where nothing of creation passes) nor the tree's mere ornament; it is *adjacent* — the first reality at the edge. Al-Rāzī reflects on the wisdom: God placed the reward of the righteous at the very border of His realm, as a host places the guest's lodging nearest his own door; the soul that reaches the terminus of all created roads finds the door of the Garden already beside it. The word *maʾwā* itself appears in the Qur'an for the ultimate resort of all return: *"Unto your Lord is the terminus"* — *ilā rabbika al-muntahā* — this very chapter will say at verse 42, and the paired vocabulary (*muntahā/maʾwā*) binds the tree and the Garden into one sentence of doctrine: the end of the road is a Person, and the lodging of the road is His gift.

**Which Garden? The Scholars' Careful Distinctions**

The commentators, with their characteristic precision, distinguish the *jannat al-maʾwā* of this verse from the *jannah* of the Hereafter proper where its three readings are weighed. The first reading makes it the seventh heaven — so that the Ascension narrative's stations run: the Prophet rises through the seven heavens, arrives at the lote tree, and the "garden of refuge" names the *level* of the seventh heaven, the highest created apartment, in which — per the reports al-Qurṭubī cites — the souls of the prophets and martyrs reside until the Resurrection. The second reading makes it the highest Garden of Paradise, contiguous with the boundary, whose rivers — the two hidden rivers of the ḥadīth — flow beneath the lote tree. The third reading, preserved in the mystic commentaries, takes *maʾwā* existentially: the Garden is whatever shelter the wayfarer finds in God at the end of his ascent. Ibn Kathīr's treatment holds the readings together without forcing them: the names in the Qur'an often shade into one another because the realities they name are one reality approached from below — the seventh heaven, the Garden, the refuge of spirits are stations of one Homecoming. The believer who recites this verse is not being asked to resolve the cartography; he is being told *the direction in which all his dead have gone and all his hopes must travel*: up, past the tree, toward the garden by the boundary.

**The Qur'an's Architecture of Gardens**

The word *jannah* dominates the Qur'an — it appears over a hundred and fifty times — and its placements are never casual. The first garden in the Qur'an is the Garden of Eden, lost by a father's slip (2:35–36); the last gardens are the Gardens of Eden of the Hereafter, regained by a community's obedience (9:72); and here, at the summit of the Ascension, stands the Garden of Refuge, garden of the *departed righteous*. The scholars note the Qur'anic consolation in this arrangement: the Garden that was lost at the beginning of the human story is re-hung at the very boundary of heaven, where the first to arrive are the prophets and the truthful — Adam's exile is answered by Abraham's descendants' homecoming. And the *maʾwā* of the righteous dead answers the hardest grief of the living: Ibn ʿAbbās was asked where the souls of the believers dwell before the Resurrection, and cited this verse and 4:69 — they are in the Garden of refuge (recorded by al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Abī al-Dunyā in his collection on souls). The mother who has buried a believing child reads this verse as the address of her hope: not the grave, not the void, but the garden by the lote tree, in the seventh heaven, beside the presence of God.

**The Ascension Narration and the Garden's News**

The Ṣaḥīḥ traditions of the Ascension carry the believer's eye past the tree into the geography the Prophet was shown. Al-Bukhārī's narration places the four rivers at the lote tree, two hidden (of Paradise) and two manifest (the Nile and Euphrates) — a startling doctrine of continuity: *the rivers of the Gardens and the rivers of the earth are kin*, the earthly waters being, per the commentators' reading of the narration, offshoots of the heavenly. Muslim's narration has the Prophet describe Paradise itself: *"Its soil is musk, its stones are pearls, its vegetation glorifies God"*; and its gateway promise: *"In it is what no eye has seen, no ear has heard, and has not occurred to the heart of any man"* (al-Bukhārī 4779; Muslim 2824 — the Prophet's commentary on 32:17). The Garden of refuge is the threshold of all this. The scholars draw the sermon the Qur'an intends: the Prophet's vision vouchsafes the reality of the reward; the daily religion of the believer — the small honesty, the hidden charity, the tear at dawn — is the road to it; and the road's last landmark is a tree with rivers beneath it and the presence of the Lord beyond it.

**Refuge as Vocation**

One final turn the commentators give the verse: *al-maʾwā* is not only the Garden's name; it is every believer's *destination-designation* — the Qur'an names the Hereafter the true abode: *"and the abode of the Hereafter — that is the [true] life"* (29:64). To live as a Muslim is to hold, in every decision, two addresses: the temporary lodging of this world and the refuge at the road's end. The Prophet's community internalized this so deeply that their caliph ʿUmar governed a dominion from a patched cloak and slept, when he could, in the mosque courtyard — his famous counsel: *"Live as strangers in this world; pass through it as passers-by; and take, while you are in it, provisions for beyond it"* (transmitted in the literature of zuhd, and attributed also to Ibn ʿUmar in al-Bukhārī's chapter headings). The verse that places a Garden beside the boundary-tree thus quietly administers the whole economics of desire: the heart that knows where the refuge is spends its striving there — and the heart that forgets will spend it, and be spent, among the trees of this world only.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:16

> **when there covered the lote tree that which covered.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Deliberately Does Not Describe**

This is one of the most stylistically extraordinary verses in the Qur'an: *mā yaghshā al-sidrata mā yaghshā* — *"that which covered the lote tree [was] that which covered."* The Arabic repeats the same verb-phrase — *mā yaghshā... mā yaghshā* — literally *"what it covered, it covered"* — using the indefinite relative *mā* ("that which") twice, defining the covering *by itself*. Al-Zamakhsharī, the master of Qur'anic rhetoric, explains the figure: the repetition with the indefinite pronoun declares the referent *ineffable* — it cannot be named, only pointed at; the sentence is an eloquent shrug of the tongue before an object beyond its range. Al-Ālūsī and al-Zamakhsharī both note that the structure itself communicates: language *reaches* the tree and *fails* at the covering. This is not a poet's coyness; it is revelation teaching its own limits — the Qur'an, the most eloquent of speech, confesses before this scene that its instruments end.

**The Two Great Readings: Splendor and Spectacle**

The classical commentators transmit two principal readings of the covering, and both are preserved with care. The first, recorded by al-Ṭūsī and cited by the Study Quran's sources: the tree was covered with *light and brilliance, beauty and purity, pleasing to the eye beyond all description* — the covering was a radiance. The second, transmitted by al-Qurṭubī, Ibn Kathīr, and al-Ālūsī from the early narrations: the tree was covered with *angels* — a host beyond count, described in some reports as *golden butterflies* (*farāshti dhahab*), circling and settling upon it like a living mantle of wings; Ibn Masʿūd's narration in al-Bukhārī includes the Prophet's own gesture at this point in the story, and al-Bukhārī's Ascension narration from the Prophet has him say of the tree:

> *"When it was covered by that which covered it, its colors — I knew not what they were."* (al-Bukhārī 349)

The Prophet who could name the fruits (like the jars of al-Ḥajar) and the leaves (like elephants' ears) here halts at the *colors* of the covering: sight had arrived where vocabulary departed. The two readings — splendor and angels — harmonize in the tradition: the angels themselves are made of light, and their circling masses *are* the brilliance; the mantle of the tree is a mantle of living worship.

**The Echo at the End of the Sūrah**

One of the most beautiful observations the commentators make is that this same phrase *mā ghāshā* — *that which covered* — returns at verse 54 of this very chapter, in the opposite emotional key: *"such that there covered them that which covered"* (53:54), said of the destroyed city of Lot. The Qur'an thus builds an intentional rhyme across forty verses: one covering of *glory* at the summit of the Ascension; one covering of *ruin* upon a city of sin; and between them the whole moral history of man. Al-Rāzī notes the lesson in the pairing: created reality has coverings of both kinds, and which covering descends upon a soul is decided not by chance but by its response to revelation. The same universe that mantles the obedient with indescribable splendor buries the defiant under indescribable terror — and the double *mā yaghshā* in both places tells us that both are beyond description, glory and horror alike out-running the tongue. The believer learns to ask, with trembling and hope, only one question: *which covering is being prepared for me?*

**Ineffability as a Qur'anic Method**

The commentators widen the lens: the Qur'an repeatedly marks its highest content with the grammar of the indescribable. Of the Hereafter's joys: *"No soul knows what has been hidden for them of the delight of eyes, as a recompense for what they used to do"* (32:17) — and the Prophet's gloss: *"which no eye has seen, no ear has heard, and has not occurred to the heart of any man"* (al-Bukhārī 4779; Muslim 2824). Of God's own self: *"[He is] Light — how could I see Him?"* (Muslim 167). Al-Ghazālī built his theology of the divine beauty on this pattern: the greatest realities announce themselves by *defeating* description, and the defeat is itself information — the mind learns its size at the frontier of the unsayable. In modern terms, the Qur'an practices what philosophers of language would later call the *apophatic discipline*: to speak truly of the transcendent is to mark the places where speech stops. The believer is thus immunized against two errors at once: the crudity that pictures the unseen as merely bigger versions of the seen, and the despair that concludes the unseen is nothing at all because it cannot be pictured. The verse says: *more than you can say — and not, therefore, less than real.*

**The Psychology of the Veiled Splendor**

Why does God veil such scenes rather than publish them? The commentators answer from the purposes of faith. Ibn Taymiyyah's principle: faith in the unseen is the *substance* of worship — *"those who believe in the unseen"* (2:3) is the first description of the reverent — and were the veil lifted for all, faith would collapse into sight and worship into coercion; the veil preserves the dignity of choice. Yet the veil is *partial* by mercy: enough was shown — through one certified heart — to make the reality undeniable to the trusting, while the obstinate retain their refusal. Al-Ālūsī adds the psychological note: a constant spectacle would also *cheapen* — wonders repeated become wallpaper; the rarity of the unveiling is what keeps it burning in memory and driving the tradition's yearning. The Muslim's spiritual life lives on this economy: a veil, two visions, one Book, five prayers, and a promise.

**For the Reader: The Etiquette of Silence**

The practical adab the verse teaches was stated by Ibn Taymiyyah as a rule of theology and is repeated by the scholars as a rule of life: *speak of the unseen only as far as the revelation speaks, and then be silent with it.* The believer does not invent what the angels looked like, nor vote on what covered the tree, nor trim the verse to fit his imagination; he recites *mā yaghshā... mā yaghshā* — and lets the recitation itself carry him to the frontier where the greatest of creatures stood sightless before colors he could not name. There is a restfulness in that frontier the modern world never offers: every screen demands we look, comment, and caption; the Qur'an teaches that the highest honor of the eye is sometimes to reach a beauty and *stop* — as the Prophet stopped, as Gabriel stopped at the boundary — and to let the silence of the tongue be the prostration of the mind. The next verse will praise exactly that discipline in the Prophet's gaze: *it swerved not, nor transgressed.*

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:17

> **The gaze swerved not; nor did it transgress.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Character Reference for an Eyeball**

The verse is, on its face, the strangest kind of testimony: a character reference for a *gaze*. *Mā zāgha al-baṣaru wa mā ṭaghā* — *"the sight swerved not, nor did it transgress."* Two verbs in the negative, both personifying the eye: *zāgha* (from *z-w-y*, to decline, to turn aside — the root of *inzilāf*, deviation, and of the crescent's slant) and *ṭaghā* (from *ṭ-gh-w*, to exceed bounds — the root of *ṭughyān*, tyranny and deluge, the very word for the flooded injustice of Pharaoh, 20:24). Al-Zamakhsharī notes the twin structure: one verb forbids the eye's *deviation* (looking away), the other forbids its *excess* (looking beyond). Between them they fence the perfect act of seeing: *steady and bounded*. The Qur'an certified the Prophet's heart (v. 11); now it certifies his eye — not because eyes usually lie, but because before an overwhelming spectacle the eye *does* usually fail in one of these two directions: it flinches away, or it grasps beyond what is given. His did neither.

**The First Reading: Steadfast Before the Wonders**

The commentators, led by al-Ālūsī and the early tradition, first read the verse as praise of the Prophet's *composure before the unveiled*: when the horizon filled with the six-hundred-winged form, when the lote tree was mantled in its indescribable covering, his gaze did not *swerve* — it did not bolt, blink away, or wander in panic; he *looked*, deliberately, steadily, as a witness must. The scholars note the psychological realism: terror and awe normally destroy observation — the witness of a catastrophe reports fragments, the child before the lightning covers his face. The Prophet's gaze held. And it did not *transgress*: he did not strain beyond what was disclosed, did not attempt to peel the veil, did not demand the Essence behind the self-disclosure. Al-Ṭūsī's summary: *"he did not turn away from the truth he sought, and he did not seek beyond what was given him."* The verse thus certifies the two virtues of the perfect witness: *courage* (he looked) and *discipline* (he stopped).

**The Second Reading: The Gaze of the Heart**

The mystic commentators extend the verse from the eye of the head to the eye of the heart — *baṣar* to *baṣīrah*. On their reading, the verse teaches the etiquette of every seeker at the frontier of the divine: the inner gaze must neither *swerve* from truth under the pressure of awe (the rationalization by which seekers flee what overwhelms them) nor *transgress* by claiming what was not given (the fabrication by which mystics inflate their experiences). Ibn Taymiyyah, ever the guardian of sound method, applies the same rule to theology: the believer's intellect must neither deviate from the revealed truth (innovation as deviation) nor exceed it (speculation as trespass); the straight path is as narrow as the verse's two negations. Al-Rāzī observes that the Qur'an thereby defines *obedience* itself: to receive what is given, neither fleeing nor grasping — the posture of Gabriel's own *"I do not descend except by command"* and of the believer's whole religion, which is exactly this: a gaze that stays where God stationed it.

**The Moral Ecology of a Single Glance**

That the Qur'an dedicates a verse to the discipline of *looking* tells the believer that the eye is a moral organ. The Prophet's own teachings build a complete ethics of the gaze around the principle this verse certifies in him: *"Do not follow a glance with a glance: the first is for you, the second is against you"* (transmitted by al-Tirmidhī 2777 and Abū Dāwūd 2149); *"Let no man look at a man's ʿawrah... "*" and the command to lower the gaze in 24:30–31 with its physiological reasoning — *"that it may be purer for them."* The scholars systematized: the eye feeds the heart; the heart's diet is what the eye provides; a guarded gaze is therefore not prudery but *nutrition control* for the inner life. Modern media — engineered to hijack the gaze, to monetize the swerve, to reward the transgression — reads this verse as an indictment in a single line: civilization trains eyes to *zughyān* (swerve) and *ṭughyān* (excess); revelation trained the best eye in history to steadiness and bounds. The believer's practice — lowering the gaze, guarding screens, refusing the addictive scroll — is the daily enactment of verse 17's two little negations.

**Steadiness as the Witness's Vocation**

There is a vocational lesson for every Muslim who would bear witness to truth in hostile rooms. The Prophet's unswerving gaze was not stoicism; it was the composure of a heart already settled in what it knew. The companions inherited the trait in moments history remembers: Bilāl under the rocks of the sand-hill, repeating *"Aḥad, Aḥad"* — *One, One* — with the boulder on his chest (recorded in the sīrah and by Ibn Isḥāq); ʿUmar's bearing before the delegation at his deathbed; the women of the early community, stripped before the tyrant, who did not look away from their faith. The scholars of character note the pattern: *what the eye refuses to flee, the heart refuses to surrender.* Verse 17 is the Qur'an's earliest and highest portrait of that steadiness — and its standard is available at every scale: the student who does not glance away from an unpopular truth in a seminar; the employee who does not swerve from honesty in the audit; the addict who does not allow his eyes the next look. In all of these, the believer walks a few paces behind the Prophet's gaze.

**From the Guarded Eye to the Guarded Tongue**

The verse's position is also its lesson: it stands between the ineffable covering (v. 16) and the certified sign (v. 18) — between what cannot be described and what will now be declared. The Prophet's discipline was to *see fully, describe only what was commanded, and stay silent within the veil*. The scholars generalize this into the ethics of testimony in all fields: the witness, the journalist, the scholar, the parent — all are commanded to report *what they saw*, neither flinching from it (which is dishonesty) nor exceeding it (which is fabrication). The Qur'an's own self-description of its authorship fits here: *"He does not speak from caprice"* (v. 3) governs the tongue; *"the gaze swerved not, nor transgressed"* governs the eye; and the next verse certifies the product of both: *he saw the greatest of the signs of his Lord* — a report whose every word now stands on the twin discipline of a guarded eye and a sealed tongue.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:18

> **Indeed, he saw the greatest of the signs of his Lord.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Summit Verse**

The first movement of the sūrah closes at its apex: *laqad raʾā min āyāti rabbihi al-kubrā* — *"indeed, he saw of the signs of his Lord the greatest."* The oath-particle *laqad* re-appears, as at verse 13, to press the fact upon any lingering doubt. Two grammatical features concentrate the verse's power. First, the partitive *min* — "of the signs" — which the commentators, following al-Jalālayn and al-Ṭabarī, read in two legitimate ways: on the vision-of-Gabriel reading, it may be rendered *some of the greatest signs* (he saw Gabriel, *one* of the greatest signs); on the Ascension reading, *the greatest of the signs* (the superlative *kubrā* gathered in one disclosure). Second, the possessive *rabbihi* — *his* Lord — the possessive of intimacy and honor: these were not *the* signs merely, they were *his*, granted to him personally, as God grants each worshipper his own portion of nearness. Ibn Kathīr notes that with this verse the section begun at "By the star" completes its circuit: the star swore; the star's Lord displayed His greatest sign; the oath is discharged with interest.

**What He Saw: The Two Catalogs**

Al-Jalālayn and al-Ṭabarī record the Gabriel-reading's content: the Prophet saw *some of the greatest signs* when Gabriel stood upon the horizon in his true form with six hundred wings — and, per another report al-Ṭabarī transmits, the green curtain that veiled the horizon at that moment, a spectacle the Prophet mentioned among the wonders of that day. Ibn Kathīr adds the ḥadīth basis: the Prophet's own testimony of Gabriel's wings (al-Bukhārī 3232; Muslim 174) and the revelation's own praise of the angelic realm. The Ascension reading, carried by Ibn ʿAbbās and elaborated by al-Ālūsī from the mystic commentators, fills the verse with a different inventory: the Prophet saw *the wonders of the Dominion* — *malakūt*, the Qur'anic term for the visible world's invisible governance (cf. 6:75, where Abraham is shown *the dominion of the heavens and the earth*) — and *the secrets of the archangelic realm* — *jabarūt*, the level beyond the Dominion, the term of 18:65 traditions and of the verse *"in the name of God, its gate is the Jabarūt"* as the commentators gloss *Mashriqayn wa maghribayn* (55:17). Either way, the verse asserts the same hierarchy: what the Prophet witnessed outranks every sign previously shown to men — greater than the parting sea, the she-camel of Ṣāliḥ, the smoke and the cry; the signs of his Lord that he saw were the *greatest*.

**Signs: Āyāt — The Word of Both Miracle and Verse**

The commentators dwell on the word *āyāt* — signs — because it carries the Qur'an's double usage in one term: *āyāt* names both God's wonders in creation and the *verses* of the Qur'an itself. The verse thus welds what the Prophet *saw* to what mankind would *read*: the greatest sign his Lord showed him is the same category of reality as the Book in the believer's hands. Al-Rāzī draws the epistemological chain: the Prophet was shown the macro-signs of the unseen precisely so that his delivery of the micro-signs (the verses) would carry their authority; conversely, the believer who trusts the verses holds a title-deed to the truth of what the Prophet saw. The Qur'an's own claim fits: *"We will show them Our signs in the horizons and in themselves until it becomes clear to them that it is the truth"* (41:53) — the universe is a commentary on the Book and the Book on the universe, and the Prophet stood at the hinge, having read both at their source.

**The Greatest Sign and the Limits of Comparison**

Ibn Taymiyyah uses this verse in his discussions of prophetic rank: miracles (*muʿjizāt*) differ in kind, and the Prophet's portion — the Qur'an *plus* the Ascension — is such that the tradition calls him *the possessor of the two signs*. Yet the scholars immediately guard the doctrine of God from the verse's grandeur: the *signs* are the greatest, but the *Lord* is not a sign. What the Prophet saw, however exalted — Gabriel in his true form, the lote tree, the covering, the self-disclosure — all of it belongs to the category of *āyāt*: created or created-ordered displays that *point*. The eye that reaches the sign has reached the pointer, not the Referent; the Referent remains behind the veil of light of which the Prophet spoke: *"His veil is Light. Were He to remove it, the splendors of His Face would burn all that His sight reaches"* (Muslim 179). This is the creedal fruit of the whole section: a witnessed summit, and a summit that still points upward.

**Why the Section Ends Here: The Reader's Standing**

The placement of verse 18 is pedagogically perfect: the authentication section ends with a certified maximum — the greatest signs, seen by a certified heart and eye — and the very next verse swings at the idols: *"Have you considered al-Lāt and al-ʿUzzā?"* The rhetorical drop is deliberate, and al-Rāzī names its logic: having seen the ceiling of reality, the reader can never again mistake the floor for the sky. The idols of verse 19 — stones, trees, a star — are about to be weighed against the lote tree, the six hundred wings, the two bows' length; the comparison is not even a contest, and the Qur'an wants the believer to *feel* the disproportion. For the modern reader tempted to negotiate with the idols of his own age — wealth, appetite, self-image, ideology — the sequence is the same sermon: look once, truly, at what stands at the top of the world, and every competing claim shrinks to its actual size: a stone with a name.

**The Believer's Portion of This Verse**

The verse closes, for the attentive reader, with a shareable promise. The Prophet said:

> *"Whoever travels a path seeking knowledge, God makes easy for him a path to the Garden."* (Muslim 2699)

and the mystic commentators added: the *signs* shown to the Prophet in their fullness are shown to his community in their share — in the verse recited with presence, in the sunset that stops the commuter's car, in the stillness of the prostration. No believer will see what the Prophet saw; every believer may see *of* the signs, in his own portion, what makes the same verdict inevitable: the Lord of the lote tree is real, His signs are the greatest, and the star that set above Makkah sets tonight over a world that has never stopped reciting what His servant brought back — the Qur'an, the greatest sign carried by the man who saw the greatest signs.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:19

> **Have you considered al-Lāt and al-ʿUzzā,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Turns from Heaven to the Market of Idols**

At verse 19 the sūrah pivots — from the seventh heaven and the lote tree to two stones and a grove. The change of altitude is the argument: *a-fa-raʾaytumu al-Lāta wa al-ʿUzzā* — *"Have you then considered al-Lāt and al-ʿUzzā?"* The interrogative *a-fa-raʾaytum* — "have you *seen*, have you *considered*" — is the Qur'an's standard courtroom formula for forcing an examination of evidence (*afaraʾayta*: 96:11, 107:1, 109:1). The reader is summoned to inspect the idols as evidence. Al-Rāzī notes the rhetorical descent: the previous eighteen verses displayed the ceiling of reality — the greatest signs of the Lord — precisely so that the objects now placed on the table would be seen at their true size. After two bows' length, a stone is only a stone.

**Al-Lāt: The Goddess of Ṭāʾif**

The classical sources — Ibn al-Kalbī's *Book of Idols*, al-Ṭabarī, and the citations gathered by Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī — preserve the history. Al-Lāt (some derive it from *al-Ilāhah*, "the Goddess") was worshipped at Ṭāʾif, Makkah's cool sister city in the mountains, by the tribe of Thaqīf; her shrine held a white stone with inscriptions, served by a custodian, draped in cloth, ringed by a sacred enclosure where no weapon could be drawn and no game hunted — the Arabs' peace-treaties were made in her courtyard. The Quraysh would send their children to be blessed there, and travelers would shave their heads at her temple as at the Kaʿbah. When Islam conquered Makkah, the destruction of al-Lāt fell to Abū Sufyān and al-Mughīrah ibn Shuʿbah at the heads of their clans — a detail the historians preserve to show that Thaqīf's idol died last and hardest: they asked for a year's delay, then for a respite from its demolition, then begged that they not be made to break it themselves; Abū Sufyān's answer became proverbial among the Muslims: *"Woe to you! By God, the day will come when a woman of Thaqīf will go out adorned, with no lord but God"* — and the white stone was ground to dust and the gold and jewels spent on the Muslims of Madinah.

**Al-ʿUzzā: The Might of Quraysh's Own Idol**

Al-ʿUzzā — "the Most Mighty," a name the scholars connect to al-ʿAzīz, the Divine Name, mutilated by the idolaters' theft of it — was the *most venerated idol of the Quraysh themselves*, the idol of their blood and prestige. Her shrine stood at Nakhlah, on the road between Makkah and Ṭāʾif: three trees, a monument, curtains, and a custodial family (the Banū Shaybah, who hereditarily kept her service). Ibn al-Kalbī records her grim liturgy of blood: the devotees swore oaths upon her, and the evil custom of *ʿazl al-taḥyīr* — the man returning from a journey shaving his head at her shrine — and, most terribly, they *slaughtered their children to her*: her altars drank the blood of firstborns devoted by desperate fathers, a practice the sources confirm and the Qur'an elsewhere condemns: *"Thus their partners made fair-seeming to many of the idolaters the slaying of their children"* (6:137). After the conquest of Makkah, Khālid ibn al-Walīd was dispatched to destroy her; the guardians had hidden the idol, and a naked, disheveled woman — per the narration, the demon of the shrine in its apparent form — came out cursing; Khālid struck her down and burned the trees. The historians record the Prophet's satisfaction at the report: *al-ʿUzzā is no more* — and with her, the ideology that might belongs to stones.

**The Interrogative as Method: Look at What You Worship**

The verse's question — *have you considered?* — teaches the method Islam applied to all false absolutes: *turn them around, inspect them, ask them the questions*. The Qur'an rehearses this inspection across the Book: of the idols, *"Do they create anything? Are they partners in the heavens and the earth? Have they a scrap of knowledge? Do they hear? Do they see? Can they help you or harm you?"* (passim: 7:194–195; 13:16; 34:22–23; 46:4). The inspection is devastating because idols cannot survive *attention* — they thrive on habit and ancestry, not examination. The scholars derive a permanent principle from the interrogative: *the cure for false worship is not first emotional but intellectual* — place the object before the mind and ask what it has ever done. Ibn Taymiyyah notes that the Qur'an's polemic against shirk is essentially this interrogative, repeated across a hundred verses; the Prophet applied it to every claim on worship, whether a stone in Nakhlah or a vanity in the heart: *consider it* — what does it create, what does it hear, what has it ever given you but what you gave it?

**The Bridge Verse: From the Lote Tree to the Two Idols**

The commentators draw the full contrast the placement produces. Verse 18: *the greatest signs of his Lord* — the six-hundred-winged teacher, the boundary-tree, the covering no tongue can carry. Verse 19: *al-Lāt and al-ʿUzzā* — carved stone and dressed trees. Between the two stands the whole argument of revealed religion: reality has a summit, and it is not where the idolaters' devotions are pitched. Al-Biqāʿī observes that the Qur'an could have attacked the idols with mockery alone; instead it *positions* them under the shadow of the Ascension, so that the reader weighs them in heaven's light. This is the Qur'an's general method with falsehood: not merely to forbid, but to *dwarf* — to place the false absolute beside the true one until the false collapses of its own smallness. The believer's lifelong task — the demolition of every rival to God in his own heart — is conducted by the same maneuver: set the rival beside the Lord of the lote tree, and watch it shrink.

**From the Stones to the Names: Setting Up the Next Verse**

The verse's plural — *al-Lāt and al-ʿUzzā* — is only the first roll-call; verse 20 will add Manāt, *the third, the other*, and the three together formed the triad of Arabian idolatry, the very deities whose names would shortly be offered back to God as *daughters* (vv. 21–22) and whose "intercession" the famous interpolation claimed (vv. 19–20c). The sūrah is assembling its target: the most prestigious, the most local, the most beloved idols of the audience — chosen, per Ibn Kathīr, *because they were the most famous* and nearest to Makkah, the ones every listener had kissed, funded, or feared. The chapter that opened by swearing by a star will close its argument at another star (Sirius, v. 49), and between the two celestial bookends it will dissect the idols star, stone, and shrine — beginning, here, with the two whose names still ring in the ears of every reciter of Sūrah al-Najm: al-Lāt, the goddess whose temple made treaties, and al-ʿUzzā, the might whom Quraysh feared to lose — and whom Khālid ibn al-Walīd's sword proved to be nothing at all.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:20

> **and Manāt, the third, the other?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Name: Manāt, the Apportioner**

The roll-call completes: *wa Manāta al-thālithata al-ukhrā* — *"and Manāt, the third, the other."* Manāt's name derives from the root *m-n-y*, *to apportion, to decree, to measure out* — the same root as *al-mawt* (death, the appointed measure) and *al-māniyah* (the apportioned lot), which the pre-Islamic poets used for death itself. The sources — Ibn al-Kalbī, al-Ṭabarī, the citations of al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr — describe her shrine at *Mushallal* near *Qudayd*, on the coastal road between Makkah and Madinah, and her worshippers: the tribes of Khuzaʿah — the Kaʿbah's hereditary gatekeepers of the pre-Islamic era — and, decisively for history, the two Yathrib tribes of *Aws and Khazraj*. The name says the theology: Manāt was the goddess of *fate* — the apportioner of death and lot — and a people who worship fate have made a theology of their own helplessness. The Aws and Khazraj, whose wars with each other (the day of Buʿāth) had bled them for decades, would be the very people who, hearing this verse's sūrah recited, would weep and say *"do not make us the last to follow him"* — and become the Anṣār, the Helpers. The goddess who apportioned their despair would be broken by her own tribesmen's conversion: ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib led the force that smashed Manāt, and the Aws and Khazraj stood in the ranks.

**The Grammar of Contempt: "The Third, the Other"**

The commentators savor the verse's word order, which al-Zamakhsharī calls the Qur'an's most economical insult. In formal enumeration, one says: *the first, the second, and the third*. The Qur'an inverts the tally: Manāt is called *al-thālithah al-ukhrā* — literally *the third, the other* — as though the speaker's lip curls even at the counting: after the two (al-Lāt and al-ʿUzzā), *the third — the other one*. Al-Ālūsī notes the reading of some grammarians that *al-ukhrā* adds the note of *otherness*: the third is of another kind, a thing counted only to be finished with. The rhetorical rule the scholars extract: the Qur'an deflates falsehood by refusing it even the dignity of orderly enumeration — the idols are not presented as a pantheon of three august powers but as a scatter of objects, the last of which barely merits its ordinal. Beneath the grammar lies the monotheist's whole attitude to polytheism: not terror, not fascination — a count that runs out of respect.

**The Triad and the Theology of Daughters**

Together, the three names formed the apex of Arabian polytheism, and the commentators (Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, following the early reports) explain their selection here *because they were the most famous* and nearest to Makkah — the idols every listener had approached. The triad carried a specific blasphemy: the idolaters called them the *daughters of God* — sometimes all three, sometimes al-Lāt and Manāt as daughters of al-ʿUzzā — the claim the next verses will interrogate with surgical irony (*"Unto you males, and unto Him females?"*). The scholars note the sociological detail that makes the blasphemy obscene: the same Arabs who ascribed daughters to God *despised* the birth of daughters among themselves, burying some infant girls alive (16:58–59) — ascribing to heaven the very burden they refused on earth. Manāt, the apportioner, had herself been apportioned a role she never asked for: the role of the "daughter" whom the fathers of Arabia would have smothered at birth had she been born to them. The Qur'an will make the contradiction the centerpiece of its refutation.

**The Infamous Passage: The Story of the Cranes**

Verses 19–20 became the site of the most controversial narrative in the entire commentary tradition: the story of *al-gharānīq*, "the cranes." The early histories — al-Ṭabarī's *Tārīkh*, Ibn Saʿd's *Ṭabaqāt*, al-Wāqidī's sīrah — transmit that when the Prophet recited these two verses in the Kaʿbah's precinct, longing for his people's acceptance, Satan cast upon his tongue an interpolation: *"Those are the high-flying cranes, and their intercession is to be hoped for"* — words that appeared to grant the three idols an honored rank; the Quraysh, overjoyed, prostrated with him at the sūrah's end, and the persecution eased briefly — until the correction descended in Sūrah al-Ḥajj:

> *"...that when he recited, We made it known..."* — the verse reading: *"And no messenger We sent before thee, nor prophet, but that when he recited, Satan cast into his recitation; then God abolishes what Satan casts, and God confirms His signs — and God is Knowing, Wise: that He may make what Satan casts a trial for those in whose hearts is sickness..."* (22:52–53)

The scholars of ḥadīth and history weighed this story for centuries. The analysis, as Ibn Kathīr and the muḥaddithūn state it: the story is *not* established by any sound chain — it is not in the two Ṣaḥīḥs nor in the rigorously authenticated collections — and it *contradicts* the Qur'an's explicit guarantees: *"nor does he speak out of caprice"* (53:3), *"it is naught but revelation revealed"* (53:4), *" falsehood approaches it not from before it nor from behind it, a sending-down from One Wise, Praised"* (41:42), and *"We have not neglected in the Book any thing"* (6:38). The great ḥadīth master Ibn Ḥajar surveys the reports and finds them unsupported; and Ibn Taymiyyah rejects the story decisively in his *Majmūʿ al-Fatāwā*, arguing that what Satan cast upon the *hearing of the listeners* — their hearts and hopes — the verse 22:52 explains, not any corruption of the Prophet's tongue; the report in the Ṣaḥīḥ of Muslim (402) — Ibn Masʿūd's *"I prostrated, and Satan and the idolater and the believer prostrated"* — is explained as Satan's deception of the *crowd*, not of the Prophet. The story survives in the academic literature as a case study in the discipline of transmission: a narrative repeated for centuries remains unestablished without chains, and the Qur'an's own self-certification outranks every anecdote about it.

**Why the Controversy Matters for the Reader**

The scholars draw three lessons from the episode's long career. First: *the authority of any report is its chain*, and the ummah's sciences were built precisely to protect the Prophet's tongue from the embellishments of narrators — the story of the cranes is the demonstration case. Second: the Qur'an's doctrine of prophetic protection is *internal* to the Book — al-Najm certifies its own verses in its own verses (2–4), and no history, however early, can add to or subtract from that certification. Third: Satan's stratagem of *tampering with sacred words in the mouths of the heedless* is a permanent method the believer must recognize — in the Hadith forgeries that proliferated in every century, in the misquotations of Scripture that circulate socially, in the clipped verses of public debate. The defense is the same in every case: return to the authenticated text, the certified chain, the whole verse in its context. The Quraysh who prostrated in their joy and the Muslims who grieved in their confusion were both cured by one thing: the revelation *as revealed* — and so is every generation after them.

**The Roll-Call Ends, the Interrogation Begins**

With Manāt named, the sūrah's evidence table is set: three idols, three tribes' treasures, one theology of fate, one slander of daughters. The question mark that ends the verse is the beginning of the demolition — for the very next breath asks the question that wrecks the triad's theology: *have you considered...?* — and what follows (vv. 21–23) will weigh the idols' names, their gender, and their origin, and find them *naught but names that you have named*. The scholars note the order of the assault: first the objects (vv. 19–20), then their theology (vv. 21–22), then their epistemology (v. 23) — the stones, then the slander, then the source. Idolatry dies in that order still, in every heart: the false absolute must be identified, its logic exposed, and its origin — conjecture and desire — dragged into the light.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:21

> **Unto you males and unto Him females?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question That Indicts a Double Standard**

The verse compresses the idolaters' theology into one interrogative and lets its own absurdity detonate it: *a-lakumu al-dhakaru wa lahu al-unthā* — *"unto you the male, and unto Him the female?"* The structure is a legal cross-examination of the ancestral religion: you assign yourselves — the believers in these goddesses — the *males*, the honored issue of Arabian society; and you assign to *Him* — the pronoun of reverence, the God whose greatness even the idolaters conceded — the *females*, the very thing you yourselves despise. Al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī, and Ibn Kathīr note that the question's grammar is deliberately absurd in two registers. First, the logic of parentage: a parent's honor is defined by offspring; the idolaters had just given God the offspring *they* would not accept — as if honoring Him by insulting Him, a contradiction their own values condemn. Second, the logic of proportion: the creature claims the *better* portion of the Creator — the dust claims sons, and the Lord of the Throne is assigned daughters. Al-Rāghib al-Iṣfahānī notes that in Arab usage this construction (*lakum... wa lahu...*) always exposes an *unfair division* — and the next verse names the division with the technical word for fraudulent apportionment.

**The Historical Reality: Daughters in the Jahiliyyah**

The verse lands with full force only against the social history the sources preserve. The pre-Islamic Arabs' contempt for daughters was not private embarrassment but public institution.
