The commentators cite the practice of *waʾd* — female infanticide — in its two forms: the girl buried alive in the desert at birth (81:8–9: *"when the buried girl shall be asked: for what sin was she slain?"*), and the girl kept alive only until she could fetch her father, then taken into the wastes and buried (as al-Daḥḥāk's poetry and the sīrah's biographies record; Ṭarafah's verse *"were it not for the dogs around me... I would bury her"* — the sentiment, if not always the deed, of a culture). Into this world, the Qur'an spoke the verses that reversed a civilization: *"When one of them is given tidings of a female, his face blackens and he suppresses his grief — hiding from the people because of the ill-tidings: shall he keep it in humiliation or bury it in the dust? Evil indeed is what they judge!"* (16:58–59). The scholars mark the revolutionary sequence: first the Qur'an *forbade* the killing (*"slay not your children for fear of poverty; We provide for you and for them,"* 6:151), then *named* the buried girl to face her killer on Judgment Day (81:8–9), then *assigned* to daughters a share of inheritance (4:11–12) when the world's lawgivers gave them nothing — and here, in verse 21, *turned the insult back upward*: the God you claim honored with daughters — you, who bury them?

**The Blasphemy of Ascribing Gender to the Transcendent God**

Beyond the social indictment stands the theological one, and the scholars are precise. God is *not gendered*: He is *al-Ṣamad*, the Absolute upon whom all depend, without issue, without parent — *"He begets not, nor was He begotten"* (112:3), the very sūrah revealed before this one, as the introduction noted. The grammatical masculine of Arabic (*huwa*, "He") is the language's neutral default, not an anatomy; the commentators emphasize that all the names and attributes of God are transcendent above the categories of sex. The idolaters' error was thus double: they imported a *creaturely* category (gender) into the Creator, and they imported it with their own *social prejudice* attached — making the divine family a mirror of their village's contempt. Ibn ʿAbbās's school and the commentators after them connect this verse to its siblings across the Book: *"They said: God has taken a son. Glory be to Him! Nay — to Him belongs what is in the heavens and the earth"* (2:116); *"Exalted be the Lord of the heavens and the earth, the Lord of the Throne, above what they describe"* (43:82); *"And they assign to God daughters — glory be to Him! — and for themselves what they desire"* (16:57). The pattern is diagnosed exactly: *for themselves what they desire* — the theology of a people is the confession of their appetites.

**The Irony as Argument**

Al-Zamakhsharī, the great stylist, identifies this verse among the Qur'an's masterpieces of *irony-as-proof* (iʿtirāḍ bi'l-istihzāʾ): the argument does not run through premises; it runs through the listener's own values. The Arabs did not need a lesson in theology to feel the indecency of the arrangement — they needed only to hear it stated plainly: *you the males, He the females*. Their own double standard — sons treasured, daughters buried, yet daughters reserved for heaven — collapsed audibly in the marketplace the moment the sentence was completed. The commentators note the psychological method: revelation often refutes not by information the opponent lacks but by *holding his own principles before his eyes*. This is why the Qur'an's questions are so devastating — *"Do you know better, or does God?"* (2:140), *"Is then He who creates as he who creates not?"* (16:17) — they recruit the opponent's conscience as the prosecution's witness. The believer learns the method for his own self-examination: hold your stated values beside your actual practice, and read the contradiction aloud; it is often the shortest road to repentance.

**The Gender Question Read Correctly**

Because this verse stands in the modern debate about women's dignity, the scholars' reading must be stated carefully. The verse does *not* depreciate the female; it *depreciates the insult* of ascribing to God what the ascribers themselves held cheap. Indeed, the same Qur'an that asks this question also honors the female beyond every contemporary culture: it names *more women* in its text than any other scripture of its size — Maryam has a sūrah; the Queen of Sheba leads her nation to faith; Pharaoh's wife, Moses' mother, and the believing wife of a tyrant are held up as models (66:11); and the Prophet's counsel — *"the best of you are the best to your wives"* (al-Tirmidhī 3895, graded ṣaḥīḥ), and *"whoever has a daughter and honors her... they will be a shield for him from the Fire"* (al-Bukhārī 1418, in the wording of the Ṣaḥīḥ's narrations on kindness to daughters; cf. Muslim 2629) — made the custody of daughters a gate of Paradise. The verse's logic, correctly read, *elevates* the female: the very category the idolaters used to insult God is the category God uses to dignify His righteous servants — the Qur'an's own image of the believers of both sexes is one verse long: *"men and women who are humble... men and women who fast... men and women who remember God much — for them God has prepared forgiveness and a great reward"* (33:35).

**From the Division to the Verdict**

The verse is the first half of a two-verse exchange: the question (v. 21) and the verdict (v. 22) — *"this, then, is an unfair division"* (*ʿidhālan*, the fraudulent share, the crooked portion). The scholars note the judicial structure: the evidence (the idolaters' contempt for daughters) has been displayed in vv. 19–20; the accusation is formulated in v. 21; the verdict will be pronounced in v. 22; and the sentence — the demolition of the idols' very *reality* — follows in v. 23: *they are naught but names*. The cross-examination of Arabian religion is proceeding in strict order, and the accused have no counsel: their own buried daughters testify from the dust of 81:9.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:22

> **This, then, is an unfair division.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Naming the Crime: ʿIḍhālah, the Fraudulent Portion**

The verse delivers the verdict on the arrangement of verse 21 in one technical term: *tilka idhā idhan ḍīzā* — *"that, then, is an apportioning that is unjust."* The key word *ḍīzā* (verbal noun of *ḍāzā*, in the usage *ḍāzahu ʿalā* — "he cheated him of his due") is defined by the lexicographers — al-Iṣfahānī in *al-Mufradāt*, and the commentators after him — as *diminishing another's rightful portion unjustly, depriving him of what is his*. Al-Ṭabarī transmits this as the early reading; al-Zamakhsharī glosses: *an apportionment made iniquitously, cutting another off from his share*. The verse therefore does not merely *dislike* the idolaters' arrangement; it *legal-classifies* it: the theology is an embezzlement. God — the actual Owner of the heavens and the earth, as verse 25 will state in so many words — has been defrauded of His due by the imaginative bookkeeping of the idolaters: they kept the sons and handed Him the daughters, kept the world and offered Him a "spiritual" share, kept obedience and rendered Him a rival's worship. The Qur'an names the operation with the vocabulary of the cheating merchant, because that is what it is.

**Justice as the Grammar of All True Religion**

The choice of a *justice* term — not a *purity* term, not a *taste* term — to condemn idolatry is theologically enormous, and the commentators draw the doctrine out. Idolatry is not primarily an aesthetic error (worshipping the wrong style) nor even an intellectual error alone (wrong data about God); it is an *injustice* — a misappropriation of what belongs to Another. This is why the Qur'an treats shirk as the one unforgivable sin if unrepented (*"God forgives not that partners be ascribed to Him, but forgives what is less than that to whomsoever He wills,"* 4:48): it is not that other sins are small, but that shirk is *theft of the highest order* — the usurpation of the Lordship, worship, and names of the Creator by created things. Ibn Taymiyyah systematized this: the rights of God (*ḥuqūq Allāh*) are real rights, and the first of them is exclusive worship; every religion is finally an account of *portions* — what is owed to God, what is owed to men — and a religion that misportions either has corrupted both. Verse 22 is the Qur'an's audit of Arabian religion: one line, one finding: *fraudulent apportionment.*

**The Slander of Daughters as the Measure of a Theology**

Why does the Qur'an select *this* particular fraud — the daughters-slander — for the technical verdict, when the idolaters' portfolio of errors was long? The commentators answer with the sociological depth of the offense. The slander was not an innocent speculation; it was the *projection of social contempt onto heaven* — a culture that buried its daughters gave its God daughters, thereby (a) declaring its own values eternal and (b) insulating those values from critique, since they now had divine precedent. Al-Rāzī notes the mechanism as the general pathology of idolatry: the idolater *colonizes* the divine with his own preferences and then appeals to the colonized deity as authority. The Qur'an's exposure of this mechanism — *you named them; He did not* (v. 23) — is therefore not a local polemic but a permanent unmasking of how false religion is manufactured: desire drafts the doctrine, ancestry certifies it, and God is cited as its author. Every age produces its ḍīzā — its fraudulent apportionments: theologies of racial hierarchy, prosperity cults, sexual ideologies baptized as spirituality, consumption sanctified as calling. The believer's test, taught by this verse: whenever a doctrine flatters the very appetite the age most wishes to indulge, suspect the bookkeeping; check the ledger of verse 23 — *who named this?*

**The Verdict's Echoes Across the Qur'an**

The Qur'an pronounces the same audit at every appearance of the daughters-slander, and the commentators gather the chorus: *"Exalted be God above what they ascribe — unto Him belongs what is in the heavens and the earth"* (43:82); *"And they say: the Compassionate has taken a son. Glory be to Him! Nay, they are honored servants"* (21:26); *"the Jews said: ʿUzayr is the son of God, and the Christians said: the Messiah is the son of God... how they are lied to!"* (9:30); *"and they assign to God a portion of the tilth and cattle He created, saying: this is for God — by their claim! — and this for our partners"*, whereupon the verse itself performs the audit: *"that which is for their partners reaches not God, while that which is for God reaches their partners — evil is their judgment!"* (6:136). The last citation is the full anatomy of *ḍīzā* rendered in ritual terms: the fraud is always *bidirectional* — God gets what the fraudsters want to shed; they keep what they want to hold. Verse 22's single word names the whole system.

**The Reader's Own Ledger**

For the individual believer, verse 22 administers a personal audit the scholars prescribe as a spiritual exercise: *write the portions.* What has the servant actually rendered to God — of time, wealth, obedience, fear, love, trust — against what he has claimed for himself? The Prophet compressed the exercise into the legal maxim of intention: *"Deeds are only by intentions, and every man shall have what he intended"* (al-Bukhārī 1; Muslim 1907) — the ledger is kept at the source. Al-Ghazālī's analysis of *riyāʾ* (showing off) is verse 22 in miniature: the worshipper who prays for the audience has apportioned the deed *ḍīzan* — God is handed the posture, the audience the reward. The cure, in all its forms, is the same word the verse implies: *return the portion*. What is God's — the worship, the trust, the first love of the heart — must be rendered to God without the fraudulent deductions; and the astonishing promise of the same Qur'an is that the Owner defrauded daily remains *"the Vast of forgiveness"* (v. 32) toward every debtor who turns back before the final audit.

**The Verdict Stands, the Case Continues**

The verse is the case's turning point: the idols' names (vv. 19–20), their theology (v. 21), and now the legal finding (v. 22) are on record. What remains — and it is the deepest cut — is the *origin* of the whole system: who invented it, on what evidence, and under what motive? That question, the heart of the sūrah's epistemology, is verse 23's business: *they are naught but names that you have named — you and your fathers — for which God sent down no authority*. The verdict of fraud will be traced to its factory: conjecture and desire.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:23

> **They are naught but names that you have named — you and your fathers — for which God has sent down no authority. They follow naught but conjecture and that which their souls desire, though guidance has surely come to them from their Lord.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Demolition in One Sentence**

This is the longest verse of the first half of the sūrah, and it is the autopsy of idolatry. Four findings, delivered in order: (1) the idols *are nothing but names* — *in hiya illā asmāʾun sammaytumūhā*; (2) the names were *invented by you and your fathers* — the ancestry confessed; (3) no divine *authority* — *sulṭān* — was ever sent down for them; (4) the whole edifice stands on two pillars: *ẓann* (conjecture) and *hawā al-anfus* (what the souls desire) — while the corrective to both, *hudā* (guidance), had *indeed come* from their Lord. Al-Rāzī calls this verse the Qur'an's complete epistemology of false religion in miniature: an inventory of the object, a genealogy of the error, a check of the credentials, and a diagnosis of the source. Every false god in history — stone, system, self — is made of exactly these materials.

**"They Are Naught but Names": The Metaphysics of the Empty Word**

The central clause echoes one of the Qur'an's most chilling theological observations, from Joseph's lips in the prison:

> *"O my two companions of the prison! Are many lords better, or God, the One, the Almighty? You worship not, apart from Him, naught but names that you have named — you and your fathers — for which God has sent down no authority; judgment belongs not save to God; He has commanded that you worship none but Him."* (12:39–40)

The philosophical point, which the commentators — al-Zamakhsharī especially — develop, is that the idols *lack existence in the relevant sense*: the stone exists; the *deity* is a word. The idolaters did not worship nothing simpliciter; they worshiped a *sentence*. The object before them was wood and stone; the object *in their devotion* — the protector, the intercessor, the apportioner — existed only in the name they had given. Al-Ālūsī adds the insight that this makes idolatry the primordial *fiction*: the first act of shirk was not the carving of an image but the christening of one — the moment a human name was laid over a created thing like a title deed over land the deed does not own. The Qur'an's exposure is therefore grammatical before it is theological: *ask who gave the name, and on whose authority* — and the deity dissolves into its christening.

**"You and Your Fathers": The Genealogy of Error**

The phrase *you and your fathers* indicts the second pillar of the idols' prestige: ancestry. The Qur'an confronts this argument — *we found our fathers upon a creed and we follow their traces* — throughout the Book: *"And when it is said to them: Follow what God has sent down, they say: Nay, we follow what we found our fathers upon — what, though their fathers understood nothing and were not guided?"* (2:170). The commentators note the subtle order in our verse: *sammaytumūhā* — *you* named them — comes first, *your fathers* second, reversing the genealogical excuse: the fathers did not hand down the names; the naming continues in the present generation *every time it repeats them*. Ibn Taymiyyah draws the sociological lesson: tradition has real authority only when it traces to revelation; traced no further than ancestry, it is the fossilization of someone's conjecture. The modern world, which prides itself on iconoclasm yet inherits its own liturgies — ideologies, lifestyles, axioms no one examined — hears this clause as current events: the question is never *how old is the creed* but *who named it, and was any authority sent down?*

**"No Authority": The Doctrine of Sulṭān**

The word *sulṭān* — here "authority, warrant, proof" — is the Qur'an's legal term for a mandate backed by legitimate power. Its negation here means the idols never received even the lowest grade of divine authorization: no revelation, no covenant, no sign, no appointment. The commentators gather the parallels: *"Say: have you seen what you call upon apart from God? Show me what they have created of the earth — or have they a share in the heavens? Bring me a book before this, or a trace of knowledge, if you are truthful"* (46:4); *"Or have they partners who legislated for them of the religion what God does not permit?"* (42:21). The demand is evidentiary, not rhetorical only: *produce the warrant.* The scholars note that this principle — *no worship without divine warrant* — became the founding maxim of Islamic law in the sphere of rites: Ibn Taymiyyah's formula, following the Imams, *al-ʿibādah tawqīfiyyah* — worship is halted upon (regulated by) revelation; invention in worship is the naming of names all over again. The verse thus quietly founds the Muslims' distinctive caution toward liturgical innovation: if the Arabs' *al-Lāt* lacked a sulṭān, so does every rite man adds to God's religion without His word.

**Conjecture and Desire: The Twin Foundations**

The verse now names the factory. *Ẓann* — conjecture — is, per the Qur'an's usage, knowledge's counterfeit: the guess that wears certainty's clothes. The Qur'an indicts it as the operating religion of the deniers: *"They have no knowledge thereof; they follow naught but conjecture, and conjecture avails not against the truth at all"* (10:66; and v. 28 of this sūrah will return to it). *Hawā al-anfus* — what the souls desire — is the other pillar, the caprice of verse 3 now diagnosed as the *positive* content of idolatry: the idolater does not merely guess wrong; he *drafts* his god from his appetites. Al-Rāzī systematizes the pairing: ẓann is the *cognitive* defect (wrong method: guessing instead of receiving revelation), hawā the *volitional* defect (wrong aim: wanting instead of obeying) — and every false religion ever produced required both: a desire to be served and a guess to serve it. The two pillars explain why guidance (hudā) is mentioned last: guidance is revelation's content — *laqad jāʾahum al-hudā* — and it is precisely what the two pillars exclude: knowledge replacing guess, obedience replacing desire. Ibn Kathīr notes the force of *laqad*: "indeed, it has *already* come" — the excuse of ignorance is not available; the revelation stood among them, recited aloud, and they preferred the naming.

**The Verse as the Believer's Mirror**

The commentators do not leave the verse in seventh-century Makkah; they turn it upon every religious heart. Al-Ghazālī's analysis of the hidden shirk of the scholars and worshippers is this verse's application: a man may worship *names that he himself has named* — the deity of his politics, the god of his prosperity-gospel, the "Jesus" or "God" whose traits are his own preferences writ large — with no sulṭān in the Book and Sunnah for what he attributes. The examination the verse prescribes is threefold, and it has saved souls in every century: (1) for each thing I obey, *who named it?* (2) for each belief I hold about God, *where is the authority?* (3) when my religion and my desires agree suspiciously well, *which is drafting which?* The Prophet's warning stands behind the exercise: *"I fear for my ummah other than shirk"* — the hidden one — and when asked what that was, he answered: *"riyāʾ"* (showing off in worship; transmitted by Aḥmad from Maḥmūd ibn Labīd, with sound chains). The names we lay over God are often subtler than statues; verse 23 hands the believer the chisel to remove them: *who named it, on whose authority, on which pillar?* And the verse ends with the standing offer: *guidance has indeed come* — it still comes, recited in every dawn; the factory of conjecture is never forced upon a heart that prefers the given word.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:24

> **Or shall man have whatsoever he longs for?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question That Refuses the Self as Its Own Measure**

The verse poses the question beneath all idolatry and beneath all modern autonomy: *a-li'l-insāni mā yamannā* — *"is the human being to have whatever he covets?"* The verb *mannā* (root *m-n-y*, to *wish, covet, long for* — the root also of *al-maniyyah*, the appointed death) denotes not neutral desire but *possessive longing*, the wish that demands fulfillment. Al-Rāzī reads the interrogative as a refutation of the entire project of verse 23's idolaters: having founded religion on conjecture and desire, they have in effect claimed that *desire is the measure of truth* — and the verse asks, with the Qur'an's characteristic brevity, whether the universe is actually arranged that way. Is reality a mail-order catalog of man's wishes? Al-Zamakhsharī hears the specific application: the Quraysh *longed* for the idols' intercession, longed for a religion of ancestral ease, longed for a god who needed them — and presumed the longing itself a warrant. The verse answers: longing is not a deed, and the world does not pay for wishes; the Hereafter pays *exclusively* for deeds — as verse 39 will soon declare: *"man shall have naught but that for which he endeavored."*

**The Qur'an's Economics of Desire**

The Qur'an repeatedly separates *desire* from *entitlement*, and the commentators gather the constellation. Of the witless who demand miracles to order: *"They swear by God with their strongest oaths: if a sign came to them they would believe in it. Say: signs are only with God — and what will make you perceive that even if it came, they would not believe?"* (6:109). Of the demand for angels and golden houses: *"And they say: We will not believe until we are given the like of what the messengers of God were given. God knows best where to place His message"* (6:124); and *"Nay — they say: we found our fathers upon a community, and we are guided upon their traces"* (43:22). The principle: the world is a *test* — *"Who created death and life to try you as to which of you is best in deed"* (67:2) — and a test by definition withholds whatever would nullify choice. Ibn Taymiyyah formulates it: were every desire granted, obedience would be compulsion and disobedience impossible; the wisdom of God is that *the world answers labor, not longing* — longing is raw material, and only the labor that disciplines it becomes anything.

**Desire Itself Is Not Condemned**

A crucial balance the scholars maintain: the Qur'an does not condemn desire as such; it condemns desire as *measure and master*. The very book that asks this question promises the obedient *whatsoever they long for*: *"Therein is whatsoever the souls desire and the eyes delight in, and you shall abide therein"* (43:71). The Prophetic promise is even more lavish: in the Garden, *"there is what no eye has seen, no ear has heard, and has not occurred to the heart of any man"* (al-Bukhārī 4779; Muslim 2824) — and the last of the people of Paradise to enter is offered, per the ḥadīth of Muslim, *the like of the world and ten times over*, then whatever he wishes (Muslim 186–187). The discipline is therefore not the extermination of desire but its *sequencing*: desires are seeds, and the life of obedience is the field — *"Say: if you love God, follow me; God will love you and forgive you your sins"* (3:31). Al-Ghazālī's psychology, built on this foundation: desire untrained becomes the tyrant of verse 3's *hawā*; desire trained becomes the engine of worship — the same longing that made the Quraysh cling to idols, redirected, makes the believer cling to prayer.

**The Modern Creed of Desire**

The verse reads the modern age with startling accuracy. Contemporary culture has institutionalized the answer the verse refutes: the market promises *whatsoever you long for*, the algorithm *learns what you long for*, the ethics of self-expression declares longing itself the highest authority — *my truth*, the phrase of the age, is simply *a-li'l-insāni mā yamannā* answered *yes*. The scholars' analysis exposes the cost. First, the economy of disappointment: reality does not pay for wishes, so the desire-measured life is a treadmill of frustration and envy — the Qur'an's portrait of the world-devotee: *"let not their wealth and children impress thee; God only wishes to punish them thereby in the life of this world, that their souls depart while they are disbelievers"* (9:55). Second, the tyranny of the wish: a man who must obey his desires has no self to govern — the psychologists' "addiction" is the Qur'an's *hawā* made visible. Third, the loss of the Hereafter: if desire is the measure, then the unpleasant truth — judgment, accountability, the narrowing way — is simply edited out, which is verse 23's fourth pillar all over again. The believer's liberation, taught by this verse, is the discovery that *not every longing is a right, and the soul that consents to God's measures gains every longing that was worth having.*

**From Rhetorical Question to Chapter Theme**

The verse is a hinge in the sūrah's architecture: behind it lie the idols (built by desire and conjecture); ahead lie the two great affirmations that answer it — verse 25 (*"Unto God belong the Hereafter and the world"*: ownership settles who portions what) and verse 39 (*"man shall have naught but that for which he endeavored"*: labor, not longing, is the currency). The commentators note the chapter's method: it refutes desire-worship twice — once as *theology* (idols), once as *eschatology* (the denial of judgment) — because both are the same heresy at different altitudes: the claim that man's wishes, not God's word, define reality. The believer who has absorbed verses 24–25 possesses a complete theory of the good life: desire exists; the world's Lord owns both worlds; and the only question at the audit is not *what did you long for?* but *what did you do?*

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:25

> **Yet unto God belong the Hereafter and this world.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Ownership Statement That Settles the Dispute**

Where verse 24 asked whether man is entitled to his longings, verse 25 states the fact that answers it: *fa-lillāhi al-ākhiratu wa al-ūlā* — *"then, unto God belong the Hereafter and the first [this world]."* The particle *fa* — "then, therefore" — draws the inference from the refuted claim: desire does not portion reality, because *ownership* portions reality, and the title deed reads God's name over *both* terms of existence — *al-ākhirah* (the Hereafter, the end-term) and *al-ūlā* (the former, the first term, this present life). The commentators note the elegant inversion: the idolaters had apportioned God a *portion* of reality (daughters, intercession, a shrine's share) — as though He were a co-claimant in their ledger. The verse overturns the ledger: He does not hold a portion; He holds *the whole* — both the world they could see and the Hereafter they denied. Al-Rāzī states the doctrine: God's ownership of the two worlds is not metaphorical possession but *creating, sustaining, and absolute disposing authority* — *"Unto God belongs whatsoever is in the heavens and whatsoever is on the earth"* recurs across the Qur'an (2:284; 5:120; 24:42; and v. 31 of this very sūrah) — and a man who grasps this ceases to negotiate with the Owner.

**The Twin Denials of the Idolaters**

The commentators specify the two denials this verse corrects, and they are precise. First, the denial of God's lordship over *this* world: the idolaters conceded — the Qur'an acknowledges this repeatedly — that God *created* the heavens and the earth (29:61; 31:25; 39:38; 43:87), yet assigned *running* of the world's affairs to their idols: provision, healing, fate, intercession. The verse corrects: the *ūlā* — the visible, daily, economic, agricultural world — is His; the shrine at Nakhlah holds no deed to it. Second, the denial of the *ākhirah*: the Quraysh's auditors said plainly, *"there is naught but our life in this world: we die and we live, and naught destroys us but time"* (45:24) — and this denial the verse also corrects: the *ākhirah* is His, to bring about, to people with the raised dead, and to run by perfect justice. Al-Ālūsī draws the synthesis: whoever concedes the first term but denies the second has not actually conceded the first — for the ownership of the *end* is included in the ownership of the *means*; a landlord who owns the field owns the harvest. To believe truly in this world's God is to believe in the world to come; the creed that cuts the second half off has corrupted the first.

**The Order of the Two Terms: Why the Hereafter Is Named First**

The scholars savor the word order: *al-ākhirah* is named *before* *al-ūlā*, though in time it comes after. Several purposes converge, per the commentators. First, rhetorical priority: the disputed term (the idolaters denied the Hereafter outright) is named first, as a court names first the contested claim. Second, value-priority: in the Qur'an's scale of realities, the Hereafter is the *greater* term — *"the life of this world is naught but play and amusement; and the abode of the Hereafter — that is the [true] life, did they but know"* (29:64) — so the greater is named before the lesser, as one says "the sun and the candle." Third, the sequence mirrors the believer's decision-structure: when the two terms conflict — when this world's gain requires Hereafter's loss — the believer's order of operations must already be set: Hereafter first. The Prophet's counsel enshrines it: *"Be in this world as a stranger or a passing traveler"* (al-Bukhārī 6416, from Ibn ʿUmar) — not as a hatred of the world but as the correct filing of two addresses under one Owner.

**Both Worlds Owned by One Lord: The Cure for Despair and for Pride**

Al-Qurṭubī observes that the single ownership of both terms produces two balanced sciences in the believer: against *despair*, and against *pride*. Against despair: the man who loses in this world — wealth, health, status, the graves of his loved ones — has not left the Owner's domain; the ākhirah where recompense is stored is His, and no loss is final *in His house*: *"We will surely try you with something of fear and hunger and loss of wealth and lives and fruits; but give glad tidings to the patient"* (2:155). Against pride: the man who gains in this world holds a lease, not a deed — and the Lessor may repossess at any term: the Qur'an's repeated portraits of the ruined wealthy (Qārūn, 28:76–82; the garden's owner, 68:17–33) are case law. Ibn Taymiyyah formulates the balance as the believer's two-winged heart: *fear for the first term, hope for the second, and trust in the One who owns both.* The worshipper stands in prayer between the two: the *ūlā* is the prayer's setting, the *ākhirah* its aim — *"Seek the abode of the Hereafter with what God has given thee, and forget not thy portion of the world"* (28:77).

**The Verse in the Sūrah's Argument**

Position gives the verse its force. It stands between the refuted theology of desire (v. 24) and the refuted theology of angelic intercession (vv. 26–30): desire has no claim because ownership is not ours; intercession has no channel because *"intercession avails naught with Him save for him to whom He permits"* (v. 26) — and both corrections flow from the one fact stated here: *unto God belong the Hereafter and this world.* The commentators note that the verse also foreshadows the sūrah's ending: the final verses will speak of the *imminent* Hour (v. 57) that none unveils but God (v. 58) — the ākhirah in motion; and of prostration and worship (v. 62) — the ūlā's proper response to the Owner of both. The whole chapter is, in a sense, an exposition of verse 25: the Lord of both terms has spoken (vv. 1–18); the rivals who contested His ownership are names (vv. 19–23); the terms themselves are His (v. 25); and the judgment that settles every account issues from the same single court (vv. 31–46).

**The Believer's Practical Deed: Living as a Trustee**

The verse's practical science the scholars call *the discipline of trusteeship* (amānah): whatever the believer holds in the ūlā — wealth, position, body, children, hours — he holds *as a trust from the Owner of the term*, to be spent as the Owner directs and surrendered at the Owner's summons. The Prophet modeled the trusteeship in every register: he distributed the *fayʾ* revenues until his cloak was patched with palm fiber, saying, *"O God, make the provision of the family of Muhammad sufficient"* (Muslim 1055, from ʿUmar's narration of the Prophet's prayer on the minbar); and his famous teaching to ʿUmar on the captives of Badr — *"had they all been destroyed, you would be more wretched than their camels"* (Muslim 1763) — was the lesson that even victories and spoils are the Owner's, disposed as He wills. The believer who internalizes verse 25 holds everything *lighter* — loss cannot desolate him, gain cannot corrupt him — because nothing in his hands was ever really his, and everything ahead of him is guaranteed by the One who owns the road there: *unto God belong the Hereafter and this world.*

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:26

> **And how many an angel is there in the heavens whose intercession avails naught, save after God grants leave unto whomsoever He wills and is content withal?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument Moves to the Upper Registrations**

The sūrah has demolished the idols below; verse 26 moves the argument to the sky above: even the *angels* — the highest, holiest, most real of the beings the pagan imagination misplaced its trust in — possess no intercession of their own. The rhetorical form, *wa kam min malakin* — *"and how many an angel..."* — is the Qur'an's formula of massive quantity: the number of the angels is vast beyond counting; the Prophet saw them filling the lote tree (v. 16); the Qur'an describes them as *"none can count the warriors of thy Lord save He"* (74:31). And *of all those multitudes*, the verse says, not one intercedes except upon two simultaneous conditions. Al-Ṭabarī draws the *a fortiori* logic the Qur'an intends: if the angels — present in God's own heavens, made of light, sinless, endlessly praising — cannot move Him by a whisper without leave, then stones in a Ṭāʾif temple and a star in Canis Major avail exactly nothing. The argument does not deny the angels' existence or dignity; it re-positions them: they are servants in the court, not shareholders in the throne.

**The Two Conditions: Leave and Contentment**

The verse attaches two governing clauses to the angels' intercession, and the commentators treat them as a complete doctrine of mediation. First: *illā baʿda an yaʾdhana Allāhu* — *"save after God grants leave"* — intercession exists only as a *conferred authority*, never as an inherent power; the angel does not advocate; the angel is *permitted* to advocate. Second: *li-man yashāʾu wa yarḍā* — *"unto whomsoever He wills and is content withal"* — the permission has a *double filter*: it is for those *He* selects, and only when He is *content* (radiya) with them. The scholars unfold the implications with care. Ibn Kathīr gathers the sister-verses: *"And intercession avails naught with Him save for him to whom He grants leave"* (34:23); *"On that day no intercession avails save of him to whom the Compassionate grants leave and whose speech He accepts"* (20:109); *"they [the angels] speak not before He speaks, and they act by His command"* (21:28); *"And how many an angel in the heavens whose intercession avails naught save after God grants leave for whom He wills and is content"* (53:26) — and the Book's summary: *"Say: Unto God belongs intercession altogether"* (39:44). The doctrine that emerges: intercession is not a *rival channel* to God's grace; it is a *protocol within* God's grace — He honors some by permitting their advocacy, and He answers the advocacy because it is His own will wearing the garment of honor for His servant.

**The Prophet's Intercession: The Balance of Hope**

The same doctrine, correctly understood, governs the greatest intercession of all — the Prophet's *shafāʿah* — and the ḥadīth corpus is precise about its conditions. The Prophet said, from Abū Hurayrah: *"For every prophet there was a supplication he made for his people, and I have reserved my supplication as intercession for my ummah on the Day of Resurrection"* (al-Bukhārī 6304; Muslim 758). And Muslim transmits the comprehensive scene: on the Day, all creation seeks intercession, and the people come to Adam, Noah, Abraham, Moses, Jesus — each saying, *myself, myself* — until they come to Muhammad ﷺ, who says, *"I am for it, I am for it"* and goes to the Throne and prostrates, and God inspires him with praises he does not yet know, and it is said: *"O Muhammad, raise your head and ask, you will be given; intercede, your intercession will be accepted"* (al-Bukhārī 447; Muslim 193). Note the protocol in the very scene: the intercessor himself *prostrates* and is *permitted* to speak — even the best of creation intercedes *within* the leave of verse 26, never outside it. And the Prophet bounded his own intercession honestly: *"My intercession is for those of my ummah who committed grave sins"* (Abū Dāwūd 4739; al-Tirmidhī 2437 — graded ḥasan) — i.e., for the sinners of faith who enter the Fire, not for those who die in shirk, for of the idolaters he was forbidden even to seek forgiveness: *"It is not for the Prophet and those who believe to seek forgiveness for the idolaters"* (9:113).

**Why the Pagans' Intercession-Theology Was Impossible**

The commentators explain why the Qur'an spends so much of this sūrah on intercession. The pagan system was *transactional*: the idols were fed, clothed, funded, and honored *on the calculation* that they possessed leverage — that favor purchased access. Verse 26 destroys the calculation at its economic root: access to God is not a market; it is a court whose protocols He alone publishes. The Qur'an even mocks the ledger: *"They worship apart from God that which neither harms them nor benefits them, and they say: these are our intercessors with God. Say: would you inform God of what He knows not in the heavens and the earth? Glory be to Him, and exalted is He above what they associate"* (10:18). And the Qur'an pre-empts the pagans' best case — *perhaps God will permit them* — with the dry note of 34:23 and 10:3: *"intercession avails not with Him save for whom He permits"* — the permission is not a loophole the sly can farm; it is distributed by *knowledge* of hearts, and *"He knows best who is worthy of guidance"* (6:117, echoed in v. 30 of this sūrah). Ibn Taymiyyah draws the pastoral conclusion: hope in intercession is lawful only as hope *in God's promise through* the intercessor, never as confidence *in the intercessor against* God — the moment a man trusts the mediator more than the Judge, he has rebuilt the shrine at Nakhlah in his heart.

**The Believer's Application: The Only Sure Access**

The practical theology the verse generates is the most liberating in religion: *the servant's access to God is direct, constant, and free.* The Qur'an guarantees the nearness of 2:186 — *"when My servants ask thee concerning Me, I am near; I answer the call of the caller when he calls"* — note the singular "Me," the unmediated "I"; and the Prophet taught the poorest believer the protocol of verse 26 in reverse: *"When one of you asks, let him ask his Lord, for He gives generously and is not niggardly... none of you must say: O God, forgive me if You will"* (al-Bukhārī 6339; Muslim 2677) — ask decisively, for He is not a court that must be petitioned through advocates. The scholars summarize the believer's relationship to all intercession: love for the Prophet and hope in his promised intercession; certainty that God's mercy needs no channel; and the good works that make one *worthy* of the leave the verse describes — for the intercession benefits, per the ṣaḥīḥ texts, *"him for whom God wills"* and whose faith the Intercessor knows. The shrine once more: the pagan goes to a stone because the stone is *for sale*; the believer goes to the King because the King *wants to be found* — and the angels in their myriads watch, without leave to whisper, the astonishing spectacle of a creature in prostration speaking directly to the Lord of the lote tree.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:27

> **Truly those who believe not in the Hereafter name the angels with female names.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse of Diagnosis, Not Description**

The verse performs a diagnostic maneuver more subtle than refutation: it does not merely deny the idolaters' claim — it *dates* their error by its root. *Inna alladhīna lā yuʾminūna bi'l-ākhirati la-yusammūna al-malāʾikata tasmiyata al-unthā* — *"those who do not believe in the Hereafter — they surely name the angels with female names."* The grammatical structure is causal: the *female-naming* is presented as the *necessary fruit* of the disbelief in the Hereafter. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī note the reversal of the expected order: one would think disbelief in judgment leads to skepticism about angels entirely; instead, the verse shows that the deniers *retain* the unseen — they only *redesign* it. This is the Qur'an's profound observation about human religion: man cannot live without an unseen; remove the true one, and he will manufacture a counterfeit — not fewer beings, but *domesticated* beings, named after his own preferences.

**The Double Error the Verse Combines**

The commentators unfold the verse's two linked indictments. First, *the fabrication of names*: as 43:19 asks with withering logic:

> *"And they have made the angels — servants of the Compassionate — females. Did they witness their creation? Their testimony will be recorded, and they will be questioned."* (43:19)

Nobody *witnessed* angelic origins; the naming is sheer fabrication — *tasmiyah*, the act of laying names upon things, the very crime verse 23 catalogued (*"names that you have named"*). Second, *the contempt embedded in the fabrication*: to imagine the awesome servants of the Throne as *daughters* is to shrink them into the manageable proportions of the household — and, in the idolaters' culture, to imagine them as the very category (daughters) their society despised. Al-Rāzī observes the psychology: man diminishes what he cannot dominate; the angels, too mighty to obey and too real to deny, were *feminized* — made soft, made kin, made *negotiable* — precisely so that they could be enrolled as allies in the pagan system: the daughters of God, begging the Father on the idolaters' behalf. The verse thus exposes the manufacture of religion as a factory with two machines: *naming* (fabrication) and *feminizing* (domestication) — both running on the raw material of a denied Hereafter.

**Why Disbelief in the Hereafter Is the Root**

Why does the verse anchor the diagnosis in the Hereafter rather than in shirk directly? The commentators answer with the structure of unbelief itself. The Hereafter is the doctrine that *fixes the meaning* of all other doctrines: it declares that reality ends in accountability, that God's judgments are final, that every name and claim will be audited. A man who denies the audit is free — *internally* free — to redesign every other truth at will, because no final court will check him. Ibn Taymiyyah's aphorism: *the corruption of every falsehood begins with the corruption of its eschatology* — and the Qur'an confirms the pattern across the Book: the idolaters' shirk, the People of the Book's exaggerated claims about their prophets, the hypocrites' easy religion — all are traced to their dismissal of the Last Day (*"they denied Our signs until they met them suddenly,"* 6:31). Conversely, the believer's self-correction is powered by the same root: whoever *truly* believes the audit is coming will not dare to name God's creatures on his own authority — he will ask the Revelation what the angels are, what worship is, what God has actually said. The verse thus gives the believer a diagnostic for himself: *check your Hereafter-check, and your theology will check itself.*

**The Angels' True Profile: The Antidote to the Counterfeit**

The Qur'an does not leave the angels anonymous; it replaces the counterfeit with the true portrait, and the commentators gather it. The angels are *"honored servants who precede Him not in speech, and act at His command"* (21:26–27); they *"glorify Him by night and day, unceasing"* (41:38); they *"prostrate to God before Him"* (7:206; 15:30); they bear the Throne (40:7), record the deeds (82:10–11), guard mankind (13:11), and — decisively for this verse's context — *intercede only within permission* (v. 26). Their gender is simply *not* among their properties: they are neither male nor female; the Qur'an's masculine forms are grammatical, and the scholars note that nothing in revelation assigns them sex at all. The famous ḥadīth of Gabriel (al-Bukhārī 50; Muslim 8) presents an angel as a teacher in the form of a man — a *form*, assumed for the lesson, not a nature. The believer's imagination is thereby disciplined: the unseen is *populated by revelation's report, not by the culture's preferences* — and the discipline is exactly what verse 27 demands: *no naming without warrant.*

**The Sociology of Counterfeit Religion**

The verse's deeper lesson is sociological, and the scholars have applied it in every century: *a community's religion is legible from its Hereafter-awareness.* Where the audit is believed, religion is demanding, humble, and precise — because errors will be marked. Where the audit is dismissed, religion migrates toward whatever the community desires: gods of prosperity, goddesses of fertility, angels of the hearth, saints of the tribe — each *named* to serve a social function, none burdened with judgment. Al-Rāzī's analysis of the pagan Arabs fits modernity: their idolatry was not primarily stupidity about metaphysics; it was *convenience* — an unseen designed to bless their markets and wars while demanding no accounting. The modern marketplace of spirituality — self-esteem deities, consumerist heaven, judgment-free rituals — is verse 27 with different iconography: the female-naming of angels is the prototype of *every* theology that remodels the unseen in the customer's image. The believer's defense is the verse's implicit command: take the unseen *as described*, believe the audit *as scheduled*, and let no creature — angel, self, or system — be renamed by desire.

**From Diagnosis to Epistemology**

The verse prepares the sūrah's next movement, which will complete the case: verse 28 will state the epistemological verdict on all such naming — *"they have no knowledge of it; they follow naught but conjecture, and conjecture avails naught against the truth"* — the formal demonstration that the counterfeit unseen is *unwitnessed* (43:19's question), *ungrounded* (no sulṭān, v. 23), and *unprofitable* (avails naught). The reader watches the Qur'an's forensic method reach its conclusion: the idols were traced to names, the names to fathers, the fathers to conjecture and desire — and behind all of it, the disbelieving heart that had silenced the Day of Account so it could worship in peace. The verse stands as the sūrah's psychological keystone: *reform the Hereafter-belief, and the idols fall; kill the Hereafter-belief, and the idols multiply.*

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:28

> **Yet they have no knowledge thereof. They follow naught but conjecture; and surely conjecture avails naught against the Truth.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Formal Verdict on Their Epistemology**

The sūrah now issues the formal epistemological finding: *wa mā lahum bihi min ʿilmin* — *"and they have no knowledge of it"* — of the angels, or, on the alternative reading the commentators record, of *God Himself* (*bihī* referring to Him). The sentence is a verdict of total evidentiary bankruptcy: the entire theology condemned in verses 19–27 — the idols, the daughters, the feminized angels, the purchased intercession — possesses *not one item of knowledge*. Al-Ṭabarī notes the judicial force of *mā... min ʿilmin*: the Arabic construction negates knowledge *categorically* — not merely ignorance of details but the absence of the very *kind* of cognition that could ground the claims. The second sentence supplies the diagnosis: *in yattabiʿūna illā al-ẓann* — *"they follow naught but conjecture"* — and the third the finality: *wa inna al-ẓanna lā yughnī mina al-ḥaqqi shayʾan* — *"and conjecture avails not at all against the truth."* Three sentences: no knowledge; therefore conjecture; therefore useless before the Truth. The commentators call this the Qur'an's *syllogism of ruin*, and it recurs like a refrain: *"and most of them follow naught but conjecture; truly conjecture avails not against the truth in the least"* (10:36); *"those who dispute concerning God without any knowledge... and they follow naught but conjecture"* (22:8, 10:66); *"they follow naught but conjecture and what the souls desire, though guidance has come to them from their Lord"* (53:23).

**The Anatomy of Ẓann: What Guessing Cannot Do**

The word *ẓann* in the Qur'an names judgment without verified grounds — the guess worn as conviction. The commentators, led by al-Rāghib al-Iṣfahānī, distinguish it from *yaqīn* (certainty) and from *shakk* (paralyzing doubt): ẓann is the operative middle — the *working assumption* that drives action without deserving it. The Qur'an's indictment is not that ẓann exists — in matters of the unseen *revelation supplies what guesswork cannot* — but that man *substitutes* it for the given knowledge and then stakes his eternity on it. Al-Rāzī's analysis of what conjecture *cannot* do is the verse's commentary in full: (1) it cannot *establish* — guesses by definition lack warrant; (2) it cannot *withstand* — when the Truth arrives (in revelation now, in the Resurrection finally), conjecture does not argue, it *evaporates*: *"the day their excuses shall not profit the evildoers, and theirs shall be the curse and theirs the evil abode"* (40:52); (3) it cannot *guide* — a guess, being unstable, cannot bear the weight of a life; the man who steers by ẓann re-derives his religion each decade with each desire. Hence the verse's calculus — *lā yughnī min al-ḥaqqi shayʾan*: it *avails* (yughnī, enriches, suffices) *not one thing* against the Truth. Not partially, not provisionally: at the bar of the Real, the guess is legal tender nowhere.

**The Prophet's Parallel Warning Against Ẓann Among Believers**

The commentators pair the verse with the Prophet's famous ḥadīth on suspicion, and the pairing is instructive. He said:

> *"Beware of conjecture (al-ẓann), for conjecture is the most false of speech. Do not spy, do not vie with one another, do not hate one another... but be servants of God as brothers."* (al-Bukhārī 6064; Muslim 2563)

The scholars note the precision of the convergence: in *creed*, the Qur'an condemns ẓann because guesses about God and the unseen breed idolatry; in *community*, the Prophet condemns ẓann because guesses about *people* breed suspicion, spying, and schism. The root disease is one: acting on unverified judgment. And the remedy is one: *verification* — in creed, by revelation; in society, by proof and charity of interpretation (*"if a corrupter comes to you with news, verify,"* 49:6). The believer's epistemic discipline is therefore uniform across domains: no verdicts without evidence; no theology without revelation; no accusations without proof. A community that lives by the verse's negative — *not conjecture* — becomes immune to the twin plagues that destroy civilizations: false gods and mutual suspicion.

**Conjecture as the Religion of Convenience**

Why do human beings — ancient and modern — *prefer* conjecture to knowledge? The commentators' answer is sobering: conjecture is *cheaper*. Knowledge arrives on revelation's terms: obedience, submission, the humility of receiving. Conjecture arrives on desire's terms: it can be whatever the guesser wishes — which is why the Qur'an's two verses pair ẓann with hawā so persistently (*"they follow naught but conjecture and what the souls desire,"* 53:23; *"and what is more astray than he who follows his caprice without guidance from God?"*, 28:50). Al-Rāzī formulates the cycle: desire selects the thesis, conjecture certifies it, and the pair reproduce — each guess licensed by the wish it serves. The modern epistemic landscape — filter bubbles, motivated reasoning, the algorithmic feeding of ẓann at industrial scale — is this verse's case file. The believer's escape is not skepticism (which is another ẓann) but *submission to the given*: revelation as the fixed point by which all other judgments are calibrated. The Qur'an's question to every age is the question of 10:35–36, which the commentators cite as this verse's twin:

> *"Say: Is there any of your partners who guides to the truth? Say: God guides to the truth. Is he who guides to the truth worthier to be followed, or he who guides not unless he is guided? What then ails you — how do you judge? And most of them follow naught but conjecture; truly conjecture avails naught against the truth."* (10:35–36)

**"Against the Truth": Al-Ḥaqq as a Name of God**

A final depth: the word rendered "Truth" — *al-ḥaqq* — is itself among God's Names (*"That is because God — He is the Truth,"* 22:62; *"the day they are ushered before Him, He will say: did you not... "*" and *"God is the Truth, and He gives life to the dead,"* 22:6). The verse's logic therefore closes on the strongest possible footing: conjecture avails nothing against *the Truth* — not against correct propositions merely, but against the living, judging, uncreated Reality whose name is Truth. At the Resurrection, the verse's arithmetic will be enacted literally: every guess will be called to account by the Truth Himself — *"that He may requite those who do evil for what they did, and requite those who do good with the most beautiful"* (v. 31) — and the equation of eternity will not accept conjecture as payment. The believer leaves the verse with the cleanest of positions: what he knows, he knows by revelation; what he has not been given, he leaves to the Knower of v. 30; and his prayer gathers both into the Prophet's constant plea: *"O God, show me the truth as truth and grant that I follow it; and show me falsehood as falsehood and grant that I avoid it"* (recorded in the collections from the Prophet's supplications, cf. Ibn Mājah 3980, and in Muslim's wording of the duʿā of guidance: 2747).

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:29

> **So turn away from whosoever turns away from Our Reminder and desires naught but the life of this world.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command of Strategic Withdrawal**

The verdicts have been rendered; now comes the operational instruction: *fa-aʿriḍ ʿamman tawallā ʿan dhikrinā* — *"so turn away from whosoever turns away from Our Reminder."* The command *aʿriḍ* (from *ʿ-r-ḍ*, to turn the side, to incline away) is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ and, through him, to every carrier of the message. Al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī, and al-Ālūsī explain its scope precisely: it is *not* an embargo on calling to God — the Prophet's whole mission was the call — but an instruction of *non-entanglement* with the one who has *tawallā* — deliberately turned his back — after the truth reached him. The distinction matters: the Muslim pursues the seeker, debates the doubter, weeps over the strayer; but the confirmed despiser of revelation is *left* — his case is with God, and continued confrontation serves only to harden him, embolden his mockery, and exhaust the caller. The Prophet's own practice followed this instruction: he answered Abū Jahl's insults with silence at times, moved from the assemblies that mocked, and the Qur'an recorded the principle elsewhere: *"When you see those who engage in [mockery of] Our signs, turn away from them until they engage in other discourse"* (6:68); *"Thy duty is naught but conveyance"* (5:99); and the model command of 15:94–95: *"So declare openly what you are commanded, and turn away from the idolaters; We suffice you against the mockers."*

**"Our Reminder": The Many Names of One Book**

The phrase *dhikrinā* — "Our Reminder" — is one of the Qur'an's names, and the commentators enumerate the layers the word carries. Primarily: *al-Dhikr*, the Reminder, is the Qur'an itself — *"We sent down the Reminder that you may make clear to mankind what was sent down to them"* (16:44); *"This is a Reminder, so whoever wills, let him take a way to his Lord"* (73:19); and the refrain: *"We made the Qur'an easy for remembrance,"* (54:17). Al-Baghawī and al-Qurṭubī add the alternates the tradition reads: *Our remembrance* — belief in God generally; the *remembrance of death* — the thought that sobers every diversion (the reading of the mystic commentators per al-Ālūsī); and the *remembrance of God* as practice, tongue and heart together, which the Qur'an calls the life of the soul: *"Truly in the remembrance of God do hearts find rest"* (13:28). The multivalence is deliberate: all four readings describe the same turning-away in different keys — the man who abandons revelation, abandons faith, avoids death's thought, and refuses God's company; and the one who turns from *him* is withdrawing from all four refusals at once. The believer internalizes the reading that calibrates his own life: *is the Book my constant remembrance, or have I turned my side to it?* — for the command to turn away is addressed to the caller only; to the caller's own soul, the command runs the other way: *cling*.

**The Second Marker: Desire for This World Alone**

The verse's second clause completes the portrait of the man to be avoided: *wa lam yurid illā al-ḥayāta al-dunyā* — *"and desires naught but the life of the world."* Al-Qurṭubī notes the linkage: the two clauses describe one psychology from its cause and its effect — the world-devotee turns from the Reminder *because* the Reminder competes with his inventory; and his turning confirms what his creed already said: *"there is naught but our life in this world: we die and we live, and we will not be raised"* (23:37). The commentators observe the economy of the portrait: not the man who *has* this world — many believers have much — but the man who *wants only* it, whose horizon terminates at his own inventory. The Prophet's counsel sharpens the distinction: *"Two ambitions (ḥaramān) never fill: the ambition for wealth and the ambition for rank"* (al-Bukhārī 6437); and *"O son of Adam, were you to devote yourself to My worship, I would fill your chest with richness and close your poverty; and if you do not, I would fill your hands with occupation and not close your poverty"* (a ḥadīth qudsī transmitted by al-Tirmidhī 2465 and Ibn Mājah 4107, graded ḥasan). The turning-away command is thus also a warning *to the caller*: do not envy the world-devotee his busyness; his full hands are his poverty.

**Why Turning Away Is Mercy**

The scholars insist — and the verse's grammar supports it — that the command to turn away is not contempt but strategy of mercy. Ibn Taymiyyah's analysis of *daʿwah* methodology: the call has two phases, *tablīgh* (conveyance) and *taslīm* (leaving the outcome to God), and phase two is not failure but obedience: *"and as for those who turn away, We have not sent thee as a guardian over them"* (4:80); *"thy duty is but to convey, and Ours the reckoning"* (13:40); *"thou dost not guide whomsoever thou lovest, but God guides whomsoever He wills"* (28:56 — revealed at the most painful turning-point of the sīrah, the death of Abū Ṭālib unconverted). The turning-away also *protects* the believer: the mockers' assembly corrupts — *"when you pass by those who engage in vain discourse, pass by with dignity"* (25:72) — and *protects* the truth itself, which loses its dignity when it must beg from those who spit. And it *witnesses* finally against the denier: the Prophet said, *"Whoever turns away from my sunnah is not of me"* in another context (al-Bukhārī 5063), and the Qur'an's logic here: the turned back was *given* the Reminder before the side was turned; the withdrawal of the caller is the last sermon.

**The Caller's Heart During the Turning**

The tradition preserves the emotional register of this command, because the Prophet lived its hardest application. He turned away from Ṭāʾif bleeding, saying only: *"O God, to You I complain of my weakness... if You are not angry with me, I care not"* (the famous supplication of the return from Ṭāʾif, transmitted in the sīrah literature). He turned away from the noses of the Quraysh who threw entrails on his back in prostration — his daughter Fāṭimah wept as she cleaned him, and he said: *"Weep not, my daughter; God will make your father victorious"* (al-Bukhārī 4953). And he turned away, years later, with the words the ummah recites at every funeral: *"To God we belong, and to Him we return"* — the turned-away world of the dead not his to recover. The scholars derive the caller's etiquette from these scenes: turning away is *not* the cancellation of love — the Prophet loved his people to his last breath, saying of Makkah's storm of angels, *"no, I hope God will bring forth from their loins those who worship God alone"* (al-Bukhārī 1043; Muslim 1795) — it is the redirection of love into patience, supplication, and the long game of generations. The caller turns his face; he never turns his hope.

**The Verse's Edge for the Believer**

One last turn of the verse lands on the reciter: the command describes the man whose *only* desire is dunyā — and every believer must occasionally ask whether the description has crept into his own biography: the Prayer shortened for the meeting, the Qur'an postponed for the quarter's results, the Hereafter believed *theoretically* and planned *never*. The commentators' closing counsel: the Prophet never turned away from a *seeker*, never from a *sinner repenting*, never from a *question* asked sincerely — the turned-away are only the *turned-away*. So long as a heart can still ask, it has not yet been abandoned; and the believer's business is to be present at every asking, his own included.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:30

> **That is the extent of their knowledge. Surely thy Lord knows best him who strays from His way, and He knows best him who is rightly guided.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Ledger Closes: The Extent of Their Knowledge**

The verse closes the file on the deniers with a dry summation: *dhālika mā lahum mina al-ʿilmi* — *"that — that — is the extent of their knowledge."* The demonstrative *dhālika*, flung like a hand closing a ledger, gathers the entire preceding case into a single accounting: their knowledge of God amounts to the idols' names (v. 23), their knowledge of the angels to fabricated female names (v. 27), their knowledge of the unseen to conjecture (v. 28), their knowledge of the Hereafter to denial (v. 27), their knowledge of destiny to the inventory of the marketplace (v. 29). Al-Qurṭubī records both readings the tradition transmitted: it is the *sum total* of their knowledge of religion — a zero-sum; or it is the *limit* of their interest — they do know the world's roads and markets, and that is *all* they chose to know. Al-Rāzī adds the poignancy: the phrase does not say they know *nothing*; it says they know *that* — the maximal inventory of a wasted mind: the dunyā's merchandise, catalogued perfectly, and behind it a void.

**The Repetition with Verse 23: The Sūrah's Refrain**

The commentators notice that this verse repeats, with variation, the finding of verse 23 — *"they follow naught but conjecture and that which their souls desire"* — and that its second sentence is the same sentence revealed in Sūrah al-Anʿām: *"Surely thy Lord knows best those who stray from His path, and He knows best the rightly guided"* (6:117), with its sister at 6:117's context in 28:85 and 68:7. The repetition is doctrine, not filler: the pairing of *man's bankrupt knowledge* with *God's perfect knowledge* is the sūrah's steadying chord, sounded at the point where the Prophet's patience would be most tried. Al-Ālūsī explains the pastoral intent: the caller, surrounded by the wise of the world who mock his "simplicity," must be reminded whose epistemology will be vindicated: the deniers' knowledge ends at the ledger's edge; the Lord's knowledge does not even have an edge — *"and they encompass nothing of His knowledge save what He wills"* (2:255). The caller preaches from the bank of the infinite; it is enough.

**Divine Knowledge as Comfort and as Warning**

The second sentence carries a double edge the commentators unfold with care: *"Surely thy Lord knows best him who strays from His way, and He knows best him who is rightly guided."* As *comfort*: the Prophet is assured that the confused state of his people is *measured* — God knows precisely who strays and why, who is guided and how; no stray soul is lost in the divine accounting, no hidden believer unrewarded. As *warning*: the same precision removes every hiding place — the denier's sophistication, sincerity, or ignorance will be *known* at its true value; and the believer's secret lapses are equally measured. Ibn Kathīr notes the juridical import: the phrase appears in the Qur'an precisely in contexts of contested guidance, as though God said: *leave the classification of hearts to Me* — the Muslim preaches, but never judges the outcome's ledger; and never, likewise, assumes another's ruin before the Knower's verdict. The Prophet's counsel enshrines both edges: *"Do not become a helper of Satan against your brother"* (al-Tirmidhī, graded ḥasan) and the ṣaḥīḥ scene of the funeral at which a companion protested against praying over a man: *"you have decided upon your brother"* — and the Prophet answered: *"God's decree overtakes whom He wills"* (al-Bukhārī 1399; Muslim 963). The scholars' extract is constant: *preach the categories, leave the persons to the Knower.*

**The Verse as the Bridge to Verse 31**

Structurally, verse 30 is the hinge between the sūrah's two halves: the first half (vv. 1–30) has been an epistemological demolition — *their knowledge is that* — and the second half (vv. 31–62) opens with the ontological ground of judgment: *"Unto God belongs whatsoever is in the heavens and whatsoever is on the earth, that He may requite those who do evil..."* (v. 31). The commentators note the logic of the seam: *because* God's knowledge is total (v. 30) and *because* His ownership is total (v. 31), judgment can be total — the two verses are the pillars on which the whole doctrine of requital (jazāʾ) rests. A god who did not know could not judge justly; an owner who was not God could not summon all title-holders. Al-Najm builds its eschatology not on threat but on metaphysics: the Knower and the Owner are one, and the audit that follows (vv. 31–46) is simply His knowledge and ownership *in motion*.

**The Believer's Use: The Freedom of Not Knowing**

The verse's practical gift is the freedom of *intellectual humility*, which the scholars call one of the marks of the sound heart: to know the limits of one's knowledge and to rest there without anxiety. The believer holds three registers without confusion: what *revelation* states (belief, with evidence); what *experience* teaches (probable opinion, held flexibly); what *only God knows* (the hearts, the future, the Hour — *"none knows the unseen in the heavens and the earth save God,"* 27:65). The man who respects the third register cannot be tyrannized by the second or shamed by the first. And the verse's closing cadence — *He knows best him who is rightly guided* — plants the seed the sūrah will harvest in its final verses: the guided are known to God, the strayers are known to God; therefore *strive* (v. 39) — for the striving is what the Knower will find, and the finding is all that will matter when the ledger that never ends finally closes upon the world.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:31

> **Unto God belongs whatsoever is in the heavens and whatsoever is on the earth, that He may requite those who do evil for what they did, and that He may requite those who do good with the most beautiful,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse of the Two Requitals**

The second half of the sūrah opens with a verse that states the constitution of the moral universe: ownership, and the *purpose* of ownership — *li-yajziya* — *"that He may requite."* The structure is staggering in its implications, and the commentators pause on it: the verse does not merely say God owns all and *will* judge; it says He owns all *in order that* He may repay each according to his deeds. Al-Rāzī unpacks the logic: requital requires (a) full ownership of the assets repaid, (b) perfect knowledge of the deeds, (c) absolute power over the term in which payment is rendered — and verse 30 has just certified the knowledge, this clause the ownership; the Hereafter (v. 25) will be the term. The moral universe is thus a *trust structure*: everything in the heavens and the earth is held so that accounts can be settled to the last atom — *"and God created the heavens and the earth in truth, that every soul may be requited for what it earned, and they shall not be wronged"* (45:22). Ibn Kathīr notes the twin clauses of the requital: the evildoers are repaid *bi-mā kānū yafʿalūn* — "for what they used to do" — their deeds echoed back to them; and the doers of good are repaid *bi-al-ḥusnā* — "with the most beautiful" — a repayment that exceeds the deed itself, as the next verse's grammar will show.

**Ownership Precedes Judgment: Why the Verse Opens with Possession**

Why does the verse open with ownership rather than with judgment? The commentators give three converging reasons. First, *jurisdiction*: only the Owner can summon the assets; the pronouncement of universal judgment is intelligible only from universal title — *"unto God belongs whatsoever is in the heavens and whatsoever is on the earth"* recurs as the formula of final accountability at 2:284, 5:120, 57:5. Second, *the refutation continued*: the idolaters had partitioned reality among shrines and gods; the verse reannexes the partition — the heavens are His (their stars, their angels), the earth is His (their temples, their shrines) — so that the judgment about to be described is *within* the territory they thought they had carved up. Third, *reassurance*: the moral accounting is not a metaphysical add-on; it is the *purpose of the estate* — creation exists as the venue of the audit, as 45:22 states in the identical structure. The believer who grasps this sees the world correctly: every object, creature, and hour is inventory in a courtroom already in session.

**"Those Who Do Evil": The Anatomy of the Deed Repaid**

The verse's first requital clause: *li-yajziya alladhīna asāʾū bi-mā ʿamilū* — *"that He may requite those who did evil for what they did."* The verb *asāʾa* denotes *active wrongdoing* — not mere falling into sin but *producing* evil, the manufacture of harm. The commentators note the correspondence built into the repayment: *bi-mā ʿamilū* — the evil-doer is repaid *out of his own account*, the deed itself returned as currency. This is the Qur'an's consistent doctrine of the reciprocity of evil: *"Whoso does evil will be requited only with its like"* (40:40); *"and whatsoever evil befalls thee is from thyself"* (4:79); and the Prophet's gloss on the psychology: *"No man is wronged but the wrong of his wronging redounds upon the wronger"* — the tradition's phrase: *he who wrongs is the first loser*, for the deed follows its doer like a head (*"and every man's deeds have We attached to his neck,"* 17:13). Yet the same tradition balances the terror with the door of repentance: the deed returned is the deed *unrepented*; the sinner who turns is met by the Lord's own announcement: *"I am the Forgiving, the Merciful... I forgive all sins"* (39:53's continuation quoted at v. 32 below). The requital clause is therefore both sentence and alarm bell — the alarm rung while the house still stands.

**"The Most Beautiful": Al-Ḥusnā and the Grammar of Overflow**

The second requital clause changes register entirely: *wa yajziya alladhīna aḥsanū bi-al-ḥusnā* — *"and that He may requite those who do good with the most beautiful."* The commentators savor the root-play: *aḥsanū* (they did *iḥsān* — made good, made beautiful) and *al-ḥusnā* (the most beautiful) share the root *ḥ-s-n*, so that the clause says, in effect: *He repays the beauty-makers with the Most Beautiful.* Al-Bayḍāwī and the majority read *al-ḥusnā* as *the Garden* — the most beautiful of destinations; others read it as *the vision of God*, the most beautiful of rewards; and the tradition harmonizes: the Garden's most beautiful feature is its residents' gazing upon the Face of their Lord, per the ḥadīth of Ṣuḥayb: *"When the people of the Garden enter the Garden... a caller calls: you shall have everlasting life wherein you shall never die... and He who made you beloved to you has made you beloved — and lo, you shall be with your Lord, and He shall delight you"*, and the Prophet recited: *"For those who did good is the most beautiful and increase"* (10:26), explaining: *"when the people of the Garden enter the Garden and the people of the Fire enter the Fire, a herald will call: O people of the Garden, there is with you an appointment with God, He wishes to reward you... then the veils are lifted and they gaze upon His Face; and by God, He will not have given them anything more beloved to them than gazing upon Him."* (Muslim 181–183). The verse's arithmetic thus breaks its own law: evil is repaid *with its like*, goodness is repaid *with the Most Beautiful* — a repayment that is *increase*, as 10:26's verse ends: *"and increase (ziyādah)"* — glossed by Ibn ʿAbbās, per Muslim's narration: *"the vision of God's noble Face."*

**The Verse's Address to Both Fear and Hope**

Al-Qurṭubī notes the psychological architecture: the verse holds *fear* and *hope* in one sentence, on purpose. The first clause walks the wrongdoer past his fantasies — the deed returns, the account opens; the second clause walks the good-doer past his weariness — the repayment is not parity but excess, not wages but gift. The believer is commanded to live *between* the clauses — the Prophet's formula: *"None of you should die save while hoping in God"* (Muslim 2877), and his report of God's word: *"I am as My servant thinks of Me"* (al-Bukhārī 7405). The sūrah will soon seal the balance with verse 32: the good-doers are those who *"shun grave sins... save what is slight — thy Lord is of vast forgiveness"* — hope fenced inside obedience. The verse of the two requitals is thus the chapter's justice-theme in nuce: the same Lord, the same court, one scale that returns the deed — and one hand that adds what no deed earned.

**The Modern Echo: A Universe That Keeps Receipts**

The commentators close with an application the modern reader feels in his bones. Every civilization that loses the doctrine of requital invents substitutes — karma, consequence, "what goes around" — proverbs groping toward 45:22 without its Owner. The verse gives what the proverbs lack: *certainty* (the Owner's oath-backed guarantee, not folk wisdom), *precision* (each soul repaid for *its* deeds, 2:286's prayer: *"grant us not what we cannot bear"*), and *hope* (the requital of good is excess, not exchange). The believer therefore lives as the only fully accounted person in the marketplace: no deed escapes the ledger, but no repentance is refused the eraser — and the knowledge that the *final* receipt will be read aloud at the most beautiful of repayments makes the small honest deeds of an unrecorded Tuesday feel, in faith, like the treasures of a kingdom: *"Whoever does an atom's weight of good shall see it; and whoever does an atom's weight of evil shall see it"* (99:7–8).

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:32

> **those who shun grave sins and indecencies, save what is slight — truly thy Lord is of vast forgiveness. He knows you best, from when He brought you forth from the earth and when you were hidden in your mothers' bellies. So do not declare yourselves pure; He knows best the reverent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Portrait of the Good-Doers Completed**

Verse 31 promised the most beautiful repayment for *al-muḥsinūn* — the doers of good — and verse 32 now paints their portrait: *those who shun the grave sins and the indecencies, save what is slight*. The commentators note the portrait's realism: the blessed are not described as *sinless* but as *shunning* (*yajtanibūna* — actively avoiding) the *kabāʾir* (grave sins) and *fawāḥish* (outrageous indecencies), while carrying, as every son of Adam must, a residue of *lamam* — slight sins. Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī record the tradition's lists: the "grave sins" were enumerated variously by the early scholars — some said seven, citing the Prophet's warning:

> *"Avoid the seven destroyers." They said: O Messenger of God, what are they? He said: "Ascribing partners to God; sorcery; killing the soul God has forbidden save by right; consuming the orphan's wealth; consuming usury; turning back on the day of march; and accusing the chaste, believing women of unchastity."* (al-Bukhārī 2766; Muslim 89)

Others said the grave sins number *seventy-some*, or *three hundred*, or *five hundred* — the transmission of Zarrarah from Imam al-Ṣādiq lists the categories widely — and al-Qurṭubī concludes: the precise enumeration belongs to God; what is agreed is the definition of the lexicographer recorded in the sources: *"a grave sin is one whose retribution is not removed except by repentance."* The believer's map is therefore drawn: some sins are fevers; some are fractures — and the fractures require the surgery of *tawbah*.

**"Save What Is Slight": The Doctrine of Lamam**

The phrase *illā al-lamam* — "save what is slight" — names the residue, and the commentators develop its doctrine with care. Al-Jalālayn: *lamam* is the minor sin from which one repents. The Prophet's ḥadīth, which the sources (Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī) cite to define it, extends sin's anatomy below the deed:

> *"Verily God has decreed for the son of Adam his share of fornication, which he will inevitably commit: the fornication of the eye is the look; the fornication of the tongue is the speech; the soul wishes and longs, and the private parts either confirm it or deny it."* (al-Bukhārī 6243; Muslim 2657)
