Ibn Masʿūd's commentary on the ḥadīth, recorded by Ibn Kathīr: *"The eyes fornicate by looking, the lips by kissing, the hands by touching, the feet by walking [toward the sin]; the private part either confirms all this or denies it: when the act occurs, he is a fornicator; otherwise it is lamam."* The doctrine that emerges: the believer's battle with the small — the glance, the idle step, the fleeting wish — is *expected warfare*, not defeat; the hero of the tradition is not the man who never felt sin's gravity but the man who *shunned the grave*, fought the slight, and kept his garments washed. The Prophet's balance: *"Every son of Adam is a sinner, and the best of sinners are those who repent"* (al-Tirmidhī 2499, graded ḥasan; Ibn Mājah 4251) — and his teaching on the small deeds' weight: *"Do not belittle any good deed, even meeting your brother with a cheerful face"* (Muslim 2626).

**"Vast Forgiveness": The Theology of the Wide Door**

Then the verse pivots to the attribute that makes the portrait livable: *inna rabbaka wāsiʿu al-maghfirah* — *"truly thy Lord is of vast forgiveness."* The word *wāsiʿ* — vast, capacious — the commentators root in *w-s-ʿ*, breadth of extension: forgiveness whose *breadth* matches the *breadth* of His mercy — *"and My mercy encompasses all things"* (7:156). Al-Baghawī notes the verse's placement, as we saw: after the good-doers are described, the vast forgiveness is announced so that *none despair* — neither the lagging believer nor the fallen one; the Qur'an's own commentary is 39:53:

> *"Say: O My servants who have been prodigal against their own souls! Despair not of the mercy of God; surely God forgives all sins; He is the Forgiving, the Merciful."* (39:53)

And the ḥadīth corpus seals it with God's own oath: *"I am as My servant thinks of Me, so let him think of Me as he wishes"* (al-Bukhārī 7405), and the parable of the man who killed a hundred souls and was told: *"what has come between you and repentance?"* (al-Bukhārī 3470; Muslim 2766). The scholars state the conditions plainly — remorse, ceasing, restitution where possible, and resolve — and the Prophet's daily practice: *"By God, I ask God's forgiveness and repent to Him more than seventy times a day"* (al-Bukhārī 6307) — the vast forgiveness sought by the sinless. The verse's comfort has one fence, and the same verse builds it: the vast forgiveness is *toward* those who shun the grave; the door is wide, but the door is a door — and grace presumed becomes the presumption the verse's ending forbids.

**"He Knows You Best": From Adam's Dust to the Womb**

The verse now grounds both the forgiveness and the caution in one staggering sentence: *huwa aʿlamu bikum idh anshaʾakum mina al-arḍi wa idh antum ajinnatun fī buṭūni ummahātikum* — *"He knows you best from when He brought you forth from the earth, and when you were hidden in your mothers' bellies."* The commentators read the two stations of the sentence as the two ends of human history: the race's origin — Adam brought forth from the earth (*anshaʾakum min al-arḍ*, the creation of dust per al-Jalālayn and al-Qurṭubī) — and the individual's origin — the embryo hidden in the womb (*ajinnatun*, from *ajin*, dough: the clotted, kneaded form of 23:14). Between the two stations, God has known *every* human being: the race in its first father, the person in his first darkness. Ibn Kathīr connects the womb-clause to the covenant of the spirits: *"Am I not your Lord?" they said: "Yea, we testify"* (7:172) — and to the ḥadīth of the angel who writes, in the womb, the provision, term, deeds, and destiny (al-Bukhārī 3208; Muslim 2645). The lesson the verse draws is epistemic humility: *He knows you best* — the Arabic *aʿlamu bikum* — He knows you *more than you know yourselves*; the creature who cannot remember his own formation dare not certify his own purity.

**"Do Not Declare Yourselves Pure": The Ethics of Self-Assessment**

The verse's final command — *fa-lā tuzakkū anfusakum* — *"so do not declare yourselves pure"* — the commentators root in *z-k-w*, the root of growth and purity (*zakāh*): do not *grow yourselves* in reputation, do not certify your own souls. The Qur'an's parallel: *"Have you not seen those who declare themselves pure? Nay, God purifies whomsoever He wills, and they shall not be wronged a thread's width"* (4:49). And the Prophet enforced the command socially, as Ibn Kathīr records:

> *"When one of you must praise his companion, let him say: I consider so-and-so to be such-and-such, and God is his Reckoner, and I do not certify anyone's purity before God — if he knows of him what he says."* (al-Bukhārī 2587, in its wording)

The story behind the ḥadīth: a man praised another excessively in the Prophet's presence, and he said: *"Woe to you! You have cut the neck of your companion!"* (al-Bukhārī 2587). The scholars extract the etiquette: speak of deeds seen, leave souls to their Lord; and the deeper ethic — *self-certification is the enemy of growth*: the man who declares himself pure has stopped the diagnosis, and the sick man who denies the sickness dies of it. Yet the tradition holds the balance: *self-assessment* is commanded — *"rehearse your souls before you are rehearsed"* (transmitted from ʿUmar's counsel); *"the wise man is he who calls himself to account"* (al-Tirmidhī 2451, graded gharīb) — what is forbidden is not self-examination but *self-declaration*: the audit is commanded; the acquittal is God's alone. And the verse ends where the believer must end: *huwa aʿlamu bi'l-muttaqīn* — *He* knows best the reverent. The reverent's reverent may be the man no one noticed; the noticed saint may be known otherwise. Live so that the Knower's verdict is the only one you fear — and the only one you seek.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:33

> **Have you seen the one who turned away,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Portrait Series Resumes: A Third Human Type**

With verse 33 the sūrah turns from the portrait of the blessed (v. 32) to open a gallery of the ruined: *a-fa-raʾayta alladhī tawallā* — *"have you seen the one who turned away?"* The Qur'an's interrogative *afaraʾayta* is its standard summons to observe a specimen (*96:11; 96:13; 107:1; 109:1*), and here the specimen is *the turner-away* — *tawallā*, the intensive form of turning: not drifting but *deliberate back-turning*, the same verb as verse 29's *tawallā ʿan dhikrinā*. Al-Ṭabarī and the commentators identify the type: the man who has received the Reminder, weighed it, and rotated his life away from it — toward what, the next verse will show: a religion of self-sufficiency, minimal giving, and final refusal. Al-Rāzī notes the diagnostic sequence: the man does not begin by denying; he begins by *turning* — creed follows posture. The Prophet's psychology of the turned back: *"and We have turned their hearts and eyes away since they did not believe the first time"* (6:110) — the turning is mutual: he turns first; the truth stops knocking.

**The Man Behind the Verse: Al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah**

The occasion of revelation, recorded by al-Ṭabarī and al-Bayḍāwī and summarized in the source commentary, gives the portrait its name. Al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah was Quraysh's aristocrat: wealthy, eloquent, the tribe's negotiator. Hearing the Qur'an, he said — per the reports — *"By God, there is in it a sweetness and upon it a grace; its heights are full and its depths gush"* (transmitted in the tafsīr literature) — and the Qur'an itself records the truce his conscience brokered with his prestige: *"Leave Me [to deal] with him whom I created alone... he reflected and determined... then he turned back and was arrogant... then he said: this is naught but magic handed down"* (74:11–24). Before his final refusal, per the report the commentators transmit, the Quraysh schemed to purchase a Muslim's apostasy: al-Walīd offered wealth to a believing man on condition he abandon the Prophet's religion; the man accepted and returned; and al-Walīd *gave little of the promised sum and then withheld the rest*. The verses of this gallery (33–34) are said to have descended upon that transaction (Bḍ, Ṭ) — the aristocrat's petty default becoming the eternal specimen of the type: the man whose *little* giving and *final* withholding measured the smallness of a soul that had heard the lote tree's news.

**Turning Away as the Root of Every Ruin**

The commentators, reading the gallery as a sequence, observe the order of the man's collapse: (1) he *turned away* — from reminder, from guidance, from the Prophet's call; (2) *gave little* — his religion, cut from obedience, was reduced to transaction; (3) *then withheld* — even the transaction failed; (4) *and did he possess knowledge of the unseen...* (v. 35) — beneath it all, the presumption of security without knowledge. Ibn Taymiyyah's analysis of apostasy and hypocrisy follows the same ladder: no one falls from the heights at once; the fall begins at *tawallī* — the quiet rotation of the heart away from what it knows — and the wallet is merely where the rotation first becomes visible. The believer's self-audit is therefore prescribed at the earliest rung: *watch the turning, not the fall* — the missed prayer defended with a thousand excuses, the Qur'an's shelf gathering dust, the assembly of believers attended less often — these are the *tawallā* that precede and announce the ruin. And the Qur'an's mercy stands in the same verb: the turned man can *turn back* — *tawbah* is the same rotation reversed — until the day the neck is too stiff to turn at all (per the sound anchor is 4:17–18: *"Repentance with God is only for those who do evil in ignorance and then repent soon;... and repentance is not for those who [continue to] do evil until, when death comes to one of them, he says: indeed, I have repented now."*).

**The Qur'an's Method of Portraiture**

It is worth standing back to observe the method this verse inaugurates. The Qur'an rarely refutes a vice in the abstract; it *casts* it — a living man observed, weighed, and left upon the page as a type. The gallery of al-Najm (vv. 33–35) has its siblings across the Book: the man who *"considered himself self-sufficient"* (92:8); the garden's owner who *"entered his garden while wronging himself"* (18:35); and the man of the seventy-fourth sūrah's gallery, al-Walīd himself: *"he reflected and determined... then he turned back and was arrogant... then he frowned and scowled"* (74:18–22). The scholars note the pedagogical power of the portrait over the precept: *the listener meets a person, not a principle* — and persons are harder to forget. The believer who recites this verse is invited to the gallery regularly, not for voyeurism but for diagnosis: *is the specimen walking? has he been seen near my house?* — for the Qur'an's portraits are mirrors angled at every congregation since.

**The Question to the Prophet — and the Question to Us**

Finally, the address: *have you seen?* — the question is put to the Prophet ﷺ, who had seen al-Walīd in the flesh: his silks, his eloquence, his stature in the assembly. The question teaches the Prophet's successors a skill the modern world has inverted: *to see with the verdict already framed by revelation*. The world saw al-Walīd and thought: success. The Qur'an asked the Prophet to look again and see: *the one who turned away*. Ibn ʿAbbās's school drew the rule: the outward eye measures luggage; the eye of revelation measures orientation. The believer inherits the second eye through the Book: he looks at the celebrated and asks not *what do they have?* but *which way are they facing?* — for the only datum that survives the grave is the *tawallā* or the *tawbah*: the direction of the return.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:34

> **and gave little, then withheld?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Economics of a Shrinking Soul**

The portrait's middle panel: *wa aʿṭā qalīlan wa akdā* — *"and he gave little, then withheld."* The two verbs sketch a soul in motion — *away*. First *aʿṭā*: he *gave* — note the past tense: there *was* giving; the man is not depicted as a lifelong miser. He gave *qalīlan* — little: a trickle, a loan of piety, the minimum that maintained his standing. Then *akdā* — and the Arabic here is one of the Qur'an's most startling lexemes, and the commentators linger on it. Al-Bayḍāwī, al-Iṣfahānī, and al-Zamakhsharī (as the source notes, citing Bḍ, Iṣ, Z) trace *akdā* to *al-kudyah*: rock-hard, waterless ground. The Bedouin scene: a man digs a well, drives his shovel down through soft earth, the soil promising — and then the blade rings against *akdā*: bare stone. The digger straightens up, wipes his face, and says the idiom the lexicographers record: *kharra bi-akdā* — *"he struck the rock"* — the idiom for any labor that fails at the last. The Qur'an minted the engineer's despair into theology: *wa akdā* — *and he hit rock.* His giving struck waterless stone; the well of his soul was dry from the start.

**The Sequence as Diagnosis: Little, Then Nothing**

The commentators build the portrait's psychology in three moves. First, *aʿṭā qalīlan*: the little giving was *self-purchase* — the trifle given to buy exemption from the real claim; the Qur'an elsewhere diagnoses the species: *"and when they are told: spend of what God has provided you, those who disbelieve say to those who believe: shall we feed whom, if God willed, He would have fed? You are only in clear error"* (36:47). Second, the *then* (*thumma*): time passes, and the trickle cannot be sustained — for giving flows only from a heart connected to the Giver, and his was turned away (v. 33). Third, *akdā*: the total stoppage — and with it, per the source commentary's insight, *even the past little comes to naught*: for the man had given, if at all, as the hypocrites give — to be seen, to be safe — and the Qur'an rules on such capital: *"like the parable of a rock on which is dust: a downpour strikes it and leaves it bare; they avail nothing of what they earned"* (2:264). The dig's history does not matter when the shovel hits stone: no water was ever down there.

**The Rock Beneath the Well: Why Giving Dries**

Al-Rāzī's analysis of *why* the man's giving failed is a complete pastoral psychology of charity. Giving is the overflow of three convictions: that the wealth is God's (so releasing it is safe), that the Hereafter pays (so releasing it is gain), and that the receiver deserves (so releasing it is just). The turned-away man holds none of the three: the wealth is *his* (so releasing it is loss); the Hereafter is *doubtful* (so releasing it is waste); the receiver is *contemptible* (so releasing it is humbug). His little giving, propped by social convention, could not survive the convictions' absence — hence *thumma akdā*: the conventions wore out, the rock surfaced. The believer's maintenance of generosity is therefore not willpower but *creed maintenance*: the daily correction of the three convictions — *"believe in God and His Messenger and spend from that over which He has made you successors; for those of you who believe and spend — theirs is a great reward"* (57:7). The Prophet's teaching supplies the hydrology: *"Charity does not decrease wealth"* (Muslim 2588); *"When a man spends upon his family seeking God's reward, it is charity for him"* (al-Bukhārī 5351); *"Give, for God will give to you"* (al-Bukhārī 1432: *"and spend, O Bilāl, and do not fear diminishment from the Lord of the Throne"*). The well deepens with every bucket drawn.

**Zakāh and Ṣadaqah: The Law Behind the Portrait**

The commentators note that this portrait stands behind the Qur'an's most severe legal warnings about wealth. The surah's gallery anticipates the legislation of Madinah: the threat against hoarders (*"those who hoard gold and silver and spend it not in the way of God — give them tidings of a painful chastisement,"* 9:34–35); the burned backs of the hoarded treasure on the Day (*"it will be heated in the Fire of hell and their foreheads, sides and backs branded therewith: this is what you hoarded for yourselves,"* 9:35); and the terrifying ḥadīth of the camel of the miser: *"There is not a possessor of a camel... who does not pay its due but it will come on the Day of Resurrection larger and fatter, trampling him with its hooves"* (al-Bukhārī 1460; Muslim 988). The positive institution — *zakāh*, the obligatory purification — is the community's structural answer to the man of verse 34: the believer's giving is not mood but *law*, not trickle but *rate*, so that the soul's rotation cannot quickly silence the hands. And *ṣadaqah*, the voluntary overflow, keeps the heart's tap from rusting: *"and whatever good you put forward for your souls, you will find it with God"* (2:110).

**The Mirrored Mercy: Withholding Judged, Withholding Forgiven**

A final balance for the reciter who recognizes the specimen. The verse condemns the *sequence* — turn, trickle, stop — not the *struggle*: the poor believer who gives little *out of little* is praised by the same Book: *"those who spend in ease and hardship"* (3:134); the widow's mite logic of the Prophet's word: *"the upper hand is better than the lower hand"* (al-Bukhārī 1429) — and the tradition's tenderest case: the man who had nothing but his prayer for the givers. The condemnation falls on *akdā* — the principled stoppage of a heart already turned — and its cure is one verse away in every believer's memory: the return (*tawbah*) that re-digs the well. — the recorded vigilance of the early Muslims, Abū'l-Dardāʾ among them, counting their silver nightly for fear of the verse — marks the safe road: the giving hand checks the heart's turning; the stopped hand follows it. Verse 34 asks every reader only one hydrological question: when your shovel strikes — is there water down there?

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:35

> **Does he have knowledge of the Unseen, such that he sees?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question Beneath the Portrait**

The gallery of the turned-away man closes with the question that exposes his engine: *a-ʿindahu ʿilmu al-ghaybi fa-huwa yarā* — *"has he knowledge of the unseen, so that he sees?"* The rhetorical structure negates the premise: *no, he has no knowledge of the unseen* — and therefore his entire security system is built on a hallucination. Al-Rāzī, reading the verse against its context (vv. 33–34), renders the sense: *this man stopped giving and turned away because he believes his past deeds suffice him and his wealth will be preserved — and upon what evidence? Has he inspected the Unseen? Can he see his own end?* The verse thus names the psychology of refusal: the turned-away man is not reckless; he is *certain* — certain of a future no creature can see. The Qur'an's parallel interrogatives complete the species: *"Or have they [knowledge of] the unseen, so they write it down?"* (52:41); *"Say: none in the heavens and the earth knows the unseen except God"* (27:65); *"Or do they have the treasures of the mercy of your Lord, the Almighty, the Bestower?"* (38:9). The turned man has made himself, in his own accounting, the auditor of a future whose books he cannot open.

**The Unseen (Ghayb): The Qur'an's Boundary of Knowledge**

The word *ghayb* — the unseen — is one of the Qur'an's most load-bearing terms: it names everything real yet hidden from created perception, and the Qur'an partitions it rigorously. Some unseen belongs to God *alone*: the Hour (*"they ask thee of the Hour: when is its landing? ... none can tell its time save He,"* 7:187; and the ḥadīth of Gabriel's fifth question and the Prophet's answer: *"the questioned knows no more than the questioner"* — al-Bukhārī 50; Muslim 8); the souls' fates (*"none knows what is hidden for them of the delight of eyes,"* 32:17); the essence of the divine. Some unseen is *loaned* by God to His prophets: the dreams of Joseph, the tidings of the earlier books, the details of verse 31's requital — *"[He is] the Knower of the unseen, and He reveals not His unseen to any, save to a messenger with whom He is content"* (72:26–27). The verse 35's man claims the *first* category without warrant. The commentators note the precision of *fa-huwa yarā* — "so that he sees": the claim is not of guessed opinion but of *sight* — as though the man's certainty had the confidence of an eyewitness. Ibn Taymiyyah's analysis of arrogance fits: the essence of *istiqbār* (arrogance) is not the loudness of a claim but the *certainty without grounds* — the soul enthroned over its own ignorance.

**The Doctrine of Divine Reserve: Why Some Knowledge Is Withheld**

The commentators step back to state the theology of the verse: *why* does God withhold the unseen? The scholars give four converging reasons. (1) *The test requires it*: a man who saw his own death and judgment could no longer choose — and choice is the purpose: *"to try you as to which of you is best in deed"* (67:2). (2) *Provision requires it*: were next year's wealth visible, trust (*tawakkul*) would be impossible and despair universal; the hidden morrow is the space where faith lives: *"that you may not despair over what has eluded you nor exult over what He has given you"* (57:23). (3) *Dignity requires it*: the seen God would coerce worship; the veiled God invites it — the doctrine the commentary on vv. 16–17 already developed. (4) *The reserve is mercy*: the Qur'an's own argument at 6:59 — *"and with Him are the keys of the unseen; none knows them save He; and He knows what is on the land and in the sea; and not a leaf falls but He knows it"* — teaches that the unseen is *managed* knowledge: the withholding is not God's absence but His guarding of the plan. The man of verse 35 wants his hands on the keys; the believer holds the better deal: the Keyholder's *promise* without the burden of the keys.

**The Three False Securit Theology Refutes**

Al-Qurṭubī systematizes the man's three unspoken premises, each refuted elsewhere in the Qur'an. (1) *My past suffices*: refuted by the ḥadīth of the deed's acceptance — *"Fear God wherever you are..."* and the warning that deeds are sealed at death's door: *"his deeds are cut off"* (Muslim 1631) — sufficiency belongs to the final hour, not the middle one. (2) *My wealth is safe*: refuted by the parable of the garden's owner who awoke to a barren field — *"So his fruits were encompassed [with ruin], and he began to turn his palms over what he had spent"* (18:42) — and by Qārūn's swallow-up: *"and We caused the earth to swallow him and his mansion"* (28:81). (3) *I am exempt*: refuted by the universals of the requital verses — *"every soul shall taste death"* (3:185) and the audit of 99:7–8. The believer's counter-portfolio is the Prophet's counsel: *"Work, for each will be facilitated toward that for which he was created"* (al-Bukhārī 4949; Muslim 2647) and *"Take up deeds that are within your strength"* (al-Bukhārī 6461) — live on the deeds' side of the veil, since the knowledge-side is God's.

**Modernity's Verse: The Illusion of the Dashboard**

The verse's application to the present age is almost uncomfortable. The modern world has built the most elaborate instruments in history for *managing* the future — actuarial tables, insurance, portfolios, predictive algorithms — and the instruments' genius can whisper the verse 35 delusion: *I see.* The commentators' caution, drawn from the Qur'an's own examples, holds: the Pharaoh who "knew" his empire was eternal drowned in a year (the drowning at 10:90–92); the man who knew his harvest was safe lost it overnight (68:17–33); the Quraysh who knew Makkah invincible watched it open (110:1). *Planning* is commanded — the Prophet planned the Hijrah, dug the Trench, mustered the armies — but planning is not *presumption*: the believer plans as a servant holding a map drawn by the Planner. The Prophet's formula fences the two: *"Tie your camel and trust in God"* (al-Tirmidhī 2517, graded ṣaḥīḥ) — the camel tied, the unseen trusted. And his most practical answer to the verse 35 psychology is the daily litany of the morning: *"O God, I ask You for pardon and well-being in my religion, my world, my family, and my wealth... conceal my faults and calm my fears... O God, I ask You for pardon, well-being, and trust in this day and the morrow"* (Abū Dāwūd 5074, graded ṣaḥīḥ) — the believer asks for the morrow's protection precisely because he cannot see it.

**The Question Hangs in the Air**

The verse leaves the question unanswered because the answer is self-evident: *he does not see.* The turned-away man (v. 33), the stopped well (v. 34), the claimed sight of the unseen (v. 35) — the gallery is complete, and the next verse will ask the only remaining question: *has he at least read the warnings?* — *"Or has he not been informed of what is in the scriptures of Moses"* (v. 36) — the tradition's testimony, the second source of knowledge after sight. The man who claims the first source (sight of the unseen) without possessing it, and refuses the second (the scriptures' report) — his epistemology is now empty from both ends. The Qur'an has him cornered; and the corner, as always, is the entrance of mercy: the cornered can still read.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:36

> **Or has he not been informed of what is in the scriptures of Moses**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Witness Is Summoned**

If the turned-away man has no sight of the Unseen (v. 35), the verse now asks whether he has even *heard*: *am lam yukhbar bi-mā fī ṣuḥufi Mūsā* — *"or has he not been informed of what is in the scriptures of Moses?"* The verb *yukhbar* — *been informed* — makes the indictment documentary: there *exists* a report, ancient, written, revered; the man's refusal is therefore not ignorance but *neglect of testimony*. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr identify the *ṣuḥuf* — the "scrolls" or "pages" — as the Torah of Moses and the scrolls of Abraham (named in v. 37), the written inheritances of the two greatest lawgiving prophets. The verse inaugurates the sūrah's longest doctrinal section (vv. 36–48): after the argument from *sight* (the Prophet's vision, vv. 1–18) and the argument from *inspection* (the idols examined, vv. 19–30), comes the argument from *the record* — the moral axioms common to all revelation, printed in the scriptures the Arabs' neighbors revered and the Qur'an now re-certifies.

**Ṣuḥuf: The Word for the Pre-Qur'anic Books**

The lexicographers and commentators note the deliberate choice of *ṣuḥuf* — "scrolls, pages" — rather than *Tawrāt* or *Injīl*. Al-Rāghib al-Iṣfahānī defines *ṣaḥīfah* as a written leaf or gathered pages; the Qur'an uses *ṣuḥuf* for the recorded revelation given to the earlier messengers — *"the scrolls of Abraham and Moses"* (87:18–19) — and the phrase *maṣāḥif* for written copies. The commentators draw two doctrines from the vocabulary. First, *antiquity and independence*: the moral law the sūrah will now recite (vv. 37–48) is not the Prophet's innovation; it is the *old* jurisprudence of heaven — as the Qur'an says of itself elsewhere: *"It is surely in the scrolls of the ancients — the scrolls of Abraham and Moses"* (87:18–19) — so the Makkan listener who revered "the People of the Book's" traditions hears his own ancestors' moral axioms come from them too. Second, *the correspondence argument*: the Qur'an's agreement with the earlier scriptures on fundamentals is itself a proof of its truth — *a book cannot forge its way backward into the archives of its enemies* — and the Qur'an makes the point explicitly: *"He has sent down upon thee the Book in truth, confirming what was before it, and He sent down the Torah and the Gospel"* (3:3).

**Moses Before Abraham: The Order Explained**

The commentators notice a curiosity the source records: Abraham precedes Moses chronologically, yet the *scrolls of Moses* are named first (v. 36) and Abraham's follows (v. 37). Al-Bayḍāwī's answer, transmitted by the tradition: *the scrolls of Moses were more widely known* among the audience — the Torah's centrality to the Jews and Christians of Ḥijāz and the Near East made it the document every hearer knew by reputation; the Qur'an therefore summons the more familiar witness first, then adds the more ancient one. Ibn Kathīr adds the parallel structure: the two scriptures are cited as *paired credentials* of the moral law, exactly as the Qur'an pairs them at 87:18–19, and the *contents* to be cited (vv. 38–48) are those both records share — the doctrines on which heaven's books speak with one voice. The order is thus courtroom strategy: begin with the document the jury knows; finish with the older, deeper source; the two together silence the claim of novelty.

**What the Scrolls Contain: The Axioms Previewed**

The verse's *bi-mā fī* — "of what is in" — promises content, and the next verses deliver it: (1) *none shall bear the burden of another* (v. 38) — individual accountability; (2) *man shall have naught but that for which he endeavored* (v. 39) — the labor-principle; (3) *his endeavor shall be seen* (v. 40); (4) *he shall be repaid the fullest repayment* (v. 41); (5) *the ultimate end is unto thy Lord* (v. 42); (6) *He causes laughter and weeping* (v. 43); (7) *death and life* (v. 44); (8) *the pair's creation from a drop* (vv. 45–46); (9) *the second genesis* (v. 47); (10) *He enriches and contents* (v. 48). Al-Rāzī notes the architectural point: this decalogue of axioms is the *moral physics* common to revelation — the things a man cannot negotiate with because they are the shape of reality, not the rules of a tribe. The turned-away man of vv. 33–35 is now asked: you who pretend to sight of the unseen — have you at least *read the physics*?

**The Qur'an's Method with Earlier Scripture**

The verse models the Qur'an's entire relationship with the earlier books, which the scholars summarize in four movements. *Certification*: the Qur'an confirms the truth of what God actually revealed in them — *"confirming what is between its hands"* (5:48). *Correction*: it distinguishes that revelation from the accretions around it — *"so woe to those who write the Book with their hands and then say: this is from God"* (2:79) — the doctrine of *taḥrīf*, the alteration Ibn Taymiyyah documents as *addition, omission, and interpretation* rather than wholesale forgery. *Re-certification*: it re-issues the true core — exactly what vv. 37–48 do: the axioms of Moses and Abraham are *still* the law, now in the final edition. *Supersession*: it declares itself the safe-keeping edition — *"Today I have perfected for you your religion"* (5:3) — so that the believer needs no rescue operation in corrupted archives; the axioms stand certified *here*. The verse 36's question — *has he not been informed?* — is therefore also addressed to every audience since: the information *exists*; the excuse is not ignorance; it is the turned neck of v. 33.

**The Verse as a Mirror for the "Uninformed" Modern Man**

The commentators close with the verse's permanent application. Every age has its turned-away men who plead the excuse of information's absence — *if I had known, I would have believed*. The verse dismantles the plea: the man had been *informed* — the scrolls were in the public domain, recited in the synagogues of Yathrib, venerated in the churches of Najrān, quoted in the proverbs of his poets. Today the Book is in every pocket: the excuse has never been weaker; the *am lam yukhbar* of our generation is the app icon on a home screen. The believer's task, derived from the verse, is the Prophet's own commissioning logic: *"Convey from me, even a single verse"* (al-Bukhārī 3461) — because the world's refusal often rests not on rejection but on *never having been told* — and the difference between the two is precisely the difference vv. 36–48 are about to draw: the man who heard and turned versus the man never informed. The caller's hope lives on that difference; the verse guarantees it is real.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:37

> **and Abraham, who fulfilled,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Friend of God Enters the Court**

The second witness is named: *wa Ibrāhīma alladhī waffā* — *"and Abraham, who fulfilled."* The verse names no scripture this time; it names a *man* — and a man defined by a single verb. The commentators note the rhetorical shift: Moses is cited for his *book* (the Torah, the codified law); Abraham is cited for his *self* — because Abraham's whole biography is the moral axioms made flesh: he *fulfilled* with his feet (the migrations), his hands (the knife, the building), his heart (the trials), and his covenant (the words of v. 37's sister verse: *"when his Lord tried Abraham with certain words and he fulfilled them"* — 2:124). Al-Bayḍāwī explains the pairing: Moses supplies the *documentation*, Abraham supplies the *demonstration*; the law and the life of the law, standing together as credentials of the axioms about to be recited.

**The Reading of Waffā: Fulfillment in Three Dimensions**

The canonical reading *alladhī waffā* — "who fulfilled" — carries the root *w-f-y*: to *complete, pay in full, discharge entirely*. The commentators record also the variant reading *alladhī waṣṣā* — "who enjoined" (from *waṣṣā*, to bequeath and command), preserved in some of the early masters' readings; and the meanings converge on one portrait: the man who *delivered* — the covenant kept, the command completed, the message bequeathed. The tradition fills in what "fulfilled" contains, and the source commentary lists its three peaks. First, *the fire*: when Abraham smashed his people's idols and they answered with the pyre, and he walked in — *"We said: O fire, be coolness and peace upon Abraham"* (21:69) — the fulfillment of monotheism at the price of the self. Second, *the command to deliver*: the call of his people — *"O my people, worship God; you have no god other than He"* (29:16) — delivered through mockery, exile, and the loneliness the Qur'an honors. Third, *the sacrifice*: the dream-command regarding his son, and the answer of father and son together — *"O my father, do as you are commanded; you will find me, God willing, of the patient"* (37:102) — until the ransom descended: *"and We ransomed him with a mighty sacrifice"* (37:107). And per the ḥadīth corpus, the fulfillment had its rewards: the Friend's rank — *"and God took Abraham as a friend (khalīl)"* (4:125; and the Prophet's word: *"I am the most worthy of Abraham's khalīlship among men"*, Muslim 2370) — his name preserved in the prayer itself (*"O God, bless Muhammad and the family of Muhammad as You blessed Abraham and the family of Abraham"* — the *Ibrāhīmiyyah* of every tashahhud), and the pilgrimage every ḥājj walks in his liturgy.

**"Fulfilled": The Doctrine of Completion in Islam**

The verse's single verb carries a doctrine the scholars unfold: *religion is measured by completion, not initiation*. Abraham did not merely *begin* obedience — he *waffā*: he paid to the last installment. The Qur'an's commendations of completion ring through the Book: the word *wa-fā* appears at the covenant of 2:40 (*"fulfill My covenant, I fulfill your covenant"*), the command of 5:1 (*"fulfill the covenants"*), the character of the truthful (*"and fulfill the covenant; truly the covenant is [a debt] to be questioned of"* — 17:34), and the character of God Himself: *"and who is truer in covenant than God?"* (9:111). The Prophet's biography is the Abraham-pattern renewed: he *fulfilled* the Qur'an with his limbs until ʿĀʾishah's famous verdict: *"His character was the Qur'an"* (Muslim 746), and his dying months were the *completion* of the mission: the Farewell Pilgrimage, the sermon, the final verse, the last prayer — the sound record of the Prophet's final review of the Qur'an with Gabriel (al-Bukhārī 4998). The believer's own religion is graded on the same scale: intentions are beginnings; Islam asks for *wafāʾ* — the prayer prayed to its end, the fast kept to its sunset, the trust held to its term, the covenant kept to the grave.

**Why Abraham for the Arabs: The Lineage of Their Own Book**

The commentators note the strategic perfection of Abraham as witness to the *Makkan* audience. The Arabs knew themselves as *Ibrāhīm's* descendants through Ishmael: the Kaʿbah was, in their own tradition, *his* building (*"and when Abraham and Ishmael raised the foundations of the House,"* 2:127); the pilgrim rites were *his* liturgy worn to ruin; even the idols had colonized *his* temple. The Qur'an therefore does not import a foreign witness; it *repossesses* the audience's own ancestor — and the repossession is devastating: *"Abraham was neither a Jew nor a Christian, but a ḥanīf, a muslim, and not of the idolaters"* (3:67). The man whose temple they kept, whose rites they performed, whose name they prayed upon — *was the man who smashed the idols*. Every rite of the Jahiliyyah thus stood as an unpaid memory of the axioms the sūrah is about to recite: the idolaters' own shrine was exhibit A for the prosecution. The scholars add the theological crown: Islam's self-definition as *millat Ibrāhīm* — *"the creed of Abraham, the ḥanīf"* (2:135; 3:95; 6:161) — is not rhetoric but restoration: the final book returns the Arabs' — and mankind's — faith to its patriarchal source, purified of every name the fathers had laid upon it (v. 23's crime, reversed).

**The Axioms That Both Witnesses Carry**

The verse's position — *and Abraham, who fulfilled* — is the conjunction that fuses the two witnesses into one testimony: what was *in the scrolls of Moses* (v. 36) is *also* what Abraham fulfilled and enjoined. The list that follows (vv. 38–48) is therefore presented as the *common property* of heaven's books — the shared moral physics that no authentic scripture ever contradicted. Al-Rāzī draws the apologetic force: the Qur'an invites — even dares — its audience to check: consult the Jews' Torah, the Christians' memories of their patriarch; the axioms of accountability, labor, requital, resurrection are *there* — and their presence in the Qur'an, revealed by an unlettered man among pagans, certifies its source with the argument of 28:48–49: *"Say: then bring a book from God which is a better guide than both, that I may follow it, if you are truthful."* The verse's last gift to the believer is personal: he too is asked to be *Abraham, who fulfilled* — the man whose life, when the record is read, shows the axioms *carried out*: accountability lived, labor spent, the covenant paid in full. The next verse begins the recital — and the first axiom is the heaviest: *no bearer of burdens bears the burden of another.*

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:38

> **that no bearer of burdens shall bear the burden of another,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Axiom of the Scrolls**

Here begins the recital of what is *in the scriptures of Moses and what Abraham fulfilled* — and the first axiom is the axiom of all moral existence: *an lā yaziru wāziratun wizra ukhrā* — *"that no bearer of burdens shall bear the burden of another."* The vocabulary is heavy by design: *wizr* names a load that *crushes* — the root *w-z-r* is the word for a burdened beast, a laden ship, a chest constricted by sin (*"and We shall bind their burdens upon their backs,"* 7:41; *"that your breasts be straitened,"* 6:125's negative image). Al-Rāzī notes the axiom's necessity: without it, morality dissolves — if burdens could be transferred, punishment would be theater and obedience meaningless; the entire economy of deeds requires that each load stay welded to its owner. The Qur'an states this axiom *five times* across the Book — 6:164; 17:15; 35:18; 39:7; 53:38 — and al-Ṭabarī records that this, at 53:38, is the *first* instance in the chronological order of revelation: the foundational axiom of the new scripture, revealed at the height of the Makkan mission, engraved in the oldest scrolls.

**The Verse's Full Statement Elsewhere: Kinship Cannot Pay**

The commentators cite the axiom's longest Qur'anic form, where the limits are drawn with terrible precision:

> *"And no bearer of burdens bears the burden of another; and if one heavily burdened calls for his burden to be carried, naught of it shall be carried, even were he of near kin."* (35:18)

The clause *"even were he of near kin"* forecloses the last human hope: blood does not transfer guilt. The father's piety cannot pull the son from the Fire; the son's grief cannot lighten the father's grave. The Qur'an dramatizes the foreclosing at the Flood: Noah cries to God for his own son — *"My Lord, my son is of my family, and Thy promise is true"* — and the answer draws the boundary between natural and spiritual kinship: *"He is not of thy family; he is [a deed] other than righteous"* (11:45–46). And it dramatizes the reverse: the believing wife of Pharaoh prays under the tyrant's roof, and her faith saves *her* (66:11) — the household of the greatest tyrant contained the axiom's brightest proof: *no one rides another's burden, up or down.*

**The Axiom's Liberation: Original Sin Refuted and Collective Guilt Refused**

Ibn Taymiyyah and the commentators before him note that this axiom, revealed in Makkah among polytheists, quietly refutes two errors humanity gravitated toward. First, *inherited guilt*: the notion that later generations carry the sin of an ancestor — the doctrine the Qur'an rejects in the Jews' claim *"the fire shall not touch us save a number of days"* and in every theology that loads Adam's slip upon his children's souls: *"Whoso is guided is guided only for his own soul, and whoso strays strays only against it; and no bearer of burdens bears the burden of another; and We never chastise until We send a messenger"* (17:15). Every soul begins clean: *"each is held in pledge for his deed"* (74:38) — the ledger opens at zero and fills only with what the hand itself writes. Second, *tribal guilt-refusal*: the pagans' doctrine that the tribe's solidarity would settle accounts — *"we found our fathers upon a creed, and we follow their traces"* answered by *"even though their fathers understood naught and were not guided?"* (2:170) — and the Prophet's harder word to his own kin: *"O Quraysh, redeem yourselves, for I avail you naught against God... O Fāṭimah, daughter of Muhammad: ask me what you will of my wealth, but I avail you naught against God"* (al-Bukhārī 4741; Muslim 205). The axiom cuts both ways: no one inherits your sin — and no one inherits your salvation.

**The Balance: Influence Is Real — and Charged**

Yet the axiom is not the doctrine of moral isolation. The Qur'an and Sunnah hold, in the same hand, the doctrine of *influence*: the man who *leads* another into wrong carries his own burden *plus* a share of the led. The ḥadīth the source cites:

> *"Whoso establishes a good practice (sunnah ḥasanah) has its reward and the reward of whoso acts upon it after him, without their rewards being diminished; and whoso establishes an evil practice (sunnah sayyiʾah) has its sin and the sins of whoso acts upon it after him, without their sins being diminished."* (Muslim 1017)

And the Qur'an's own double-burden clause: *"that they may bear their burdens complete upon the Day of Resurrection, and part of the burdens of those they lead astray without knowledge"* (16:25) — the leaders of the Fire refusing entry ahead of their followers (7:38–39; and the double chastisement cursed at 33:68). The scholars reconcile the two doctrines exactly: *the ledger of deeds is private; the ledger of influence is shared.* My sin does not transfer; my sedition *multiplies*. The influencer's terror is therefore real — the Prophet's word to the jurist who would judge rashly: *"whoever judges rightly has two rewards; whoever judges wrongly has one"* (al-Bukhārī 7352) — while the teacher's terror is even realer, per his warning about scholars whose learning misleads: *"Whoever is asked about knowledge and conceals it will be bridled with a bridle of Fire"* (Abū Dāwūd 3658; al-Tirmidhī 2649).

**The Axiom in Daily Life: The End of Blame and the Burden of Agency**

The verse's practical harvest, which the commentators enumerate, is threefold. (1) *The end of excuse*: the phrase *"so-and-so misled me"* will not open the Last Day's locks — *"they said: our Lord, we obeyed our masters and our great ones, and they led us astray; our Lord, give them double chastisement"* (33:67–68) — the led are punished too, *for being led*. Every addiction, every betrayal, every lapse has its accomplices; the accomplices answer for their share, and the soul answers for its own. (2) *The end of despair for parents*: the parent cannot *believe* for the child — but the axiom frees the believing parent from the false mathematics of total responsibility: seed and water belong to the sower; the growth is God's (*"thou guidest not whom thou lovest,"* 28:56). The parent's duty is the Prophet's duty: convey, love, supplicate — and stand at the boundary of the child's own choice. (3) *The dignity of agency*: the axiom is the moral charter of the individual — you are *your own* ledger, answerable for *your own* hand, capable — this is the axiom's bright side — of a full account settled *by yourself*: *"whoso does an atom's weight of good shall see it"* (99:7). No tribe can drown your good deeds; no ancestor can bankrupt your account. The heaviest verse in the scrolls is also the most freeing: *no one else can live your Islam, and no one else can lose it.*

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:39

> **and that man shall have naught but that for which he endeavored,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Axiom: The Law of Labor**

The second axiom of the scrolls is the economics of destiny: *wa an laysa li'l-insāni illā mā saʿā* — *"and that the human being shall have naught but that for which he strove."* The verb *saʿā* names *striving with fatigue* — the root *s-ʿ-y* is the gait of effort: the walking that tires (*"and that man shall have naught but that for which he strove"*), the running of the pilgrim between Ṣafā and Marwah (which the rite itself calls *saʿy*), the traveler's push through the day's heat. The commentators mark the verb's choice: the verse does not say man shall have *what he wished* (v. 24 has just dismissed that) nor *what he was given*; he shall have *what he wore out his legs to reach*. Al-Rāzī calls this verse the *charter of moral responsibility in one clause*: destiny is not inherited, not allotted by luck, not purchasable by lineage — it is *worked*, and only the work pays out. The Qur'an's sister statements frame it: *"that He may try you as to which of you is best in deed"* (67:2); *"for each are ranks according to what they did"* (46:19); *"and every man's deeds have We attached to his neck"* (17:13).

**What the Verse Negates: Every Borrowed Salvation**

The commentators draw out the axiom's negatives with care, for its force is in its *illā* — "only." (1) *Not lineage*: the Prophet's word, in his final illness, to the dearest lineage on earth: *"O Fāṭimah, daughter of Muhammad! Ask me what you will of my wealth, for I avail thee naught against God"* (Muslim 205; al-Bukhārī 4741) — birth itself purchased nothing without deeds. (2) *Not the intercession of idols* — the sūrah's whole first half has demolished the purchased mediator. (3) *Not mere wishes*: the ḥadīth of the three men trapped in the cave (al-Bukhārī 2272; Muslim 2743) shows even *righteous deeds* interceding only as *earned* capital — the dutiful son, the chaste laborer, the honest steward each cited *spent* virtue, not intentions. (4) *Not the pure gift of status*: salvation in Islam is by *grace through works* — the Prophet's own formula, which he repeated until it became the believers' creed: *"None of you will enter the Garden by his deeds alone."* They said: not even you, O Messenger of God? He said: *"Not even I, unless God covers me with His mercy"* (al-Bukhārī 6463; Muslim 2816). The axiom is thus the *means* doctrine inside the *grace* doctrine: mercy is the currency, and striving is the outstretched hand.

**What the Verse Affirms: The Dignity of Effort**

The positive side the commentators unfold is equally vast: the verse *dignifies labor as the only currency of the soul*. Three corollaries follow. First, *equality of access*: the axiom is aristocracy's refutation and democracy's charter — the slave Bilāl's *saʿy* outranked the nobles' lineage; the Prophet's word: *"God does not look at your bodies or your forms, but at your hearts and your deeds"* (Muslim 2564). Second, *the meaning of time*: if only striving pays, then the hours are the currency, and their waste is literal bankruptcy — the Prophet's counsel: *"there are two blessings most people defraud themselves of: health and free time"* (al-Bukhārī 6412). Third, *the cure of despair*: no soul is condemned to its past; the ledger pays *forward* — the ḥadīth of the man who killed ninety-nine and strove toward repentance and died *of the people of the Garden* (al-Bukhārī 3470; Muslim 2766) — the striving *itself*, wherever it stands in the story, is what the verse's *mā saʿā* counts.

**The Deeds That Outlive the Doer: The Three That Continue**

The axiom has a famous proviso, and the commentators at this verse cite it — for it answers the obvious question: *can a man have what he did not personally finish?* The Prophet said:

> *"When the son of Adam dies, his deeds are cut off except three: a continuing charity, or knowledge that is benefited from, or a righteous child who supplicates for him."* (Muslim 1631)

The scholars reconcile: these three are not *exceptions* to the labor-law but its *extensions* — each is *the man's own saʿy* continuing to pay: the well he dug still waters; the lesson he taught still teaches; the child he raised by his own striving now strives for him. the sound example: the early Muslims asked about their dead of the pre-Islamic era, and were taught the boundary of the axiom. The practical harvest: *build what outlives you* — the ḥadīth's three items define the only estate planning the Hereafter honors: endowed charity, planted knowledge, and children raised to remember you before God. The Prophet's own "continuing charity" is the Qur'an itself; the believer's may be as small as a tree planted, a hadith narrated, a daughter taught Surah al-Fātiḥah — the axiom counts them all as *mā saʿā*: what you strove.

**Striving as Worship: The Verse in the Believer's Week**

The commentators close with the verse's devotional application. Islam sanctified *saʿy* so thoroughly that the pilgrim literally *strives* seven times between two hills in commemoration of a mother's striving — Hagar's run for water — and drinks from the well her striving found. The lesson engraved in the rite matches the verse: God *answers* the striver; the desert of Bakkah yielded water to the running of a bondwoman, and Zamzam has not stopped in four thousand years. The believer therefore reads verse 39 not as threat but as invitation: every great thing in the tradition began as someone's *saʿy* — the night vigil of the Qurayshī orphan, the parched miles of the Anṣār, the memorized verse in a jailed scholar's cell. And the verse's shadow warns in the same measure: the *striving* of the wrong direction also pays — the Quraysh strove against the Prophet and received the wages of that labor; the hypocrites strove to undermine and were paid in kind: *"We shall proceed to the deeds they did and make them scattered dust"* (25:23). The prayer of the verse's student is therefore the Prophet's daily plea for the right direction of striving: his constant istighfār — *"I seek God's pardon and turn to Him"* more than seventy times a day (al-Bukhārī 6307; Muslim 2702). *Mā saʿā*: the ledger asks only one question — *what did you strive for?* — and eternity is the payout schedule.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:40

> **and that his endeavoring shall be seen,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Axiom: The Audit Is Witnessed**

The sequence of the scrolls' axioms continues: *wa anna saʿyahu sawfa yurā* — *"and that his striving shall be seen."* The future tense *yurā* — *shall be seen* — lifts the labor-law of verse 39 into the courtroom of the Last Day: the striving will not merely *pay* (v. 41), it will first be *displayed*. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr read the verse against its sister texts: *"On that day mankind shall come forth in scattered groups to be shown their deeds"* (99:6); *"and they find what they did present"* (18:49); *"that day you shall be exposed — no secret of yours shall be hidden"* (69:18). The commentators note the implications for the laborer: (1) *nothing is lost* — the smallest effort is on the record, as 99:7–8 declares atom by atom; (2) *nothing is private* — the hidden deed will be *seen*, and per the ḥadīth: *"every soul will be paired with the ledger: 'this is your book; its recording does not neglect a small thing or a great save it has counted it'"* (18:49's wording quoted in the tafsīr); (3) *nothing is merely human* — the seeing is God's first, and the witnessing assembly's after: *"and you shall be brought back to the Knower of the unseen and the seen, and He will inform you of what you used to do"* (9:105's formula quoted by the source).

**The Verse's Charge Sheet: Living as the Watched**

The verse's practical force the scholars develop as the doctrine of *murāqabah* — the vigilance of living before the Watcher. The Qur'an establishes the watching in the present: *"He is with you wherever you are, and God of what you do is seeing"* (57:4); *"and We are nearer to him than the jugular vein"* (50:16); the recording angels: *"each has watchers before him and behind him, guarding him by God's command"* (13:11); *"two receivers, seated right and left — he utters no word but there is a watcher ready"* (50:17–18). Verse 40 announces the *release* of that surveillance footage at the audit: *"and his striving shall be seen."* The commentators supply the Prophetic psychology that turns the doctrine into character: *iḥsān* itself — the third degree of religion in the hadith of Gabriel — is defined as *"that you worship God as though you see Him; for if you see Him not, He sees you"* (al-Bukhārī 50; Muslim 8). The man who *lives* verse 40 does not need the cameras of conscience-building gurus; his conduct is uniform because his audience is constant — the Prophet's counsel to Ibn ʿAbbās: *"Know God in ease, He will know you in hardship"* (al-Tirmidhī 2516, graded ḥasan), and the ḥadīth of the seven shaded, which includes *"a man who remembers God in private and his eyes flow [with tears]"* — the private audience rewarded in public.

**What Is "Seen": The Three Audiences of the Audit**

The commentators distinguish three layers of the seeing, each with its date. (1) *God sees now*: the omniscient witness of 57:4 — nothing to wait for; (2) *the deed is seen in its consequences now*: every striving ripples — the teacher's student, the liar's wreckage, the donor's village — the Qur'an's parable: *"a good word is as a good tree... and an evil word is as an evil tree"* (14:24–26); (3) *the full seeing comes at the Resurrection*: the film of the life played to its owner — *"they find what they did present, and thy Lord wrongs no one"* (18:49), and the description of the Assembly where *"you shall see a people [as] drunk, yet they are not drunk, but the chastisement of God is severe"* (22:2) and the faces of the two companies read aloud (80:38–41). The believer plans for the third seeing while remembering the first: the man who would be ashamed of a deed before his family has already been seen by a greater Audience — the Prophet's criterion for the secret sin: *"All my ummah are excused except the one who sins openly"* (al-Bukhārī 6069; Muslim 2990) — and its psychology: *concealment before men is possible; concealment before God is not.*

**Endeavor (Saʿy) Versus Deeds (ʿAmal): Why the Verse Says "Striving"**

Al-Rāzī notes the verse's word: not merely *ʿamal* (deed) but *saʿy* (striving) — the *effort* itself will be seen, not only its outputs. The doctrine matters at the margins where effort and output diverge: the daʿwah that no one attended, the sermon that changed no one, the reform that failed, the charity that was stolen, the fast broken by illness — the *striving* is on the record, and its reward is guaranteed by the Prophet's word: *"Whoever intends a good deed and does not do it, God writes it as a full good deed; and if he does it, ten to seven hundred fold and many times over. Whoever intends a sin and does not do it, God writes it as a full good deed; and if he does it, God writes one sin"* (al-Bukhārī 6491; Muslim 131). And the ḥadīth of the disqualified deed — the martyr who sought fame, the scholar who sought fame, the giver who sought fame, dragged to the Fire (Muslim 1905) — is the same doctrine in the negative: the *striving's aim* is what is seen; the deed is only the striving's footprint. The believer's craft is therefore aim-craft: *"Deeds are only by intentions"* (al-Bukhārī 1) — and the intent is the first thing the audit screens.

**The Verse's Comfort: Your Labor Is Not Sand**

For every worker in God's cause whose results seem invisible, verse 40 is the standing reassurance: the striving *shall be seen* — if not by the community, then by the Lord of the ledger; and what He sees, He pays. The commentators cite the Qur'an's own rebuke and consolation of the despairing reformer: *"perhaps you would kill yourself with grief because they believe not"* (26:3); *"thy Lord has not abandoned thee nor despised thee"* (93:3); *"so let not thy soul expire in sorrow over them"* (35:8); *"thou art not one to compel them"* (88:21–22); *"thy duty is but conveyance, and Ours the reckoning"* (13:40). The laborer's salary is not the harvest; it is the *seeing* — and the seeing is certain. The next verse states the wage schedule: *he shall be repaid for it the fullest repayment* — the striving seen (v. 40) is the striving repaid (v. 41); between them, the believer walks his Tuesday as a man whose every step is being filmed for the only screening that will matter.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:41

> **whereupon he shall be repaid for it the fullest repayment,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Wage Announced: Thumma Yuwaffāhu Ajrahu**

The axioms reach their payout clause: *thumma yuwaffāhu ajrahu al-awfā* — *"then he shall be repaid his reward in full — the utmost."* The grammar is precise: *yuwaffā* — the root *w-f-y*, the same root as Abraham's *waffā* in verse 37: *to complete, to pay to the last installment* — God's repayment is a *discharge in full*, the account not merely settled but *over-settled*: *al-awfā*, "the fullest," the superlative of complete payment. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī note the doubling of the assurance: *ajrahu* (his reward — personal, earmarked) + *al-awfā* (the fullest — measured to overflowing). The Qur'an's parallel formulas ring around it: *"Whoso brings a good deed shall have ten the like of it"* (6:160); *"the parable of those who spend their wealth in the way of God is as the parable of a grain that grows seven ears, in every ear a hundred grains; and God multiplies for whomsoever He wills"* (2:261); *"and naught is the recompense of good but good"* (55:60). The mathematics of the Hereafter is not exchange but *expansion* — and the commentators cite the Prophet's report of God's joy at the repentant's multiplication (Muslim 2747); and the faster multiplication: *"Whoever fasts Ramadan out of faith and hope, his former sins are forgiven"* (al-Bukhārī 38; Muslim 760) — the repayment outstripping the debt entirely.

**The Fullest Repayment Is a Person: The Vision of God**

The tradition's crowning reading, which the source records (al-Rāzī), identifies *al-awfā* — the fullest repayment — with *the vision of God*; and the ḥadīth corpus supplies the full doctrine. Muslim transmits from Ṣuḥayb:

> *"When the people of the Garden enter the Garden and the people of the Fire enter the Fire, death will be brought and placed between the Garden and the Fire, then slaughtered; then it will be said: O people of the Garden, everlasting life and no death; O people of the Fire, everlasting life and no death. Then the Prophet recited: 'And warn them of the Day of Regret, when the matter is decided...' Then a herald will call: O people of the Garden, you have an appointment with your Lord, He wishes to reward you. They say: and what is that? ... So they come, and thrones are set, and then their Lord appears to them, and they say: before Him are those who prostrated... Then the veils are lifted and they gaze upon His Face — and by God, He will not have given them anything more beloved to them than the gazing upon Him."* (Muslim 181–183)

The Prophet then recited: *"For those who did good is the most beautiful and increase"* (10:26) — glossing *ziyādah* (increase) as *"the gazing upon the Face of God."* And Muslim transmits the promise of the Sunday of the Garden:

> *"You shall see your Lord on the Day of Resurrection as you see this moon, and you shall not be wronged in seeing it."* (Muslim 182)

The scholars' doctrine: the vision is real, suited to the seer's created capacity, and *the* reward of rewards — for it answers the deepest hunger of the heart, the ache that no created beauty stills. The commentators note the placement's poetry: the sūrah that opened with the Prophet's vision of the greatest signs now promises the *believer* a seeing — graded to his station, but the same direction of travel: from the oath by the star to the Face unveiled.

**The Word "Then": The Order of the Audit**

The particle *thumma* — "then, after that" — sequences the repayment *after* the seeing of v. 40: striving is displayed, *then* repaid. Al-Rāzī draws the juridical order: display (the evidence), then discharge (the sentence) — the audit precedes the wage, and the wage follows *only* the audited record: *"and his striving shall be seen, then he shall be repaid for it the fullest."* The believer who grasps the sequence lives it in miniature at every prayer's end: the service rendered, then the Master's recompense — after prayer, the believer *expects* — *"and whoever hopes to meet his Lord, let him do righteous deed and associate none in the worship of his Lord"* (18:110). The *thumma* is also a mercy: the display comes first so that the wage is *seen to be earned* — the divine court does not pay blind; it pays *witnessed*, and the witnessing is the honor.

**Al-Awfā in the Ledger of the Two Companies**

The commentators set the verse beside its dark mirror in the same sūrah: the evil-doer of v. 31 is repaid *"for what they did"* — *bi-mā kānū yaʿmalūn*, at parity; the good-doer is repaid *al-awfā* — at excess. The asymmetry is doctrine: *"if you lend God a goodly loan He will multiply it for you and forgive you"* (64:17); and the Prophet's word on the two ledgers: *"God has written the good deeds and the bad deeds... whoever intends a good deed and does not do it, God writes it as a full good deed; whoever intends it and does it, ten to seven hundred times... whoever intends a sin and does not do it, a full good deed; whoever does it, one sin"* (al-Bukhārī 6491). The believer's hope is thus mathematically grounded: the *fullest* repayment is God's sworn accounting style; the only species that gets parity is disbelief, and even that because it *requested* parity: *"We wrong them not, but they were the wrongdoers"* (43:76). The verse's comfort is therefore not vague optimism but *terms of contract* — and the contract's signature is verse 39's striving.

**The Fullest Repayment and the Believer's Ambition**

The tradition turns the verse into ambition. ʿUmar's counsel, recorded in the literature: *"Trade this world for the Hereafter and you will profit both; and do not trade the Hereafter for this world and lose both."* The Prophet described the highest ambition of the people of the Garden themselves: after the veils lift and they see Him, *"they forget the Garden's delights as though they had never seen them"* (the wording of Muslim 181–183's narration). The believer's workweek is thus re-scaled by v. 41: the paycheck of Friday and the promotion of springtime are *provision* — the *ūlā* of verse 25 — while the *awfā* is the real payday, seen by the striving's audit, paid by the Hand that promised: *"so whoso was given the Book and believes in what has been sent down to thee... they shall have their reward twice over"* (28:54; cf. 34:37's principle: *"and their striving shall be known, then you shall not be wronged"*) — and the final word of the commentators on this verse: the man who has seen the wage schedule no longer haggles over the job description; he only asks, with the Prophet's own ambition: *"O God, I ask You for Paradise and what draws near to it of word and deed"* (Abū Dāwūd 1490, graded ṣaḥīḥ) — and flees the opposite — for the fullest repayment is coming, and it is coming *for everyone*, in one of two currencies.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:42

> **and that the ultimate end is unto thy Lord,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fourth Axiom: Everything Terminates in Him**

The recital of the scrolls' axioms now turns from man's labor to man's *destination*: *wa anna ilā rabbika al-muntahā* — *"and that the terminus is unto thy Lord."* The word *al-muntahā* is the sūrah's own coinage returning: it named the *lote tree of the boundary* in verse 14 — creation's farthest landmark, the point beyond which no creature passes. Now the same word is applied to all existence: every road ends at *thy Lord*. Al-Rāzī and al-Ālūsī unfold the doctrine's layers. (1) *The return of all things*: every effect returns to its cause, every creature to its Creator — *"as He originated you, you shall return"* (7:29); *"unto Him is the return, all of it"* (11:4); *"unto God is the [final] return"* (64:3). (2) *The termination of all changing things*: perfections are God's alone; created goods are roads, not homes — the mystic commentators' principle: *the Giver is the terminus, the gifts are milestones.* (3) *The court of final appeal*: all judgments, disputes, and ledgers end at His bench — *"then they are returned to God, their true Protector"* (6:62). The commentators also record the grammatical note: *wa anna* may be read *wa inna* — "and truly" — making the verse a fresh oath-sealed assertion; either reading serves the same doctrine: the end of the road has a name, an address, and a Face.

**The Two Readings of the Terminus: Judgment Day and the Necessary Being**

The commentators distinguish two great readings of *al-muntahā*. The first, the majority reading: the *Day of Judgment* — the verse continues the sūrah's audit-theme: every striving is seen (v. 40), repaid (v. 41), *and the destination of the whole process is thy Lord's court*. Al-Qurṭubī gathers the sister verses: *"faces that day shall be humbled before the Living, the Self-Subsisting"* (20:111); *"the day they are ushered forth — naught of them is hidden from God"* (40:16). The second reading, carried by al-Rāzī among the philosophers' jurists: the terminus is God *as the Necessary Being* — the One whose non-existence is impossible, the source on which all contingent being rests and to which, in the order of reality, everything is referred. The two readings harmonize: *historically*, every soul arrives at His court; *ontologically*, every soul depends on His being at every instant — the Prophet's own compressed creed: *"my life and my death are [devoted] to God, Lord of the worlds"* (6:162) — and the believer's prayer lives in the first reading while his intellect rests in the second.

**The Terminus of Two Roads: The Sūrah's Fork**

Al-Qurṭubī notes that the verse's comfort depends on the road traveled, and the Qur'an itself forks the terminus in the very vocabulary of *muntahā*: *"the parable of the Garden promised to the reverent: rivers flowing beneath, its food everlasting and its shade — that is the terminus (ʿuqbā) of the reverent; and the terminus of the disbelievers is the Fire"* (13:35). The single Lord is the terminus of *both* roads — as Judge to the one, as Beloved to the other. The commentators supply the emotional theology of the fork: the believer's arrival is reunion — the ḥadīth of the Garden's call: *"O soul at rest, return to thy Lord, pleased and pleasing"* (89:27–28 recited over the scene); the disbeliever's arrival is exposure — *"they shall come forth, their eyes humbled as those rising from graves"* (70:43). And the fork's door remains open *here* alone: the Prophet's word: *"God accepts the repentance of the servant so long as the soul has not reached the throat"* (al-Tirmidhī 3513, graded ṣaḥīḥ). The verse's gaze — *the terminus is unto thy Lord* — is therefore the believer's daily compass-check: whichever road I am walking *this hour*, I am walking *toward Him*; the only question is His posture at the end of it: the Host or the Judge.

**"Thy Lord": The Possessive That Comforts the Messenger**

The verse says *ilā rabbika* — *thy* Lord — the possessive of intimacy addressed to the Prophet ﷺ in his hardest season. The commentators note the pastoral logic: the Messenger, mocked and boycotted, is reminded that the terminus is *his* Lord — the same Lord who showed him the lote tree and the two bows; the man who has seen the road's end cannot be destabilized by its middle. The pattern recurs across the Qur'an at the Prophet's darkest hours: at the boycott's valley, at Ṭāʾif's stones, at the death of his own — *"thy Lord has not abandoned thee nor despised thee"* (93:3); *"and truly thy Lord will give thee, and thou shalt be content"* (93:5). The believers inherit the possessive through him: the Lord of the Prophet is *our* Lord — *"Say: God — then leave them in their play"* (6:91's principle: *"God suffices me; there is no god but He; upon Him I trust, and He is the Lord of the mighty Throne,"* 9:129). The believer's recitation of this verse at a graveside or a hospital corridor is the tradition's oldest comfort: the terminus is not the wall of the world; it is *a Person* — and He has a relationship with the dying believer that the ḥadīth describes in the tenderest terms: *"When God loves a servant, He summons Gabriel: I love so-and-so, so love him..."* (al-Bukhārī 7377; Muslim 2637) — the terminus road runs through friendship.

**The Verse in the Sūrah's Architecture**

Structurally, verse 42 is the keystone of the axioms (vv. 38–48): after accountability (v. 38), labor (v. 39), seeing (v. 40), repayment (v. 41), the recital now names the *court itself* (v. 42), and the remaining axioms describe the Judge's powers — laughter and weeping (v. 43), death and life (v. 44), creation of the pair (vv. 45–46), the second genesis (v. 47), enrichment and contentment (v. 48). Al-Rāzī notes the crescendo: the axioms move from *what man owes* (burden, labor) to *what God does* (seeing, repaying, terminating, creating, reviving, providing) — the scrolls' theology ends where all sound theology ends: with God. And the believer leaves the verse with its prayer: the five daily petitions of the Fātiḥah (*"Thee alone we worship, and Thee alone we ask for help — guide us upon the straight path"*) are simply verse 42 in the grammar of a day: the terminus is unto thy Lord — so worship *toward* Him, ask *of* Him, and walk *the road whose end is Him.*

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:43

> **and that it is He who causes laughter and weeping,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fifth and Sixth Axioms: The Lord of the Heart's Weather**

The recital of the axioms now descends into the most intimate register of human experience: *wa annahu huwa aḥḥaka wa abkā* — *"and that He is He who makes to laugh and makes to weep."* The two verbs are intensives — *aḥḥaka* (from *ḥ-k-k*, per the commentators the intensive form of *daḥka*, to laugh) and *abkā* (from *b-k-y*, to weep) — the Arabic form that names not the occasion of an emotion but its *authorship*. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī read the verse as the declaration that God decrees the causes of every joy and every sorrow, every smile in the marketplace and every tear at every graveside: *"that is God's bounty, He gives it to whomsoever He wills; and God is of bounty immense,"* (62:4); *"and if God touched thee with hurt, none removes it save He; and if He touched thee with good, He is over all things powerful"* (6:17). The commentators place the verse against its background: the Makkan pagans assigned joy and grief to their idols and their fates — Manāt the apportioner, al-ʿUzzā the might; the axiom returns the heart's weather to its true Owner: *He* causes laughter and *He* causes weeping.

**The Three Readings: This World, the Hereafter, the Two Together**

Al-Qurṭubī records the three classical readings of the verse's reference, and each carries doctrine. (1) *This world*: God causes the joys and sorrows of earthly life — the reading that makes the verse a charter of patience: no calamity arrives from a rival power; (2) *the Hereafter*: God causes the *laughter of the Garden* — *"faces that day shall be shining, radiant, laughing, joyous"* (80:38–39) — and the *weeping of the Fire* — *"O Mālik, let thy Lord finish with us"* (43:77) — the reading that makes the verse a promise and a warning; (3) *the inversion of the two worlds*: God causes the believers to *weep now and laugh then*, and the deniers to *laugh now and weep then* — the reading al-Qurṭubī attributes to some, and the Qur'an's own doctrine: *"that He may make the truth stand and bring to naught the false, however much the guilty disliked"* (8:8) and the Prophet's warning: *"If you knew what I know, you would weep much and laugh little"* (al-Bukhārī 6413; Muslim 2359). Al-Qurṭubī also transmits the word of the early mystic master Dhū'l-Nūn al-Miṣrī:

> *"He makes the hearts of the believers and the gnostics laugh with the sun of the knowledge of Him, and He makes the hearts of the deniers and the disobedient weep with the darkness of His wrath and punishment."* (transmitted by al-Qurṭubī)

The three readings are one doctrine at three altitudes: the Author of the heart's weather is one; the forecast differs by road.

**The Theology of Tears: Weeping as Worship**

The commentators gather the tradition's enormous honor-roll of weeping. The Prophet himself wept at his son's death and explained: *"the eye sheds tears and the heart grieves, and we say naught but what pleases our Lord; and we are saddened by thy parting, Ibrāhīm"* (al-Bukhārī 1303; Muslim 2315) — tears are creaturely, complaint is the trespass. He wept at the graves he visited, until the ground was wet beneath him (Abū Dāwūd 3210, graded ṣaḥīḥ); he wept in the night prayer over the Qur'an's warnings, until his beard was wet and those behind him wept (al-Bukhārī 5051; Muslim 800) — and his promise: *"Two eyes shall not the Fire touch: an eye that wept from fear of God, and an eye that spent the night guarding in the way of God"* (al-Tirmidhī 1639, graded ṣaḥīḥ). And Muslim transmits the seven categories shaded on the Day *"when there shall be no shade but His"* — among them *"a man who remembers God in private and his eyes flow"* (Muslim 1031). The scholars systematize: *khashyah*-weeping (awe), *shawq*-weeping (longing), *taubah*-weeping (repentance), *raḥmah*-weeping (compassion) — all four are *invited*; the condemned weeping is only the grief that complains of the Decree or weeps for what it cannot take along. The verse's doctrine dignifies every genuine tear: it was authored — and can therefore be *answered*: *"truly thy Lord is of vast forgiveness"* (v. 32) waits behind this verse like a handkerchief.

**The Theology of Laughter: The Sanctioned Smile**

Laughter in the tradition is likewise governed, not banned. The Prophet laughed — genuinely, repeatedly: the companions testify *"I have not seen anyone smile more than the Messenger of God"* (al-Tirmidhī 3574, graded ṣaḥīḥ); he raced ʿĀʾishah and laughed at losing (*"this is for that"*, Abū Dāwūd 2578, graded ṣaḥīḥ); he smiled until his molts showed at the believers' stories (the ḥadīth of the man who swore to divorce: the ṣaḥīḥ of the Prophet's smile in Muslim 2492). Yet he fenced the laughter: *"Do not laugh much, for much laughter deadens the heart"* (al-Tirmidhī 2305, graded ṣaḥīḥ); *"woe to him who speaks and lies to make the people laugh — woe to him, woe to him"* (al-Tirmidhī 2319, graded ḥasan); and the Qur'an's own fence: *"let not a people mock another people"* (49:11). The balance the scholars draw: laughter is the heart's permitted exhale — *He causes it* — but the heart that lives in laughter has misfiled its residence; the resident of this world is a tenant in a tenement of weeping and weddings, and the Prophet's own ratio stands: *"weep much, laugh little"* for the one who has *seen what he has seen*. The believer's aim, per the commentators: the smile that consoles (*"your smile in your brother's face is charity"*, al-Tirmidhī 1956, graded ṣaḥīḥ) without the laughter that forgets.

**The Verse's Psychology: Emotional Sovereignty and the Believer's Stability**

The commentators close with the verse's deep pastoral fruit: *emotional sovereignty belongs to God* — and this is the believer's stability. The man who believes *He causes laughter and weeping* is armored against both poles of modern despair: against *despair in grief* (the tears are authored; they have an end-term; the Author is merciful — *"with hardship comes ease,"* 94:6), and against *corruption in joy* (the laughter is licensed; it has a leash; the Author is watching — the verse of the heedless laugher: *"leave them to eat and enjoy and be diverted by hope"* (15:3)). The believer's prayer enshrines the verse's balance — the Prophet's daily petition: *"O God, I ask You for firm steadfastness in the affair, and determination upon right guidance, and I ask You for thankfulness for Your blessing and the excellence of Your worship, and I ask You for a truthful tongue and a sound heart, and I seek refuge in You from the evil of all that You know..."* (al-Tirmidhī 3546, graded ṣaḥīḥ) — and the Book's own summary of the verse's two weathers: *"that you may not despair over what has escaped you, nor exult over what He has given you; and God loves not every boaster exultant"* (57:23). The heart that lodges its laughter and its weeping with the same Lord is the only heart that neither drowns nor floats away.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:44

> **and that it is He who causes death and gives life,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Seventh Axiom: The Two Levers of Existence**

The recital moves from the heart's weather to existence itself: *wa annahu huwa amāta wa aḥyā* — *"and that He it is who causes death and gives life."* The two verbs — *amāta* and *aḥyā* — are again intensives naming *authorship*: death is not the failure of biology, life not the achievement of chemistry; both are *administered*. The Qur'an hammers this axiom across the Book — the commentators count its occurrences: 2:258; 3:156; 7:158; 9:116; 10:56; 22:6; 23:80; 40:68; 44:8; 57:2; 67:2 — *"'It is We who give life and cause to die, and to Us is the destination'"* (50:43); *"He brings forth the living from the dead and brings forth the dead from the living"* (6:95); *"and God created you, then causes you to die; and among you is he who is returned to the vilest of life, that he may know nothing after knowledge"* (16:70).
