Al-Rāzī notes the verse's polemical edge in context: the pagans conceded that *God* creates and resurrects (*"It is God who gives you life, then causes you to die,"* 45:24; *"every soul shall taste death; then unto Us you shall be returned,"* 29:57) yet credited the *term* and the *timing* to their idols and their fates; the axiom removes the last superstition: even the dying breath's schedule is His — *"none dies but by God's leave, at a written term"* (3:145).

**The Ḥadīth of the Angel of Death: Death as Service**

The tradition supplies the administration's face, and the commentators gather it. The angel of death is *a servant with a register*: *"there is no one of you but his place is already written — in the Fire or in the Garden"* (Muslim 2646); and the tradition of al-Barāʾ records the two companies at the soul's taking: *"when the believer's soul is taken, the angels of mercy receive it..."* (Aḥmad, graded ṣaḥīḥ). The *term* doctrine is ṣaḥīḥ and central: *"the term, when it comes, cannot be deferred nor advanced"* (7:34), and his operative doctrine in life: *"None of you should wish for death because of harm that befalls him; if he must, let him say: O God, keep me alive so long as life is better for me, and cause me to die when death is better for me"* (al-Bukhārī 5671; Muslim 2680). The believer's relationship to death is thus *entrusted*, not feared as chaos: the Lever-puller is the same Lord of verse 25 who owns both terms and of verse 41 who repays to the fullest — death is not the exit from the story; it is the corridor between the two rooms of one house.

**Life-Giving: The Three Registers of Iḥyāʾ**

The commentators unfold *aḥyā* — He gives life — in its three registers. (1) *Biological life*: the animation of the drop, the sustaining of the heartbeat — the axioms of vv. 45–46 will take this up. (2) *The resurrection*: the reversal of death at the blast — *"It is He who gives life and causes death, and to Him belongs the alternation of night and day"* (23:80) — the doctrine to which vv. 47–48 move. (3) *The life of the heart* — the reading the mystic commentators (al-Ṭūsī, cited by the Study Quran's sources as ST) emphasize: *"He causes the hearts of His enemies to die through disbelief and darkness, and gives life to the hearts of His friends through faith and the lights of gnosis."* The Qur'an's own usage supports the third register: *"Is he who was dead and We gave him life and made for him a light whereby he walks among men as he whose likeness is in darkness...?"* (6:122). The Prophet's supplication lives in this register: *"O God, make alive my heart with Your light and my sight with Your light and my hearing with Your light..."* (Muslim 763). The axiom thus speaks to the *present tense* believer: every prayer answered, every verse that opens, every sin abandoned is *iḥyāʾ* — the same divine verb that will raise the dead raising you *now*.

**Death and Life as the Frame of the Test**

The commentators note the axiom's position in the scrolls' decalogue: it stands beside *laughter and weeping* (v. 43) as the frame within which the test runs. The Qur'an states the frame's purpose explicitly: *"Who created death and life to try you as to which of you is best in deed; and He is the Mighty, the Forgiving"* (67:2). Ibn Kathīr draws the implications: because *He* administers both levers, (1) *no anxiety* is warranted — the term is written (*"and each has a term; when their term comes they cannot delay it an hour nor advance it,"* 16:61); (2) *no despair* at bereavement — the parted are not lost but *relocated* by the same Hand (*"say: the death from which you flee shall meet you, then you shall be returned to the Knower of the unseen and the seen,"* 62:8); (3) *no arrogance* in health — the life is a loan drawn from the Life-Giver's account; and (4) *no hesitation in sacrifice* — the one who holds the levers has promised the spender: *"whoever gives a pair of anything in God's way is summoned in the Garden: O servant of God, this is better"* (al-Bukhārī 1423) — the man who trusts the Death-Giver spends freely.

**The Believer's Deathbed Doctrine: The Best and Worst of Deaths**

The tradition's practical doctrine of death completes the verse's teaching. The *best* death: as a martyr, in the prayer, in the pilgrimage, of the plague of the steadfast, of the mother in childbirth — the lists of the ḥadīth: *"it is a martyrdom for every Muslim"* (Muslim 1915; cf. the list of the best deaths, al-Bukhārī 5735); the *worst*: the death of the one who *"hated to meet God, and God hated to meet him"* (Muslim 2688) — and the death in shirk's grip, whose forgiveness the Qur'an fences (4:48). The Prophet's last counsel seals the verse's doctrine in a dying sentence: *"rather — the highest companion (al-Rafīq al-Aʿlā)"* (al-Bukhārī 3664; 4435) — the man who taught *"He causes death"* died *into* Him. The believer's recitation of this axiom at every funeral — *"to God we belong and to Him we return"* (2:156) — is the axiom in liturgy: the two verbs (*amāta, aḥyā*) bracket every life; the verse asks only which of the two returns you *friendlier* than the first one sent you.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:45

> **and that He creates the two — the male and the female,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Eighth Axiom: The Author of the Pair**

The axioms now descend into the womb: *wa annahu khalaqa al-zawjayni al-dhakara wa al-unthā* — *"and that He created the pair: the male and the female."* The word *zawjayn* — "the two of a pair" — from *z-w-j*, to couple, to match — names not merely the two sexes of mankind but the *pairing-principle* of creation itself: *"glory be to Him who created the pairs (al-azwāj) all of them, of what the earth grows, and of themselves, and of what they know not"* (36:36); *"and of every thing We created a pair, that you may remember"* (51:49). Al-Rāzī notes the rhetorical context: the verse's doctrine strikes directly at the pagan slander this sūrah condemned in vv. 21–22 — the idols' theology of *daughters*; the truth inverts it: the *pairing* — male and female both — is God's *design*, authored with equal intentionality, neither sex an accident, an afterthought, or a divine embarrassment. The commentators note also the pairing's purpose within the test: the sexes' mutual need — *"and of His signs is that He created for you from yourselves mates that you find rest in them, and He set between you love and mercy"* (30:21) — the family as the test's primary laboratory of patience, justice, and mercy.

**From the Pair to the Drop: The Link to Verse 46**

The verse's grammar binds it to the next: *khalaqa al-zawjayn... min nuṭfatin idhā tumnā* (vv. 45–46) — He *created the pair from a drop*. The commentators note the wonder the two verses compress: the *entire diversity* of mankind — male and female, the billions of paired persons across history — emerges from a *single ungendered drop*. The Qur'an's parallel wonder-texts frame the argument: *"Does man not see that We created him from a drop? Yet lo, he is a manifest disputer!"* (36:77); *"He created you from a single soul, then made from it its mate"* (39:6); *"was he not a drop of emitted semen, then a clinging clot, then He created and proportioned?"* (75:37–38). The argument is the Qur'an's standing resurrection-argument: *the first genesis is stranger than the second* — the One who coaxed male and female, bone and blood, character and face from a shapeless drop cannot be taxed by reassembling the scattered dust: *"and he strikes for Us a parable and forgets his own creation: who will quicken the bones when they are rotted? Say: He will quicken them who produced them the first time, and He is of every creation knowing"* (36:78–79).

**The Womb's Hidden Machinery: Revelation's Precision**

Al-Rāzī and the later commentators marvel at what the pair-verse implies of revealed biology: the *sex* of the child — the very distinction of male and female — is determined in a process no seventh-century eye could inspect; the drop's division, the gene's lottery, the anatomy's unfolding are all *His creation* — *khalaqa*. The ḥadīth corpus adds the timetable: the famous narration of Gabriel's catechism:

> *"...then he said: what is the growing embryo (ʿalaqah)? He said: a drop, then a clot, then a lump, then flesh and bone, then I clothe the bones with flesh; then I constitute him a new creation; so blessed be God, the best of creators."* (al-Bukhārī 3208; Muslim 2645 — the embryonic stages)

and the fourth clause of the same narration: *"then his provision, his term, his deeds, and his [ultimate] felicity or wretchedness are written"* — the prenatal audit. The scholars draw the theological fruit: *the doctrine of divine determination begins in the body* — you did not choose your sex, your face, your era, your parents; the paired creature is *received*, not self-authored — and humility before the Author of the pair is the first lesson of every biology lesson. The modern genome's map does not disturb the doctrine; it *extends* the doctrine's astonishment: the more machinery revealed, the more Authorship displayed — *"We created man from an extraction of clay, then We made him a drop in a firm lodging"* (23:12–13).

**Male and Female Equal in Piety, Different in Form: The Qur'anic Balance**

The commentators balance the verse against its siblings to state Islam's doctrine of the sexes with precision. *Spiritual parity*: *"I waste not the labor of any laborer among you, whether male or female — the one of you is from the other"* (3:195); *"the men and women who submit... for them God has prepared forgiveness and a great reward"* (33:35) — the ledger of piety is one ledger. *Physical complementarity*: the pair's design — *"they are a garment for you and you are a garment for them"* (2:187) — mutual covering, mutual warmth, mutual modesty. *Differentiated roles*: the Qur'an assigns the pairing's society its architecture — *"men are the maintainers of women by what God has preferred some of them over others and what they spend of their wealth"* (4:34) — the load-bearing and the nurturing divided *for the building's sake*, not the inhabitants' rank. The Prophet's household modeled the balance: Khadījah the merchant-employer who employed her future husband; ʿĀʾishah the scholar whose narrations are a third of the Sunnah's legal backbone; his appointed day for the women, apart from the men's day, on which he taught them what God had commanded him (al-Bukhārī 101) — for *every* believer, of both halves of the pair.

**The Pair as Sign: Every Marriage a Verse**

The commentators close with the verse's devotional harvest: *the pair is a sign* — the Qur'an's word: *"and of His signs is that He created for you from yourselves mates"* (30:21). Every wedding is therefore the recitation of this axiom in public: the groom and bride stand as *evidence* — two unmixable histories folded by the Author of pairing into one household, per His design and under His law. The believer's marriage ethics follow from the axiom: (1) *gratitude* — the mate is *His* creation, a trust from the Pair-Author; (2) *justice* — the pair's quarrel is never a war of owners, for neither owns the other; both are owned (v. 31); (3) *mercy* — the bond's cement, named by 30:21 before the love itself. And the single believer carries the axiom too: the pair is *everywhere* in creation — *"and of what they know not"* (36:36) — and the unpaired soul's loneliness is answered not by despair but by the axiom's wider reading: the whole cosmos is paired, and every pair — atom, day, garden, grief — testifies to the Unpaired: *"He, God, is One"* — the sūrah before this one, and the doctrine this axiom quietly serves.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:46

> **from a drop of semen when it is emitted,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Humblest Beginning in the Qur'an's Vocabulary**

The verse completes the eighth axiom with the most humbling noun in scripture: *min nuṭfatin idhā tumnā* — *"from a drop when it is emitted."* *Nuṭfah* denotes the insignificant droplet — the lexicographers gloss: a *small quantity of water*, what clings and could be flicked away. *Tumnā* — "when emitted, cast forth" — from *m-n-y*, the root the Qur'an also uses for death's decree (*al-maniyyah*) — the commentators note the root's double occurrence in the human story: the drop *cast* begins the life; the term *decreed* ends it; both are administered. Al-Rāzī pauses on the Qur'an's bold humility: the Book of the Lord of the Throne identifies the whole human species — prophets, sages, kings, the man now listening to the verse — with the contents of a droplet; and it does so *in the argument's service*: *"he strikes for Us a parable and forgets his own creation: 'Who will quicken the bones when they are rotted?'"* (36:78) — the skeptic who doubts resurrection has forgotten that *he himself* is the miracle he doubts.

**The Drop in the Qur'an's Resurrection Argument**

The commentators trace the drop-argument across the Book, for it is one of the Qur'an's favorite proofs: (1) *argument from the first genesis*: *"and he has struck for Us a parable, and forgotten his creation"* (36:78); *"O mankind, if you are in doubt of the resurrection, then behold: We created you from dust, then from a drop..."* (22:5 — the full ladder: dust, drop, clot, lump, formed and unformed, then the sustenance and the term); *"and it is He who produced you from one soul, then a lodging, then a repository"* (6:98). (2) *argument from the hidden*: the drop is *unseen* — swallowed in darkness — *"in a lodging firm"* (23:13) — yet its outcome, the seeing person, is manifest; so the unseen Hereafter, hidden beyond the veil, shall issue its manifest Day: *"as We brought the first creation, so shall We bring the second"* (21:104); *"say: travel in the earth and see how He originated creation; then God brings forth the later genesis"* (29:20). (3) *argument from power's cheapness*: *"Is not He who created the heavens and the earth able to create the like of them? Yea — and He is the creator, the Knower"* (36:81). The verse 46's drop is therefore not biology for its own sake; it is the *museum of divine power* the skeptic walks past daily.

**The Embryology of Revelation: The Ḥadīth's Stages**

The tradition supplies the timetable the Qur'an's noun implies, and the commentators at this verse cite it. Al-Bukhārī and Muslim transmit the Prophet's words (in the catechism of Gabriel):

> *"Each of you is constituted in his mother's womb forty days as a drop (nuṭfah), then he is a clot (ʿalaqah) the like of it, then a lump (muḍghah) the like of it; then the angel is sent and breathes the spirit into him, and is commanded with four words: to write his provision, his term, his deeds, and whether he is wretched or felicitous."* (al-Bukhārī 3208; Muslim 2645)

And the Qur'an's own ladder in Sūrah al-Muʾminūn: *"then We made the drop a clot, and We made the clot a lump, and We made the lump bones, and We clothed the bones with flesh; then We produced him as another creation; so blessed be God, the best of creators"* (23:14) — the phrase *another creation* (khalqan ākhar) the commentators read as the ensoulment: the body's architecture complete, then the *person* installed. The scholars note the fit of the revealed sequence to what instruments later confirmed — and their conclusion is sober, as Ibn Taymiyyah's school would have it: revelation's precision is a *sign*, not a laboratory manual; its purpose is the verse's own: *the audit's certainty* — the creature who was once a drop will face the Auditor who watched the drop.

**"When It Is Emitted": The Doctrine of the Decreed Moment**

The phrase *idhā tumnā* — "when it is emitted" — anchors the doctrine of the *decreed instant*: life's beginning is a *moment*, administered, on the record from its first second. The commentators connect this to the ḥadīth of the four inscriptions: provision, term, deeds, felicity — all written *before* birth, per the narration above — and to the Qur'an's covenant of the unborn: *"[God] brought forth from the loins of the children of Adam their posterity and made them testify concerning themselves: 'Am I not your Lord?'"* (7:172) — the drop already *addressed* by its Lord. The believer draws two disciplines from the decreed instant. First, *sanctity of the beginning*: the ethic of life's inviolability starts at the drop — the Qur'an's own horror at the erasure of children (*"and when the buried girl is asked: for what sin was she slain?"* 81:8–9; *"slay not your children for fear of poverty,"* 17:31) extends the inviolability to the earliest term of the modern debates, and the jurists' discussions of the ensouled stages weigh with it; the tradition's life-ethic prays over the whole ladder — the Prophet's word at a funeral: *"There is no life but the life of the Hereafter"* (Muslim 2845) — and seals it: *"whoever kills a soul... it is as though he killed mankind altogether"* (5:32). Second, *the humbling of pride*: the man who traces his glory to a droplet cannot coherently glory — *"perish man! how unthankful he is: from what thing did He create him? From a drop He created him, then proportioned him"* (80:17–19).

**The Drop in the Believer's Devotional Life**

The commentators close with the drop's liturgical life. The Prophet's most constant supplications include the drop's counterpart: *"O God, bless me in what You have provided me... and make me die a Muslim and join me with the righteous"* (al-Tirmidhī etc.); and the Qur'an's own evening of the creed: the believer at prayer recalls 23:12–14's ladder in the verses of prostration; at funerals he recites the drop's Owner's name over the ladder's end; at births he whispers the adhān into the new pair's first child — the ladder's newest rung. And the everyday fruit: *gratitude without arrogance* — the scholar of verse 46 walks with the Prophet's refusal of inflated titles: *"Do not exaggerate in praising me as the Christians exaggerated in praising the son of Mary; I am His servant, so say: the servant of God and His Messenger"* (al-Bukhārī 3445) — the greatest of the drop-born refusing the divinization of the drop-born. From a drop when emitted — the whole species, the whole test, the whole Hereafter: the verse measures the creature's smallness with one noun, and the Creator's patience with the rest of the sūrah.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:47

> **and that with Him is the second genesis,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Ninth Axiom: The Return of the Return**

The axioms arrive at their eschatological summit: *wa anna ʿalayhi al-nashʾata al-ukhrā* — *"and that upon Him rests the second genesis."* *Al-nashʾah* — from *n-sh-ʾ*, to *originate, to rise* (the root of *mashriq*, the rising-place of the sun) — names a *generation, an origination, a coming-forth*; *al-ukhrā*, "the other," the second. The Qur'an's usage maps the word exactly: the *first* genesis is the bodily creation from the drop (*"and that He created the pairs... from a drop,"* vv. 45–46); the *second* is the raising — *"and how can you disbelieve in God, when you were dead and He gave you life, then He causes you to die, then He gives you life, then unto Him you are returned"* (2:28). Al-Ṭabarī records the near-unanimous reading: *al-nashʾah al-ukhrā* is *the resurrection (al-baʿth)* — the standing-up of the dead. Al-Rāzī, ever the rationalist, offers the minority reading the source notes: the "second genesis" may denote *the breathing of the spirit* — the person's second creation beyond the body's — because *the spirit, not the clay, ennobles the son of Adam*; the majority harmony: both creations are His, both are nashʾah, and both testify to the third: the standing at the audit.

**The Drop-to-Dust Argument: The Qur'an's Master Proof**

The commentators assemble the resurrection-proof the two preceding verses built. The argument runs in three steps. (1) *The precedent*: the first genesis is fact — every listener *is* a walking precedent: *"We created you; will you not then confirm? Have you considered what you emit (tumnūna)? Is it you who create it, or are We the creators?"* (56:58–59) — the same root *m-n-y* as v. 46's *tumnā*; the skeptic's own body is the exhibit. (2) *The precedent's scale*: creation from *nothing* exceeds creation from *something rotted*: *"Does not man see that We created him from a drop? Yet he is a manifest disputer! And he has struck for Us a parable and forgotten his own creation: who shall quicken the bones when they are rotted? Say: He shall quicken them who produced them the first time, and He is of every creation knowing"* (36:77–79). (3) *The divine self-comparison*: *"And he strikes for Us a parable while forgetting his creation"* and *"is not He who created the heavens and the earth able to create the like of them?"* (36:81); the Qur'anic answer itself, 36:78–79, with the ṣaḥīḥ credentials of the raising's recognition: *"the people will be called by the wombs of their mothers"* (Muslim 2512), and the raising's mechanics: *"between the two blasts is forty"* (Muslim 2955). The argument's elegance the scholars savor: *the proof of the impossible is the impossible already done* — and it is done *in every womb, every day*.

**Nashʾah in the Qur'an's Eschatology: The Day's Profile**

The Qur'an paints the second genesis in dozens of scenes, and the commentators at this verse gather its profile. The *blast*: *"and the trumpet is blown, and lo, from the graves they stream forth"* (36:51); *"the day the earth is replaced by another earth, and the heavens, and they come forth before God, the One, the Vanquisher"* (14:48). The *reassembly*: *"do they not see that God, who created the heavens and the earth, is able to create their like?"* (17:99); the *body's witness*: *"and they shall say to their skins: why did you testify against us? They shall say: God made us speak"* (41:21). The *ledger's opening*: *"and the Book is set, and thou seest the guilty fearful of what is in it, and they say: woe to us, what a book this is! it neglects not a small thing nor a great save it has counted it"* (18:49). The *two exits*: *"as for him who is given his book in his right hand... and as for him who is given his book behind his back — he shall cry for destruction"* (69:19–25). The scholars note the sūrah's own rhythm: al-Najm has promised the audit (vv. 31, 39–41) and now certifies the *venue* — the second genesis is what makes verses 38–41 enforceable; no standing, no sentencing.

**The Deniers' History: Every Generation's Version of the Question**

Al-Rāzī reviews the deniers' arguments across history, for the verse answers them all in one root: *n-sh-ʾ*. The Makkan: *"who will quicken the bones when they are rotted?"* (36:78) — answered: *He who produced them the first time.* The materialist's: *matter cannot reorganize after decay* — answered by the Qur'an's reclassification: the power that organized it *once from nothing* is not taxed by reorganization from something: *"that is easy for God"* (14:48's parallel: *"as We began the first creation, We shall repeat it — a promise binding upon Us; truly We shall do it,"* 21:104). The skeptic's: *resurrection is mythological justice* — answered by the axiom's logic: without the second genesis, verses 38–41 are poetry; with it, they are *law*; and a moral universe without enforceable law is the true mythology. The believer's confidence stands on the same root: *nashʾah* is His — the second origination belongs to the Originator; the verse's grammar (*ʿalayhi* — "upon Him," it *rests* on Him) makes the resurrection not a possibility to be argued but a *charge* the Owner carries: *"a promise binding upon Him in truth"* (21:104).

**The Believer's Life in the Light of the Second Genesis**

The tradition turns the doctrine into daily economics. The Prophet's counsel: *"Be in this world as a stranger or a passing traveler"* (al-Bukhārī 6416) — the second genesis is the destination, so pack accordingly; his instruction for the dying hour: *"prompt your dying to the word 'there is no god but God'"* (Muslim 916) — the second genesis's passport is the first genesis's allegiance; his liturgy at the graveside: *"peace be upon you, O inhabitants of the abode of believers... we shall, God willing, join you"* (Muslim 975) — the two genesis-meetings are appointments, not farewells. And his doctrine of the *stages* between: the barzakh, the trial of the grave — the ṣaḥīḥ narration of the two companions of the grave, one carried to the gardens of Paradise, one to the pits of the Fire (al-Bukhārī 1374; Muslim 2870) — the second genesis *begins* in the grave's first questioning. The believer's prayer enshrines the whole doctrine: *"O God, I seek refuge in You from the punishment of the Fire and the punishment of the grave and the trials of life and death and the evil of the trial of the false messiah"* (al-Bukhārī 1377; Muslim 588) — the daily fence against the corridor's terrors. *ʿAlayhi al-nashʾah al-ukhrā*: the second genesis is *upon Him* — resting on the same power as the first; the drop proved it, the bones will prove it, and the ledger of verses 38–46 will open on the day it does.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:48

> **and that it is He who enriches and contents,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Tenth Axiom: The Two Hands of Provision**

The decalogue of the scrolls ends with the axiom of wealth and want: *wa annahu huwa aghnā wa aqnā* — *"and that He it is who enriches and contents."* The Arabic pairs two near-synonyms whose shade the commentators refine: *aghnā* — He *makes rich* (from *gh-n-y*, self-sufficiency, wealth) — and *aqnā* — He *makes content, suffices, preserves from poverty* (from *q-n-y*/ *k-f-ā* family; the reading *aqnā* glossed by al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī as *kafā* — "He suffices" — or as *aṭʿama wa sāqa*, "He feeds and waters"). Al-Zamakhsharī reads the pair as the two sides of provision: *ghinā* is the asset, *qnāʾ* is the peace — God gives *wealth* to some and *sufficiency* to others, and *the second is the greater gift*, for many are rich without ever being *qāniʿ* (content). The Prophet's economics echo the pairing: *"Wealth is not in abundance of goods; wealth is the wealth of the soul"* (al-Bukhārī 6446) — the ḥadīth's psychology is the verse's grammar: *He enriches* (the account) *and contents* (the appetite); the truly provided hold both.

**The Refutation Underneath: Provision Is Not the Idols' Portfolio**

The verse's polemical work in the sūrah's context is precise: the Makkan economy of idolatry was, at bottom, a *provision-theology* — the shrines funded because the idols were believed to open the accounts. The axiom re-routes the whole supply chain: *He* enriches, *He* contents — *"and there is no creature on the earth but upon God is its provision"* (11:6); *"and in the heaven is your provision and what you are promised"* (51:22). The commentators gather the doctrine's corollaries: (1) *the seeking is lawful, the grant is His* — the Qur'an commands the pursuit (*"and seek, of God's bounty, the abode of the Hereafter, and forget not thy portion of the world,"* 28:77) while denying the idol's leverage (*"those you call upon apart from God will never create a fly, were they to gather for it,"* 22:73; and 46:4's challenge: *"show me what they created of the earth"*); (2) *the rizq is decreed* — the ḥadīth of the womb's four inscriptions includes *provision* (al-Bukhārī 3208; Muslim 2645), and the Prophet's counsel: *"the Holy Spirit breathed into my soul that a soul shall not die until it has consumed its provision in full; so fear God and seek [provision] gracefully"* (transmitted by al-Ḥākim and others, with sound chains per the muḥaddithūn; the ṣaḥīḥ core: 65:3 — *"whoever trusts in God, He suffices him"*); (3) *contentment is a gift to be prayed for*, not a mood to be summoned — the ṣaḥīḥ liturgy: *"O God, I ask You for pardon and well-being in my religion, my world, my family, and my wealth"* (Abū Dāwūd 5074, graded ṣaḥīḥ).

**The Two Distributions: Why the Rich and the Contented Are Not the Same People**

Al-Rāzī reflects on the verse's implicit sociology: God distributes *enrichment* (ghinā) and *contentment* (qnāʾ) by two separate wisdoms. Some He enriches *without* contentment — the restless wealthy, the portrait of the Qur'an's Qārūn: *"so We caused the earth to swallow him and his mansion; then had he no company to help him against God, nor was he of the helped"* (28:81); some He grants *contentment without* enrichment — the satisfied poor whom the Prophet honored above the vaulting wealthy: *"the poor of the Muhājirah shall enter the Garden before the rich by half a day"* (Muslim 2988). And the rarest grant is *both* — wealth held by a contented heart — which the Prophet defined as the only success: *"not by abundance of goods — rather, success is the richness of the soul"* (Muslim 1058; al-Bukhārī 6446).

**The Verse in the Believer's Working Life**

The commentators draw the verse's labor ethic in four rules. (1) *Strive as the means, trust as the engine*: *"tie your camel and trust"* (al-Tirmidhī 2517); the Prophet's rebuke of passive begging: *"it is better for one of you to take his rope, gather firewood, and sell it, than to ask men"* (al-Bukhārī 1470) — the axiom does not cancel work; it *enlists* it. (2) *Refuse the unlawful shortcut*: the provision is *written*; its harvest cannot be advanced by corruption — the Prophet's curse upon the corrupt channel: *"God's curse is upon the briber and the bribed"* (al-Tirmidhī 1337, graded ṣaḥīḥ). (3) *Spend as the proof of trust*: *"and whatever you spend, He replaces it"* (34:39), and the Prophet to Bilāl: *"and spend, and fear not diminishment from the Lord of the Throne"* (al-Bukhārī 1432). (4) *Diagnose poverty by the heart*: the *aqnā* of the verse is the cure the age forgets — the Prophet's counsel on the only sufficiency: *"if morning finds a servant fearful of none but God, he will fear none else; and whoever's morning finds him desiring naught but what is with God, God will suffice him against wanting what is with people"* (Ibn Mājah 4142, graded ṣaḥīḥ). The axiom's last gift is thus the believer's most radical freedom: the Ledger of provision is *closed and signed* — *"no soul dies until it consumes its provision in full"* — and the man who believes it cannot be chased by an economy.

**The Decalogue Closes: From the Scrolls to the Ruins**

With verse 48 the recital of *what is in the scriptures of Moses and what Abraham fulfilled* is complete: no transfer of burdens (v. 38), nothing but one's striving (v. 39), the striving seen (v. 40) and repaid to the fullest (v. 41), the terminus at the Lord (v. 42), laughter and weeping His (v. 43), death and life His (v. 44), the pair from the drop (vv. 45–46), the second genesis upon Him (v. 47), enrichment and contentment His (v. 48). The next verse seals the list with a star: *"and that He is the Lord of Sirius"* (v. 49) — the axioms return to the sūrah's opening oath, and the history that ignored them (vv. 50–54) files past as evidence. The believer leaves the decalogue with its practical summary, the Prophet's compressed sermon: *"Fear God wherever you are, follow the evil with a good that erases it, and treat the people with beautiful character"* (al-Tirmidhī 1987, graded ṣaḥīḥ) — the scrolls' axioms in one line, walkable in one day, payable at one audit.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:49

> **and that He is the Lord of Sirius,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse Returns to the Star**

The decalogue of axioms closes with a verse that closes the sūrah's circle: *wa annahu huwa rabbu al-shiʿrā* — *"and that He is the Lord of Sirius."* With this verse the sūrah returns to the celestial body with which it opened: *by the star when it sets* (v. 1). The oath was sworn by a star; the creed now names the star's *Owner*. Al-Jalālayn, Ibn Kathīr, and al-Qurṭubī identify *al-Shiʿrā* as *Sirius* — the brightest star of the visible heavens, the alpha of Canis Major, which the Arabs called *al-shiʿrā al-ʿabūr* (the passing Sirius) and watched as the calendar's great signpost: its heliacal rising announced the summer's heat, the *shiʿrānī* winds, the seasons of pasture and disease. The commentators record the historical reason for the naming: *the Arabs of the Lakhm of al-Ḥīrah worshipped Sirius* (JJ) — and, more broadly, star-worship was the most "reasonable" of the pagans' errors: of all created things, the star seems most to *deserve* divinity — constant, remote, self-luminous. The Qur'an's answer is the possessive: *the Lord of Sirius* — the brightest thing in the sky has a *Master*, and it is not Him.

**Abraham's Star Argument: The Sūrah's Hidden Midrash**

The commentators connect verse 49 to the Qur'an's fullest star-polemic, the night scene of Abraham's search (6:75–79):

> *"So We showed Abraham the dominion of the heavens and the earth, that he might be of those with certainty. So when night overshadowed him he saw a star and said: this is my Lord. But when it set he said: I love not those that set. Then when he saw the moon rising he said: this is my Lord — but when it set he said: unless my Lord guide me I shall be of the people gone astray. Then when he saw the sun rising he said: this is my Lord — this is greater! But when it set he said: O my people, I am quit of what you associate: I have turned my face to Him who created the heavens and the earth, a ḥanīf, and I am not of the idolaters."* (6:75–79)

The argument's method is exactly the method of al-Najm: *watch the candidate's behavior.* The star sets — therefore it is a servant, not a lord; only the One who commands its setting is the Lord. Verse 49's possessive compresses the whole night-scene into two words: *rabbu* (Lord — not the star itself) *al-shiʿrā* (of Sirius — the very brightest of them). The commentators note the sūrah's architecture: it opened by swearing *by* the star (honoring it as evidence) and closes the axioms by naming the star's Lord (demoting it to estate) — the precise double movement of sound theology toward creation: *use the signs; worship only their Signer.*

**The Broader Doctrine: God Is Lord of Everything Worshiped in His Stead**

Ibn Kathīr and al-Ālūsī widen the verse's scope: the naming of Sirius is not astronomy but *theology of the rivals*. The sūrah has disposed of the idols of stone (vv. 19–23), the feminized angels (vv. 26–28), the purchased intercessors (v. 26); now it disposes of the *stars* — and the formula generalizes: *God is the Lord of everything to which the pagans ascribed an independent share of power.* The Qur'an applies the same formula to every rival: *"say: who is the Lord of the seven heavens and the Lord of the mighty Throne? They say: God. Say: will you not then be reverent?"* (23:86–87) — the pagans *conceded* the creed and *violated* it in practice; the verse forces the concession to face the violation. Ibn Taymiyyah's analysis of shirk (in his discussions of intercession and of the pagan creed) rests here: the idolaters' error was never the *denial* of God's lordship but the *partition* of its effects — and the cure is not new information but the *completion* of what they already knew: *"most of them believe not in God save while associating"* (12:106). Verse 49's possessive completes it: the brightest star, the dearest idol, the most trusted intercessor — all carry the same title in the registry: *owned.*

**The Star Calendar and the Believer's Cosmology**

The verse also dignifies the study of the heavens while fencing it. The Arabs' star-lore was *agricultural science* — and the Prophet, famously, tolerated the practical calendar while banning the astrology and the star-causality. Muslim and al-Bukhārī transmit the definitive boundary-marking from Zayd ibn Khālid al-Juhanī, who narrated that the Prophet led them in the dawn prayer at al-Ḥudaybiyyah after a night's rain, then turned to the people and said:

> *"Do you know what your Lord has said tonight? He has said: this morning some of My servants believed in Me and some disbelieved. As for him who says: we were given rain by the grace and mercy of God — he believes in Me and disbelieves in the star. And as for him who says: we were given rain by such-and-such a star — he disbelieves in Me and believes in the star."* (al-Bukhārī 846; Muslim 71)

The doctrine is exact: the star's *calendar* is knowledge; the star's *causality* is creed. The believer may navigate, plant, and measure by Sirius; he may not *attribute* to Sirius. The commentators add the Prophet's closure of the adjacent error of omens: *"There is no ʿadwah [contagion as an independent cause], no ṭiyarah [bird-omen]... none of the omens is real, but the best of them is the good omen"* (al-Bukhārī 5754; Muslim 2222–2224) — superstition's channel is closed; the Star-Owner's channel of trust is open.

**The Circle Closes: From the Oath to the Ownership**

The commentators close the verse by marking the sūrah's architecture complete. The chapter's ring now joins: *by the star when it sets* (v. 1) — the star as oath-witness; *taught him by one of awesome power* (v. 5) — the angelic realm's real officer; *the Lord of Sirius* (v. 49) — the stellar realm's Owner; and the axioms between them: the Lord of the star is the Lord of the womb (v. 46), the grave (v. 44), the ledger (v. 41), the provision (v. 48). Al-Biqāʿī notes the ring's argument: a revelation that knows the star's Lord, the drop's path, and the ledger's terms cannot be the invention of a desert man; it is the *Owner's manual* — and the next verses (vv. 50–54) will display what happens to peoples who filed the manual under fiction: ʿĀd, Thamūd, Noah's folk, the razed cities — the creeds' cemeteries. The believer recites verse 49 as the sūrah's thesis in miniature: *look up, name the Owner, and never stop at the window.*

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:50

> **and that He it was who destroyed ʿĀd the former,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verdicts of History Begin**

The sūrah now shifts from creed to evidence — the historical record: *wa annahu ahlaka ʿādan al-ūlā* — *"and that He it was who destroyed ʿĀd the former."* The commentators note the verse's grammar of continuity: it begins with *wa anna* — the axioms' refrain — so that the destructions are *the tenth-and-eleventh axioms*: the Lord of Sirius (v. 49) is *demonstrably* the Lord of national history, and the proof is the ruins every Arabian caravan walked past. Al-Rāzī notes the rhetorical choice of audience: the Quraysh doubted the resurrection and the requital; the Qur'an points them to the *archaeology of their own peninsula* — the emptied cities of ʿĀd and Thamūd, known to every merchant of the Syrian and Yemeni roads. The destroyed peoples are the Qur'an's most repeated class of witnesses — *"how many a generation have We destroyed before them"* recurs across the Book — and their function is fixed: *"that He may requite those who do evil and requite those who do good with the most beautiful"* (v. 31's clause, now illustrated).

**ʿĀd the Former: Iram of the Pillars**

Why *al-ūlā* — "the former"? The commentators, following the source and the tafsīr tradition, explain: there were *two* ʿĀd. ʿĀd the former is the tribe of *Iram* — *"have you not seen how thy Lord dealt with ʿĀd — Iram of the pillars (dhāt al-ʿimād), the like of which was not created in the lands?"* (89:6–8) — identified in the reports as the tribe of Noah's grandson of the same name, the *first* ʿĀd, whose monumental architecture — columns, edifices — was proverbial; ʿĀd the latter is the tribe of Hūd, the ʿĀd of al-Aḥqāf, whom the Qur'an's fuller narratives describe. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr transmit both identifications and the reports of Iram's fabulous prosperity — *"to whom none was given the like in the cities"* (89:8) — and its disappearance so complete that the name survived only in proverbs. The doctrine the verse draws: even the *most monumental* civilization is *mortal* — the pillars could not bear the Decree; *"and as for ʿĀd, they were destroyed by a screaming, furious wind"* (69:6) — the wind, the humblest of God's soldiers, against the mightiest of man's structures.

**The fuller Record of ʿĀd: Hūd Among His People**

The Qur'an's longer ʿĀd-narratives supply the theology behind the destruction, and the commentators gather the profile: the prophet *Hūd* sent to a people of unusual stature and industry — *"as for ʿĀd, they were arrogant in the land without right and said: who is mightier than us in strength? Did they not see that God who created them is mightier than they in strength?"* (41:15); his call — *"O my people, worship God; you have no god other than He; will you not then be reverent?"* (7:65); their sneer — *"we found our fathers upon a creed and we follow their traces"* (7:70) — the very genealogy-argument verse 23 of this sūrah demolished; their bargain — *"bring us the chastisement thou promisest us, if thou art of the truthful"* (7:70); and the answer from the horizon — *"and when they saw it as a cloud advancing upon their valleys they said: this is a cloud bringing us rain! Nay, it is that which you sought to hasten: a wind bearing a painful chastisement, destroying all things by its Lord's command"* (46:24–25). The commentators note the irony's precision: the ʿĀdites read even their catastrophe as *provision* — the cloud that looked like rain was the wind — the exact inversion of the ḥadīth of rain and the star (al-Bukhārī 846; Muslim 71): one people saw the Giver in the cloud, the other saw only the cloud. The verdict: *"and in the morning they were as though they had not dwelt there"* (46:25), with the wind's seven nights: *"He made it rage upon them seven nights and eight days, havoc: thou mightest see the people therein overturned as though they were hollow trunks of palms"* (69:7).

**Why History's Ruins Are Cited: The Epistemology of the Ruin**

The commentators ask why revelation leans on *destroyed nations* so heavily, and answer with three epistemological functions. (1) *The past as proof of power*: the resurrection-denier needs evidence of God's ability to end and begin; the ruins are *slow-motion* resurrections in reverse — a civilization stood up from nothing and was laid down again: *"say: travel in the earth and see how He originated the creation; then God brings forth the later genesis"* (29:20). (2) *The past as precedent of justice*: the requital of v. 31 is not a threat for the far future; it has an *operational history* — *"and such was the seizing of thy Lord when He seizes the towns that do evil: His seizing is painful, severe"* (11:102). (3) *The past as warning pitched at the audience's pride*: the Quraysh trusted their lineage, their market, their shrine; the ruins said: *lineages taller than yours have been leveled.* The Prophet's own sermon-logic matches: *"I and the Hour have been sent like these two"* — joining his two fingers (al-Bukhārī 6500). The ruins are the Hour's press-releases.

**ʿĀd in the Believer's Memory: The Prayer and the Attitude**

The tradition enshrined ʿĀd in the believer's liturgy and ethics. The Prophet's counsel in drought and plenty both run through the ʿĀd lesson. In drought, his community learned to seek rain from the Lord of the wind and not from the cloud (the rain-prayer liturgies of al-Bukhārī 1013); and in plenty, he feared precisely the ʿĀd error: *"By God, I do not fear poverty for you; but I fear for you that the world be opened to you as it was opened to those before you, and you vie therein as they vied, and it destroy you as it destroyed them"* (Muslim 2961). This ḥadīth is verse 50's sunnah-commentary: the destroyed are the *viers*. And the individual believer's daily recitation of the ruins — in the Qur'an's sessions, in the sight of any civilization's fallen grandeur — plants the axiom: *He destroyed ʿĀd the former*: the Lord of Sirius is the Lord of the dates on the calendar; every nation's obituary is His signature. The next verse names the second signature: Thamūd.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:51

> **and Thamūd, sparing none,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Signature: Thamūd**

The roll of the ruins continues: *wa thamūda fa-mā abqā* — *"and Thamūd, so that He spared none."* The particle *fa-mā abqā* — "so He left none" — is the Qur'an's formula of total requital: no remnant, no hostage, no descendant carrying the tribe's name forward — *"and Thamūd, so naught survived"* — the sister-verse's image of totality (69:8). The commentators note the audit's precision: for ʿĀd the wind was the instrument; for Thamūd — *"as for Thamūd, they were destroyed by the Cry (al-ṣāʿiqah)"* (69:5); *"and the Cry seized those who did wrong, and they became [as] refuse in their homes"* (51:44) — the terrifying paradox: the tribe destroyed *inside* its carved mansions, by a sound. Al-Ṭabarī notes the geographic weight: Thamūd's capital, al-Ḥijr (Madāʾin Ṣāliḥ), stood on the northern caravan road the Quraysh themselves traveled to Syria; their tombs cut into the living rock were — and are — visible to every eye that passes. The destroyed nation is not a legend from far away; it is the roadside archaeology of the audience's own trade route: *"you carve houses out of the mountains with skill"* (26:149) was Ṣāliḥ's sentence upon them — the skill survived them.

**The Profile: The She-Camel and the Tested Covenant**

The Qur'an's fuller Thamūd-narratives supply the theology, and the commentators gather its sequence. The prophet *Ṣāliḥ* — *"O my people, worship God; you have no god other than He; He produced you from the earth and settled you in it"* (11:61) — a people of engineering genius who cut their palaces from cliffs; their scoff: *"thou art but a man like us"* (26:154) — their word: *"thou art but a man like us"* (26:154) — and the sign granted: the she-camel of God, *"the sign"* — *"this is a she-camel of God, a sign for you; leave her to eat in God's earth, and touch her not with evil, or there shall seize you a painful chastisement"* (7:73). The camel was the test's perfect architecture: a *provision* made into a *covenant* — she drank at the well on her day, and their herds on theirs (54:28); the covenant's only demand was *restraint*. The commentators note the doctrine: the sign God grants the hard-hearted is not a lecture but a *test of self-control* — could a nation that could carve mountains not leave one camel to drink? They could not. Hamstrung by conspiracy, the camel fell, and the tribe announced its crime as achievement: *"and they hamstrung her, and repented not, and became on that day remorseful?"* — the Qur'an's grammar: *"so they hamstrung her and then regretted"* (27:45's context) — and the Cry came: *"so the Cry seized them at sunrise"* (15:73) — Thamūd's parallel at 69:5 and 54:31: *"one Cry, and behold, they were like the dry stubble of the penfold"*). The one cry of the creation's Lord ended the nation that had ignored the one request of His camel.

**"Sparing None": The Doctrine of Total and Partial Requitals**

The phrase *fa-mā abqā* raises a question the commentators handle with care: were *all* destroyed? The Qur'an's own narratives include survivors: *"and when Our command came, We delivered Ṣāliḥ and those who believed with him by a mercy from Us from the disgrace of that day"* (27:53; 11:58 for ʿĀd; *"and We delivered those who believed and were reverent"*). The resolution: *mā abqā* refers to the tribe as a corporate entity — its power, its culture, its name — not to every soul of faith within it; the believers were saved, the corporate body annihilated. Ibn Kathīr draws the doctrine: divine requital distinguishes *persons* but not *civilizations* — the believing individual is rescued from the sinking ship, but the ship still sinks; *"and be on your guard against a chastisement which seizes not the wrongdoers among you alone, and know that God is severe in requital"* (8:25). The phrase is also a mirror for every generation's illusions of insulation: wealth, walls, and engineering cannot *abqā* — cannot keep anything back — when the Decree arrives; the sole escape documented in the record is belief with the prophet of the time.

**The Two Ruined Brothers: ʿĀd and Thamūd Paired**

The commentators note that the Qur'an pairs the two tribes dozens of times — *"ʿĀd and Thamūd — indeed it has come to you clear [proofs] from your Lord"* (14:9; the pair at 11:61–68; 41:15–17; 69:4–8; 85:17–18) — and draws the paired lessons: ʿĀd fell to *wind* (the invisible), Thamūd to *sound* (the unseeable) — the two most immaterial of powers, against the two most materially impressive of nations. The pairing preaches the Qur'an's cosmology: power is not in what a civilization builds but in *Whom* it obeys; the wind and the Cry serve the Lord of Sirius (v. 49) as armies, and no dam of stone insures against them. The Prophet's own practice enshrined the pair: he diverted his army from their ruins at al-Ḥijr, commanding the believers: *"Do not enter the houses of those who wronged themselves except weeping, lest what befell them befall you"* (al-Bukhārī 3380; Muslim 2980) — the ruins as a *place of warning*, visited with tears, never with tourism.

**The Verse in the Believer's Economics**

For the individual believer, the commentators harvest three disciplines from Thamūd's signature. (1) *The covenant's smallness is the test's greatness*: the nation fell not for a grand doctrine's rejection alone but for one hamstring — sin's trajectory runs through the small *covenant-breakers* of daily life; the believer keeps his smallest promises as if they were the she-camel. (2) *Engineering is not security*: the age of carved mountains is the age of bridges, towers, and platforms — the verse reminds the engineers of the soul that the skill survived the skilled; build, but hold the building *as a trust* (v. 25's ownership). (3) *Repentance before the remorse*: the Qur'an's terrible aside — *"and they hamstrung her, and repented not"* — locates damnation's anatomy in the refusal to turn back; the remorse that comes *after* the Cry is the remorse of history, not the repentance of hope. The prayer of the verse's student is the Prophet's refuge: *"O God, I seek refuge in You from the punishment of the Fire and from the punishment of the grave"* (al-Bukhārī 1377; Muslim 588) — and from the corporate ruin that spares none: by keeping the covenants, small and large, while the sun still rises.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:52

> **and the people of Noah aforetime — truly they were most wrongdoing and most rebellious.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Oldest Case File**

The audit moves back before ʿĀd and Thamūd, before the wind and the Cry, to the earliest of the post-Adamic judgments: *wa qawma Nūḥin min qablu fa-innahum kānū hum ẓalamū wa aṭghā* — *"and the people of Noah aforetime — truly they were most wrongdoing and most rebellious."* The commentators note the superlative pair: *ẓalamū* — they were the *wrongdoers* (the root ẓ-l-m: darkness, the placing of a thing where it does not belong) — and *aṭghā* — the *transgressors* (root ṭ-gh-w: exceeding the bound, the word of v. 17's *mā ṭaghā* and Pharaoh's *Ṭughyān*, 20:24). Al-Rāzī reads the pairing as the anatomy of every ruined people: *ẓulm* is the wrong's substance (they misplaced worship and oppressed men), *ṭughyān* is the wrong's momentum (they exceeded every limit once the first bound was broken). And *min qablu* — "aforetime" — places the case first in the file: the precedent of precedents, the Flood's horizon under which every later nation is a re-hearing.

**The Length of the Case: Nine Hundred and Fifty Years**

The Qur'an's Noah-narratives give the case its duration, and the commentators dwell on it: *"and indeed We sent Noah to his people, and he remained among them a thousand years save fifty"* (29:14). Nine hundred and fifty years of calling — the longest mission recorded in the Qur'an — and the register of the refusal is preserved in the Book's most exhausting scenes: the mockery (*"and the council of those who disbelieved among his people said: we see thee but in manifest folly,"* 7:60); the plot to stone him (11:27's council: *"we see thee not following any save the lowest of us"*); the daily prayer that closes the case: *"my Lord, leave not upon the earth of the disbelievers a single dweller; truly, if Thou leave them, they lead astray Thy servants and beget naught but dissolute ingrates"* (71:26–27). Ibn Kathīr notes the doctrine inside the duration: prophetic patience is *measured*, not romantic — nine hundred fifty years is the ceiling of the tradition's patience, and it, too, ended in requital; the believer's counsel is the same Noah's: *"and I advise you... and my success is only through God; in Him I trust, and to Him I turn"* (11:88's formula, Noah's at 11:26's context). No mission's length, however long, suspends the ledger.

**"Most Wrongdoing": The First Inventors**

Why does the verse call Noah's people *most wrongdoing*? The commentators record the tradition's answer, which the source preserves (al-Rāzī): Noah's people were *ẓalamū* in a founding sense — *they established the customs, particularly idol worship, that others then imitated*; Wadd, Suwāʿ, Yaghūth, Yaʿūq, and Nasr (71:23; and the Prophet's narration in Muslim 2148 of the five idols as righteous men of Noah's folk, whose images were first made for remembrance, then worshiped) — the *origin* of idolatry itself. The doctrine is enormous: the sins that filled the shrines of al-Lāt, al-ʿUzzā, and Manāt (vv. 19–20) began at Noah's flood; the Quraysh's idols were the grandchildren of the first adultery of worship. Al-Rāzī draws the sociology: the first sin is rarely the last man's — error *institutions* itself, each generation inheriting the baptized inventions of the last (v. 23's *"you and your fathers"*); Noah's people were *most* wrongdoing precisely because they were *first*.

**The Flood: The Audit of Waters**

The requital itself the Qur'an narrates as a reversal of creation's order: *"so We opened the gates of heaven with water pouring, and We made the earth to gush forth springs, and the waters met in a matter decreed"* (54:11–12) — the sky rained *and* the earth erupted: every door of creation opened at once, and *"We carried him upon a thing of planks and nails, sailing under Our eyes, a reward for him who was disbelieved"* (54:13–14). The commentators note the flood's theology: water, the substance of life and of the drop (vv. 45–46), becomes the audit's instrument — the element that carried every womb carries away every refuse; and the Ark's cargo — *"every kind in pairs"* (11:40) — is the covenant of the second genesis (v. 47) written in timber: the world is *reloadable*. The final scene is the Qur'an's most heartbreaking paternal address: Noah calling his son — *"O my little one, embark with us, and be not with the disbelievers"* — and the son's answer — *"I shall take refuge in a mountain that will save me from the water"* — and the father's cry — *"this day there is no protector from God's command save him upon whom He has mercy"* — and the wave between them: *"and he was of the drowned"* (11:42–43). Verse 52's axiom stands inside that dialogue: *no bearer of burdens bears another* (v. 38) — not even a prophet's son is carried by a prophet's Ark.

**The Case's Verdict and the Sūrah's Argument**

Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr note why the oldest case stands in this sūrah's evidence: the Quraysh doubted the *possibility* of requital and resurrection; the Flood is the requital that *washed the world* — an event the Arabs' own tradition carried (the Quraysh traced themselves through Adnan to Ishmael and knew the Flood's story), corroborated by the Qur'an's certainty and by the generations' memory. And the verse's emotional note — *truly they were most wrongdoing and most rebellious* — is the Judge's own summing-up, the only verse in the ruins-roll that editorializes: the file is closed with the moral sentence before the physical one. The believers recite it as both warning and orientation: the God of the lote tree is the God of the Flood; the mercy that gave the Prophet two bows' length is the justice that gave the world one ark. And the sūrah's next verses will close the ruins-roll with the twin cities of the plain — the razed cities and *that which covered them* — the covering of v. 16 reversed into the covering of v. 54: the two mantles of God's universe, glory and ruin, awaiting the reader's choice.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:53

> **and He razed the subverted cities,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third and Fourth Signatures: The Cities of the Plain**

The ruins-roll reaches its most famous file: *wa kamāna al-muʾtafikāta* — *"and He razed the overthrown cities."* The word *al-muʾtafikāt* — from *i-n-f-k*, to overturn, to subvert — is the Qur'an's technical designation for the cities of Lot: Sodom and its sisters, *subverted* because they were turned upside down — *"and We made its uppermost to be its lowermost"* (11:82). Al-Ṭabarī records the early readings of the plural: the cities of Lot's people per Sūrah al-Furqān's phrasing (*"and the subverted cities We razed utterly,"* 25:40); per al-Rāzī: *all the towns destroyed by overturning calamities*. The commentators note the verse's unique horror in the ruins-roll: ʿĀd fell to wind and Thamūd to a cry — instruments *sent from outside*; the cities of the plain were *overturned from beneath* — the very ground they walked became the instrument: *"and when Our command came, We turned them upside down, and rained upon them stones of baked clay, heaped up, marked with thy Lord; and they are never far from the evildoers"* (11:82–83). The closing clause of that passage — *they are never far from the evildoers* — is the verse's loaded freight: the ruins were near enough to the caravan roads to be seen, and near enough to every age's sinners to be *relevant*.

**The Sin and the Sentence: Why Overturning**

The Qur'an names the cities' crime with clinical precision — the first mention of a sin whose name the whole world now borrows from Sodom: *"do you commit indecency such as no one in the worlds has preceded you with? Truly you approach men lustfully instead of women — nay, you are a prodigal people"* (7:80–81); *"and Lot believed him and said: I am a migrant to my Lord"* (29:26). And the sentence matches the crime's geometry: a people who inverted the order of desire were *inverted*; the earth they refused to obey turned upon them. Ibn Kathīr and the commentators note the Prophet's own report of the scene's terror: the tradition's severity is anchored in the repeated ṣaḥīḥ prohibitions of entering upon the deed's neighbors and upon the deed itself, condemned by name at 7:80–81 and 27:55, and in the curse every scholar cites: *"cursed is he who does the deed of the people of Lot"* (transmitted by Aḥmad and al-Ṭabarānī with sound chains, per the muḥaddithūn; and the ṣaḥīḥ of 7:80–81's reading). The tradition's pastoral order must be stated as the scholars state it: the temptation is not the damnation — the *deed* and its *justification* are; the struggler who fights is a believer, and the mercy of verse 32's vast forgiveness stands open to every struggler who returns; the razed cities are the file of those who, like verse 33's man, *turned away* and made the sin their order.

**The Guests and the Test: The Narrative's Irony**

The Qur'an's Lot-narrative turns on an irony the commentators unfold: the cities' men were *consumed by appetite for men* — and their destruction began with their appetite *for guests*: *"and when Our messengers came to Lot he was troubled for them and distressed for them, and said: this is a distressful day"* (11:77) — for he knew his people would come for them; and they came — *"and his people came hastening to him, and before they had been doing evil deeds; he said: O my people, these are my daughters; they are purer for you"* (11:78) — the offer the commentators read as the last mercy: abandon the sin, take the lawful path. Their answer was the civilization's self-indictment: *"thou knowest we have no right to thy daughters, and truly thou knowest what we want"* (11:79). The angels' verdict followed: *"O Lot, we are the messengers of thy Lord; they shall not reach thee; so travel with thy family in a watch of the night..."* (11:81). The irony completes: the men who could not restrain themselves from guests who *appeared* were destroyed by guests who *were* — the angels whose forms (vv. 6, 26's doctrine) are real presences; and the city that waylaid the road to the Kaʿbah (29:14's charge: *"do you waylay the road and commit in your gatherings evil?"* — 29:29) itself became a warning on the road: the Dead Sea's basin as the eternal road-sign of the Levant trade.

**The Wife Who Stayed: The Verse's Interior Lesson**

The narrative's most personal edge is Lot's wife: *"so We saved him and his family all together, save an old woman among those who stayed behind"* (37:134–135); *"so travel with thy family in a watch of the night, and let none of you turn round, save thy wife: truly that which befell her shall befall her"* (11:81): *"save thy wife — that which befell her shall befall her"*). The commentators read her file carefully: she was *married to a prophet* — in the Prophet's own word: *"the example of the believers is the believing wife of Pharaoh; and the example of the disbelievers is the wife of Lot"* (al-Bukhārī 2780; Muslim 2664). She was not destroyed for her body's sins, the commentators note, but for her *allegiance*: she *sympathized* with the city, informed on the guests (per the reports), turned back with her heart. The doctrine: the household of the saved is not salvation; *orientation* is. A woman in a prophet's bed went with the city; the believing wife of a tyrant went with the Garden (66:11). For every family of faith, verse 53's razed cities carry this tender terror: *check the wife's heart, the husband's heart, the children's hearts — the Ark does not save those who long for the flood.*

**The Razing in the Believer's Geography of Meaning**

The commentators close with the verse's modern weight. The cities of the plain stand at the head of two arguments the age conducts. Against the *normalization* argument — that desire redefines the order — the Qur'an sets the file: a civilization was not chastised for the temptation it bore but for the *order it enthroned*; the Muslim's stance is the Prophet Lot's: refuse, counsel, migrate — *"I am a migrant to my Lord"* (29:26) — and never the Mob's cruelty; the tradition of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd's century knew no pogroms, only prophecy. Against the *despair* argument — that the struggler is lost — the same Book opens the vast forgiveness of v. 32 to every struggler who returns, and the ḥadīth of the struggler's two rewards: *"the one who strives is the one who strives against his soul for God's sake"* (al-Tirmidhī 1621, graded ṣaḥīḥ). The verse's practical liturgy is the Prophet's at every temptation's door: *"O God, I seek refuge in You from the evil of my hearing, my sight, my tongue, my heart..."* (Abū Dāwūd 1555, graded ṣaḥīḥ) — and his counsel at the door of every temptation: *"flee to God"* — for the cities' file teaches the one certainty of the moral universe: the ground itself obeys the Lord of the lote tree; it is safer to obey Him than to stand where He has already spoken.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:54

> **such that there covered them that which covered.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second "That Which Covered"**

The ruins-roll closes with the sūrah's most haunting echo: *fa-ghashiyahā mā ghāshā* — *"so there covered them that which covered."* The phrase is an exact grammatical twin of verse 16 — *mā yaghshā al-sidrata mā yaghshā*, the covering of the lote tree — and the commentators treat the twin as deliberate architecture: one *mā ghāshā* at the summit of heaven, one at the floor of requital; one indescribable *glory*, one indescribable *terror*. Al-Rāzī names the lesson in the pairing: creation's reality has two orders of the indescribable, and which one descends upon a soul is decided not by chance but by its answer to revelation. The covering of the cities, per the commentators' reading of the narratives, was the rain of stones — *"and We rained upon them a rain; evil was the rain of those who were warned"* (26:173) — stones of baked clay, *"heaped up, marked with thy Lord"* (11:82–83); or the totality of the catastrophe — the overturning, the cry, the stones — gathered under one verb because its terror exceeded itemization. Al-Zamakhsharī notes the grammar's mercy and terror at once: the *indefinite* covering means the tongue is discharged — it cannot and need not be described; the *repetition* means the covering was total — nothing of the cities stood outside it.

**The Enigma as Method: Speech's Two Frontiers**

The verse completes a doctrine the sūrah has been building since verse 16: *revelation marks the frontiers of speech*. At the lote tree, the covering exceeded *wonder* — sight arrived where vocabulary departed (v. 16's commentary); at the cities, the covering exceeded *horror*. The Qur'an treats both with the same linguistic discipline: name the edge, halt at it. The commentators draw the believer's two obligations from the twin verses: (1) *do not attempt to describe what revelation veiled* — the glories of the unseen are received, not imagined; the horrors are feared, not dramatized; (2) *do not disbelieve what you cannot describe* — the indefinite *mā* is not a confession of non-existence but of ineffability; the modern reader who demands a photograph of the covering has misunderstood the sentence's genre: it is the speech of a witness standing at the edge of language, *which is itself the evidence*. Al-Ghazālī's principle returns: the greatest realities announce themselves by defeating description; the defeat is information.

**The Covered Cities in the Qur'an's Own Archive**

The commentators gather the sister-texts that partially lift the veil of *mā ghāshā*: *"so when Our command came We turned them upside down and rained upon them stones of layered baked clay, marked with thy Lord; and they are never far from the evildoers"* (11:82–83); for the cities of Lot (15:73–74): *"so the Cry seized them at sunrise, and We made its uppermost its lowermost, and rained upon them stones of baked clay"*); *"and He destroyed the cities that were overturned"* — the exact word of our verse — *"and covered them with what He covered"* (37:136–137) — the twin text in Sūrah al-Ṣāffāt, which the commentators cite as the verse's first exegesis. And the geography: *"truly ye pass by them in the morning and at night; will you not understand?"* (37:137–138) — the ruins on the road to Syria, the caravan's daily evidence, the Qur'an's standing dare: *look at the ground you camp on.*

**The Two Coverings as the Two Destinations**

Al-Ālūsī extends the twin-verse doctrine into eschatology: the two coverings preview the Hereafter's two mantles. The Garden's covering: *"in shade long-extended"* (56:30), the shade of the Throne for the seven (Muslim 1031), the mantle of nearness — *"and their Lord gives them to drink a pure drink"* (76:21); the Fire's covering: the verse: *"as for those whose faces shall be blackened: did you disbelieve after your faith? Then taste the chastisement for that you disbelieved"* (3:106); *"and We shall gather them on the Day of Resurrection upon their faces, blind, dumb, deaf"* (17:97). The believer reads the twin verses as the geography of his own future: every soul will be *covered* by one of the two — and the choice of covering is being made now, in the ordinary hours the sūrah has been auditing: the glance (v. 17's discipline), the giving (v. 34's well), the covenant (v. 51's camel), the heart's allegiance (v. 53's wife of Lot).

**"That Which Covered" and the Fear of God**

The practical science the commentators harvest is the expansion of *khawf* — fear — from a mood into a discipline. The Prophet feared for his ummah the *covering of luxury*: *"I do not fear poverty for you, but I fear that the world be opened to you... and you vie therein and it destroy you"* (Muslim 2961 — v. 50's text); and he taught the daily refuge from the covered terror: *"O God, I seek refuge in You from the punishment of the Fire and from the punishment of the grave and from the trials of life and death and from the evil of the trial of the false messiah"* (al-Bukhārī 1377; Muslim 588) — the covering-prayer recited every dawn and dusk. And he balanced the fear with its medicine, in ʿĀʾishah's question about the verse *"then whoso is given his book in his right hand, he shall be reckoned an easy reckoning"* (84:7–8): *"that is the presentation (al-ʿarḍ); whoever's reckoning is disputed is destroyed"* (al-Bukhārī 103; Muslim 2876) — the covered cities were the disputed case; the believer's work is to keep his case *undisputed*: the daily tawbah, the small honesty, the kept covenant. The next verse turns from the covered cities to the living audience: *so which of thy Lord's boons do you dispute?* — the question that places every reader inside the file.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:55

> **So which of thy Lord's boons do you dispute?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question That Cross-Examines the Survivor**

The ruins are filed; the verse turns to the living: *fa-bi-ayyi ālāʾi rabbika tatumārā* — *"so which of thy Lord's boons do you dispute?"* The structure is the courtroom's sudden swing: the peoples who disputed were covered (v. 54); the audience that remains is asked what exactly it intends to dispute *now*. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī read the verse in two registers. First, *to the Prophet*: after all these proofs — the vision (vv. 1–18), the idols' demolition (vv. 19–30), the moral axioms (vv. 36–48), the ruins (vv. 50–54) — which boon of thy Lord is left for the deniers to argue with thee over? The Messenger's heart is steadied: the case is complete. Second, *to the audience*: which of these gifts — creation, provision, guidance, the Book, the delay of judgment — are you prepared to litigate, and before whom? The word *tatumārā* is the sūrah's own verb returning: it is *tumārūn* of verse 12 — the disputing over what the Prophet *saw* — now generalized: every dispute with the evidence is one dispute, whatever its object.

**Ālāʾ: The Word of Boons**

The noun *ālāʾ* (singular *iṭlāʾ*, from *a-l-y*: to come near, to arrive) names not gifts in the abstract but *blessings that arrive* — the tide of God's favors reaching the shore of human life. The commentators note the word's weight: to dispute a *boon* is to bite the hand mid-gift. The Qur'an's parallel interrogative is the refrain of Sūrah al-Raḥmān — *"so which of your Lord's boons do you two deny?"* — repeated thirty-one times (55:13 and passim), and al-Qurṭubī notes our verse's relationship to it: the same audit-conscience in a sharper key. In Sūrah al-Raḥmān the question alternates between men and jinn, each addressed as *you two* — the double audience of mercy; here the question is addressed after *destruction* — the boons now enumerated against the backdrop of what happens to the disputers of them. And the Qur'an's own commentary on ingratitude's blindness: *"and God drew a parable: a town secure and at rest, its provision coming abundantly from every place; then it was ungrateful for God's boons, so God made it taste the garment of hunger and fear for what they used to do"* (16:112).

**The Inventory of the Boons in This Sūrah**

The commentators, reading the verse against its sūrah, itemize the boons already placed on the table — and the list is the sūrah's own table of contents: (1) *the oath-backed revelation* (vv. 1–4): a Book that descends like the ordered star; (2) *the certified messenger* (vv. 5–18): a heart that did not lie, a teacher of awesome power, the greatest signs; (3) *the demolished idols* (vv. 19–28): the unmasking of every rival — the gift of a clean theology; (4) *the moral axioms* (vv. 36–48): the scrolls' decalogue — accountability, labor, requital, resurrection, provision; (5) *the lessons of history* (vv. 50–54): the free tuition of the ruins; (6) *and behind them all*: life, the pair, laughter, weeping, death's term, the daily bread — the axioms' very subjects. The question's force: *to dispute now* is to have ignored a completed demonstration. Al-Rāzī observes the psychology the verse targets: human ingratitude is rarely denial of the gift; it is *reclassification* — the water becomes "the water company," the health becomes "my fitness," the revelation becomes "ancient literature"; the verse re-labels every item back to its Author: *thy Lord's boons*.

**The Dispute That Is Really With Oneself**

The commentators note the verse's word: the audience *disputes* — *tatumārā* — and the Qur'an elsewhere names the true venue of that dispute: *"and We have certainly created man and know what his soul whispers; and We are nearer to him than the jugular vein"* — and the verse of the disputed gift: *"and man prays for evil as he prays for good, and man was ever hasty"* (17:11); *"when We bless man he turns away and withdraws aside; and when evil touches him, [his] supplication is [then] extensive"* (41:51). The dispute with the boons is finally a dispute with the *terms* of the Giver — the same quarrel as verse 24's *"shall man have whatever he longs for?"* — man accepts the gifts and rejects the conditions, as a guest who eats the feast and burns the host's house rules. The cure the tradition prescribes is the daily inventory: the Prophet's dawn counsel to Muʿādh: *"have taqwā of God wherever you are"* (al-Tirmidhī 1987), and the Counting of blessings the Qur'an commands directly: *"and He gave you of all you asked of Him; and if you count God's boons you shall never number them; truly man is ever unjust, ungrateful"* (14:34) — and its twin with the remedy: *"if you are thankful I shall surely increase you"* (14:7). *Shukr* — thankfulness — is the anti-dispute: the decision to *use* every arriving gift as its Giver directed.

**From Dispute to Prostration: The Verse's Trajectory**

The commentators note the verse's position as the first of the final movement's hammering questions: *which boons do you dispute?* (v. 55), *do you marvel at this discourse?* (v. 59), *do you laugh and not weep?* (v. 60), *will you stand with head held high?* (v. 61) — and the sequence closes at the sūrah's last command: *so prostrate unto God and worship* (v. 62). The trajectory is the answer to the dispute: the disputing tongue falls silent in the prostrating body. The scholars of devotion observe: dispute and prostration use the same real estate — the forehead's five places; the man who will not lay them down is litigating; the man who lays them down has *settled*. The believer's daily five prostrations are therefore this verse answered fifty times a week — and its perfect historical answer was the courtyard of the Kaʿbah when the first public recitation of this very sūrah ended and *the idolater prostrated, and the believer prostrated, and Satan prostrated* (al-Bukhārī 3423; Muslim 402): even the impostors' bodies betrayed the verdict their mouths withheld. The question stands for every reader: the boons have arrived; the dispute is optional; the prostration is available until the covering of one kind or the other (v. 54) arrives.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:56

> **This is a warning like the warnings of old.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Continuity Verse: One Message, Many Messengers**

The verse places the Qur'an and its Prophet inside the longest tradition in human history: *hādhā nadhīrun mithlu al-nudhuri al-ūlā* — *"this is a warner like the warners of old."* Two readings stand in the commentaries, and both serve the continuity. As the source records (Bḍ): the reference is to *the Qur'an itself* — this Book is a warning of the same kind as the former warnings (the Torah, the Gospel, the scrolls of Abraham and Moses of vv. 36–37): revelation after revelation, one series. As the majority of the commentators read it (Bg, IK, JJ): the reference is to *the Prophet himself* — this warner is like the warners of old: Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Moses — the same commission, the same pattern, the same two outcomes. Al-Rāzī harmonizes: a warner is known by his book, and a book by its warners; the continuity of the one certifies the other. Either way, the verse establishes the doctrine the Qur'an hammers from end to end: *"naught is said to thee save what was said to the messengers before thee"* (41:43); *"nothing has been said to thee that was not said to the messengers before thee"* — the prophetic office is one office, held successively.

**The Pattern of the Warners: The Proof from Repetition**

The commentators draw the epistemological force of the claim: *a truth announced identically by dozens of independent messengers across millennia is not a man's invention.* The Qur'an makes the argument itself: *"and We did not send before thee any messenger but that We revealed to him that there is no god but I, so worship Me"* (21:25); *"and indeed We sent Noah and Abraham, and We placed in the progeny of both prophethood and the Book"* (57:26); *"and We sent not before thee any but men whom We inspired — so ask the people of the Reminder if you know not"* (16:43). The warners' *content* was one; their *contexts* varied — shepherd and prince, captives and kings; their miracles varied; their *message* did not. Al-Rāzī names the reasoning: either all the warners conspired (impossible across nations and centuries), or they all reported one Source (the sole alternative). The verse 56 therefore quietly answers the oldest accusation — verse 4's *illā waḥyun yūḥā* now certified by *consistency*: the newest revelation reads like the old because it came from the same Desk.

**Nadhīr: The Anatomy of Warning**

The word *nadhīr* — warner — is the Prophet's title more than any other in the Makkan Qur'an, and the commentators give its anatomy. The warner is *not* the punisher: *"thy duty is naught but conveyance"* (13:40); *"thou art not their keeper"* (88:22); *"thou art naught but a warner, and for every people a guide"* (13:7). The office's mercy is inside its name: warning is what one does *before* — the siren before the flood, the protocol before the audit. And the Prophet's love for the warned ran even past their refusal: the Prophet's word to his Companions: *"I am your predecessor at the Pond, and men from other nations will be brought to me, and when I stretch my hand they will be withheld; and I say: my companions! and it is said: thou knowest not what they innovated after thee"* (al-Bukhārī 6211) — the warner's love even for the warned who ignored him. And the Qur'an's own valorization: the believer who warns others inherits the office: *"let there be of you a nation inviting to good, enjoining right and forbidding wrong"* (3:104).

**"Of Old": The Warning's Age and the Audience's Excuse**

The phrase *al-nudhur al-ūlā* — "the warnings of old" — forecloses the excuse of novelty. The Quraysh could not say: *this man has brought something the world never heard*; the world had heard it — in the Torah read in Yathrib, in the Injīl recited in Najrān, in the proverbs of the monks; the Qur'an makes the point at its own trial: *"and it is in the scriptures of the ancients"* (26:196); *"was it not announced to them in the earlier scriptures?"* (20:133). The commentators add the *historical* dimension of "of old": the *warnings of old* are also the *fulfilled* warnings — Noah's flood delivered, ʿĀd's wind delivered, Thamūd's cry delivered, the cities covered (vv. 50–54): the warner of old *warned, and the warning came true*.
