The verse therefore argues from the record: the current warning is the same *species* of event as the ones already executed; to treat it as rhetoric is to misread the genre. The Prophet's counsel to his generation is the verse's counsel to every generation: *"I and the Hour have been sent like these two"* — his two fingers (al-Bukhārī 6500) — the warning and its day, adjacent.

**The Verse in the Believer's Self-Understanding**

For the believer, the verse defines his place in the series: he is the audience of *the newest warner*, and the heir of all of them. The scholars draw three duties. (1) *Receive the warning as the old audiences could not*: with the record before them — no generation after the Flood can claim the excuse of precedent; *"that you may not say on the day the warning arrives: had a warner come to us of old... "* — actually the verse's own logic inverted: the warnings of old HAVE come, in document and in ruin. (2) *Continue the office*: the warner of this generation is every conveyer — *"convey from me, even a verse"* (al-Bukhārī 3461) — the parent at the dinner table, the caller in the office, the writer at the desk. (3) *Warn oneself first*: the commentators' oldest counsel — the Qur'an's first addressee is the reciter; before the Book warns the market, it must warn the man in the mirror; the Prophet's own practice: his weeping in the night prayer over the warnings he *himself* transmitted (al-Bukhārī 5051; Muslim 800). The next verse brings the warning's clock into frame: *the imminent is nigh* — the warning has a delivery date.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:57

> **The imminent is nigh.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Shortest Verse, the Nearest Clock**

Three Arabic words carry the sūrah's alarm: *azifati al-āzifah* — *"the imminent has drawn nigh."* The commentator tradition notes the grammar's double emphasis: the subject *al-āzifah* — "that which draws near" — is itself a noun of *approach* (from *a-z-f*, the nearness that whispers, the hoofbeats before the horse appears), and the verb *azifat* repeats the same root: *the approaching thing has approached* — nearness compounded on nearness. Al-Bayḍāwī identifies the referent: *the Resurrection and the Hour*. The commentators note the rhetorical crescendo of the final movement: after sixty verses of evidence, the sūrah's voice shortens to a heartbeat — *azifati al-āzifah* — as though the argument is over and only the clock remains. The Qur'an's parallel texts ring the same bell: *"the Hour has drawn nigh and the moon has split"* (54:1); *"drawn nigh unto mankind their reckoning while they in heedlessness turn away"* (21:1); *"drawn near is the Hour and the moon has split"* — and the Prophet's own escalation: *"I and the Hour have been sent like these two"* — his two fingers (al-Bukhārī 6500); *"hasten in good deeds; trials shall come like patches of a dark night"* (Muslim 118).

**How Near Is "Near"? The Doctrine of Prophetic Imminence**

The skeptics' oldest jibe stands in the Qur'an's own archive: *"when is this promise, if you are truthful?"* — answered across the Book: *"they ask thee of the Hour: when is its landing? Say: its knowledge is only with my Lord; none can manifest its time but He"* (7:187). How then is the imminent *nigh*? The commentators resolve with three converging doctrines. (1) *Near in the measure of what remains*: from the Day of Judgment's own perspective, the entire human story is the last watch of the night — the Prophet's word: *"my sending and the Hour are as these two"* (the two fingers) — fourteen centuries are a finger's width beside eternity. (2) *Near for every soul*: the real clock of the Hereafter for any person is his own death — and that is *provably* imminent for everyone; the scholars' dictum: *the Hour for you is your grave's first night*, and the ḥadīth of the dying man's unseen window (the soul taken by the angels of mercy or wrath — Aḥmad from al-Barāʾ, graded ṣaḥīḥ). (3) *Near in its signs*: the Prophet listed the portents in the ḥadīth of Gabriel — *"the slave-girl bearing her mistress, barefoot shepherds competing in building, and the ten signs: the smoke, the false messiah, the beast, the sun's rising from its west..."* (Muslim 8; al-Bukhārī 50) — and the believer watching the news watches a checklist that only fills. The imminence is thus *doctrinally exact*: not dated, but *directional* — everything that remains is closer than everything that passed.

**The Āzifah's Vocabulary: Approach Without Face**

The lexicographers savor the word *āzifah*: it names the event *before* its arrival — the sound of it, the shadow of it — and never the event's face. Al-Rāghib al-Iṣfahānī notes the usage in the proverb *al-āzifah* for the Death approaching a man still in health. The commentators draw the spiritual psychology: God names the Hour by its *approach* to keep the believer in the posture of expectation — the religion's liturgical year is a rehearsal of vigilance (Ramadan, the ten nights, the Friday's reminder, the funeral's transit); the man who prays five times a day says *"Master of the Day of Judgment"* seventeen times daily — the Fātiḥah's own imminence-training. The Qur'an's parallel: *"race toward forgiveness from your Lord and a Garden whose breadth is as the heavens and the earth"* (57:21) — the verbs of the Āzifah's response are all verbs of speed: *sāriʿū* (race), *istajībū* (respond quickly), *"hasten"* — and the Prophet's counsel: *"the strong believer is better and more beloved to God than the weak believer... and what is past is past"* (Muslim 2664) — no time to relitigate yesterday; the imminent is nigh.

**The Verse's Address to the Quraysh — and to Every Comfortable Generation**

The commentators set the verse in its Makkan scene: the audience laughed at the Hour (v. 60's context), built above graves, trusted the Kaʿbah's stone and the market's tide. The verse's three words answered them as they answer every comfortable generation: the prosperity of the present is *not* the prophecy of the future — ʿĀd's wind (v. 50), Thamūd's cry (v. 51), the Flood (v. 52), the covering of the cities (v. 54) were each preceded by decades of "imminence denied." His counsel in the era of open victory: *"by God, I do not fear poverty for you... but I fear for you that the world be opened to you"* (Muslim 2961) — the clock runs faster in times of plenty. And the tradition's tenderest image for the Āzifah's approach is the Prophet's at the grave: he taught the visitors to say to the dead: *"peace be upon you, O inhabitants of the abode of believers... truly, God willing, we join you"* (Muslim 975) — the Āzifah is not abstract; it meets every family at some door.

**The Believer's Preparation: The Only Reasonable Response to an Approaching Certainty**

The commentators close with the practical logic: the only rational posture toward an approaching certainty is preparation. The Prophet's directives are the sūrah's own: *prostrate* (v. 62 — the body's preparation), *endeavor* (v. 39 — the ledger's preparation), *weep* (v. 60 — the heart's preparation). His specific counsel: *"hasten in good deeds before seven trials"* (al-Tirmidhī 2337, graded ṣaḥīḥ); *"remember much the destroyer of pleasures: death"* (al-Tirmidhī 2307, graded ṣaḥīḥ); and his summary of the prepared life: *"in the world be as a stranger or a passing traveler"* (al-Bukhārī 6416). The believer's evening audit is therefore verse 57's liturgy: one day nearer than yesterday — what did the ledger earn? And the sūrah's next verse will fence the date itself: *it has no unveiler apart from God* — the certainty without the date; the clock without the dial; the mercy that keeps the strongest motivation (imminence) working without the paralysis of a fixed hour.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:58

> **It has no unveiler apart from God.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Closes Every Almanac**

The imminent Hour has been announced (v. 57); the verse now removes it from every calendar but One: *laysa lahā min dūni Allāhi kāshifah* — *"it has no unveiler apart from God."* The word *kāshifah* — from *k-sh-f*, to uncover, unveil, remove a cover — is the same verb of the Garden's promise and the veil of Muslim 179 (*"were He to remove it, the splendors of His Face would burn..."*): the Hour is a *veiled* event, and the *unveiling* — its revelation into the world's sight — has one authorized Hand. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī give the double doctrine the verse carries: (1) *no one knows when it comes* — not the angels, not the prophets, not the souls in the barzakh; (2) *no one brings it to pass but God* — no cosmic bureaucracy unseals it. The Qur'an's anchor text is 7:187:

> *"They ask thee of the Hour: when is its landing? Say: its knowledge is only with my Lord; none can manifest its time but He; heavy has it settled upon the heavens and the earth; it comes upon you but suddenly."* (7:187)

and its twin: *"say: none in the heavens and the earth knows the unseen save God"* (27:65); and the Prophet's boundary: in the ḥadīth of Gabriel's five questions, the Hour's timing was the one thing *both* the questioner and the questioned lacked: *"...then what is the Hour? He said: the questioned knows no more than the questioner"* (al-Bukhārī 50; Muslim 8) — the most stunning admission in revelation: an angel of awesome power (v. 5) who escorted the Prophet through the seven heavens *does not know the date*.

**The Five Things God Withholds**

The commentators attach the verse to the Prophet's summary of the divine reserve:

> *"Verily God has with Him the knowledge of the Hour, and He sends down the rain, and He knows what is in the wombs, and no soul knows what it shall earn tomorrow, and no soul knows in what land it shall die; truly God is Knowing, Aware."* (al-Bukhārī 1039; Muslim 1035 — reciting 31:34)

The list teaches the *kind* of knowledge God reserves: the *switch-points* of history and biography — when the age ends, when the water falls, what the womb holds, what tomorrow earns, where death waits. These are the hinges on which choice, test, and trust turn; were they published, faith would be arithmetic. Ibn Taymiyyah's formulation: the withheld is withheld *for the sake of the given* — the revealed Book suffices for guidance precisely because the un-revealed clock keeps the guidance *chosen*. And the verse 58 guards the doctrine against its two corruptions: the date-setters (every generation's almanac of the End, from the munafiqūn's rumors to modern prophecy markets) and the fatalists (who reason from the unknown date to the uselessness of deed — refuted by v. 39's labor-law). The believer holds the balance: *certain of arrival, ignorant of the hour, working the shift he has.*

**The Hadith of the Hour Coming Suddenly — and the Signs That Delay It**

Al-Qurṭubī gathers the Prophet's descriptions of the unveiling's *manner*: *"the Hour and I were sent like these two"* (al-Bukhārī 6500); and its suddenness — *"the Hour shall come suddenly"* — the Qur'anic adverb of 7:187 (*baghtatan*) and 22:55 — and the Prophet's counsel for living *inside* suddenness: *"if the Hour comes while one of you holds a palm-shoot, let him plant it"* (Aḥmad, graded ṣaḥīḥ) — the most beautiful ethics of imminence ever spoken: *plant faster, not stop planting.* And the signs in their order — the smoke, the Dajjāl, the beast, the sun's western rising, the descent of ʿĪsā, Gog and Magog — are themselves veiled in sequence, known only as they occur: *"it comes upon you suddenly"* — the signs are the unveiling *in instalments*, the clock's hands visible only as they pass.

**Why the Veil Is Mercy: Three Wisdoms**

The commentators state the wisdoms of the withheld date. (1) *The test requires the unknown*: with a published date, obedience would cluster at the deadline and the world would corrupt in between — the theology of procrastination refuted by the theology of the veil; the Prophet's counsel: *"remember much the destroyer of pleasures"* (al-Tirmidhī 2307) keeps the deadline alive in the heart without dating it. (2) *Hope requires the unknown*: for the sinner, a known far date would license a long defiance; the unknown date keeps every dawn a possible last — the mercy that shortens every defiance to a day. (3) *The dignity of the Giver*: the Hour is God's personal affair — the day He *personally* unveils His judgment; to assign its date is to trespass the office; the verse's theology: *laysa lahā min dūni Allāhi kāshifah* — the unveiling belongs to the Unveiler of all veils, the One behind the light of Muslim 179's ḥadīth. The angels' ignorance is the doctrine's highest credential: the beings closest to the Throne hold the secret; a fortiori, every human calendar is fiction.

**The Believer's Use: Living on the Right Side of the Veil**

The tradition turns the verse into a daily practice, and the commentators list its items: the *five* rehearsals of the Hour's Master in every prayer's Fātiḥah; the *charity that crosses the veil* — the ḥadīth of the continuing charity (Muslim 1631) whose fruit arrives on the far side; the *prompt repentance* — *"God accepts the servant's repentance so long as the soul has not reached the throat"* (al-Tirmidhī 3513, graded ṣaḥīḥ); and the *graveyard visits* that calibrate the clock: *"I had forbidden you to visit graves — visit them, for they remind of the Hereafter"* (Muslim 977). The believer's poem is the Companions' summary of the verse: ʿAlī's dictum — *"this world is departing and the Hereafter is arriving; and each has children: be of the children of the Hereafter, not of the children of this world; for today is work and no reckoning, and tomorrow is reckoning and no work"* (transmitted in the literature of the early Muslims, al-Bukhārī 6416's context). The imminent is nigh (v. 57); its unveiling has one Hand (v. 58); and the hand that works until it comes belongs — in the sūrah's final verses — to the forehead that descends.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:59

> **Do you then marvel at this discourse?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Marvel of the Ordinary Miracle**

The final movement's second question: *a-fa-min hādhā al-ḥadīthi taʿjabūna* — *"do you then, at this discourse, marvel?"* The word *ḥadīth* — "discourse, report" — names the Qur'an itself, per the commentators, and the Qur'an accepts the designation with its own commentary: *"God has been sending down the most beautiful of discourses (aḥsana al-ḥadīth), a Book consistent in its pairs..."* (39:23); *"and who is truer in discourse than God?"* (4:87). The marvel (*taʿajjub*) the verse interrogates is not admiration but *scandalized amazement* — the audience's objection: *a Book? to an Arab? among us?* — the exact objection the Qur'an records: *"nay, they marveled that a warner from among themselves came to them, and the disbelievers said: this is a strange thing: when we are dead and become dust... shall we be raised?"* (50:2–3); *"is it a marvel for mankind that We have revealed to a man from among themselves..."* (10:2); *"and when it is said to them: what has your Lord sent down? They say: tales of the ancients"* (16:24). The verse names the marvel and lets its absurdity surface: what exactly is astonishing — that God speaks? that He chooses a man? that the man is *one of them*, known for forty years (v. 2's certified companion)? Al-Rāzī notes the inversion: the truly marvelous things — that stars keep their clock (v. 1), that wombs build humans from a drop (vv. 45–46), that the dead return (v. 47) — the audience gazes at daily without wonder; the one thing that is *not* marvelous — a Book arrived by the Owner of all marvels — is the one that scandalizes them.

**The Discourse's Self-Evidence: What the Qur'an Is**

The commentators answer the marvel with the discourse's own credentials, assembled across the sūrah: it descended star-like (vv. 1–4), taught by a being of awesome power (v. 5), certified by an unfailing heart (v. 11) and a steadied gaze (v. 17); it carries the axioms of the oldest scrolls (vv. 36–48); its history has executed every warning (vv. 50–54). And it stands under the challenge the Qur'an levels at its doubters — *"had it been from other than God, they would have found in it much discrepancy"* (4:82) — and the standing dare: *"and if you are in doubt concerning what We revealed to Our servant, then produce a sūrah like it"* (2:23); *"say: if mankind and jinn gathered to produce the like of this Qur'an, they could not produce the like of it, though they backed one another"* (17:88). The commentators note the marvel's psychology: the Quraysh *admitted* the Book's power — al-Walīd's confession (v. 33's file: *"by God, its speech is sweet, its root firm"*), ʿUtbah's surrender after hearing the first verses of Fuṣṣilat: *"I heard a speech the like of which I never heard; by God, it is not poetry, nor sorcery, nor soothsaying"* (recorded in the sīrah and by al-Bayhaqī) — and they still withheld obedience. That is the marvel the verse names: not intellectual doubt but *the scandal of the ordinary* — the divine arriving in familiar packaging, through a familiar man, in one's own dialect: *"truly it is the speech of a noble messenger... and your companion is not mad"* (81:19–22).

**The Prophet of the Marvel and the Marvel of the Prophet**

The verse also defends the *carrier*: the marvel targeted the man as much as the Book — *"is it a marvel for mankind that We revealed to a man from among themselves?"* (10:2). The commentators place the selection-doctrine here: the Messenger had to be *one of the people* — hearing, seeing, understood — or the message could never be tested, relayed, imitated; *"and We sent not before thee any but men whom We inspired, of the people of the towns"* (12:109). The Prophet's own insistence on his ordinariness is the tradition's marvel-management: *"I am only a man like you; to me comes revelation..."* — the verse of 18:110 and his spoken formula (al-Bukhārī 123); *"do not overpraise me as the Christians overpraised the son of Mary; I am His servant, so say: the servant of God and His Messenger"* (al-Bukhārī 3445). And the marvel's final proof was the *product*: a people who buried daughters and circled idols within a generation produced the civilization of Ibn Masʿūd's precision, ʿĀʾishah's scholarship, Bilāl's steadfastness — the Book that "marveled" the Makkans is the Book whose students did not marvel at all at becoming what they became; they simply followed the discourse.

**The Believer's Marvel Audit**

The commentators turn the verse from the Quraysh to the reader: *what do you marvel at?* The audit the scholars prescribe: (1) *marvel at the right things* — at the ordered star, the forming drop, the returned ledger, the arrived word: *"truly in the creation of the heavens and the earth and the alternation of night and day are signs for men of understanding who remember God standing, sitting, and lying..."* (3:190–191) — the Sunnah's crowning note: the verses' reciter sleeps and weeps (the ḥadīth of the Prophet's weeping at their recitation by Ibn Masʿūd, al-Bukhārī 4563); (2) *cease marveling at the wrong things* — at wealth without lineage's merit, at eloquence without truth, at the believer's "strange" discipline (*"Islam began as a stranger and shall return as a stranger; so glad tidings to the strangers"*, Muslim 145); (3) *convert the marvel* — the verse's audience marveled and *did nothing*; the believer's wonder must terminate in obedience: the marvel of the discourse is only "consumed" when lived — the marvel of the discourse is only lived when recited and followed — *"the best of you are those who learn the Qur'an and teach it"* (al-Bukhārī 5027). The next verse sets the marvel's corrective: the audience laughed where they should have wept — and the verse's question, *do you laugh and not weep?*, will hold the mirror at the angle this one left it.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:60

> **And do you laugh and not weep,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse of the Inverted Face**

The sūrah's question now attacks the face itself: *wa taḍḥakūna wa lā tabkūna* — *"and you laugh, and you do not weep."* The commentators place the verse in its double context. In the sūrah's argument: the audience has heard the discourse of verses 1–59 — the visions, the demolished idols, the axioms, the fulfilled ruins, the imminent Hour — and their faces *laughed*: the mockery recorded across the Qur'an (*"and when those who believe are mentioned they smile,"* 83:29–30; *"and they took them in mockery until their mocking made them forget My remembrance,"* 23:110). In the moral order: laughter is the *signature of the unweighed soul* — the man who has not let the audit of vv. 31–58 cross his face. The commentators note the reversal of verse 43's axiom: *He causes laughter and weeping* — God authors both; but man *chooses* which to host — and the audience had chosen the lighter tenant while the heavier stood at the door. The verse's two verbs are present-tense, continuous: *you keep laughing; you keep not-weeping* — the settled expression of a heart that has settled its accounts without the Auditor.

**The Prophet's Law of Proportion: "You Would Weep Much and Laugh Little"**

The verse's commentary in the Sunnah is the Prophet's most famous face-instruction, and the ṣaḥīḥ collections preserve it:

> *"If you knew what I know, you would weep much and laugh little."* (al-Bukhārī 6413; Muslim 2359)

And Muslim's companion report: ʿĀʾishah said she never saw the Prophet laugh so that his throat (or molars) showed; he *smiled* (al-Bukhārī 5781; Muslim 2360). The commentators harmonize: the Prophet *was not a gloomy man* — the Companions testify he was the most cheerful of them, racing his wife, smiling at children, *the best of you in smile* — but his laughter never reached the *abandonment* of the heedless, because his heart stood inside the knowledge of verses 57–58. The scholars systematize his ratio: (1) *smile* — *"your smile in your brother's face is charity"* (al-Tirmidhī 1956, graded ṣaḥīḥ); (2) *laugh moderately* — *"do not laugh much, for much laughter deadens the heart"* (al-Tirmidhī 2305, graded ṣaḥīḥ); (3) *never laugh at sacred things* — *"woe to him who lies to make men laugh — woe to him, woe to him"* (al-Tirmidhī 2319, graded ḥasan); (4) *weep* — the verse 60's prescription: at the Qur'an, at sin, at death, at the Hereafter.

**The Medicine of Tears: The Sunnah's Weeping Curriculum**

The tradition built a full curriculum of weeping, and the commentators at this verse assemble it. *Weeping at the Qur'an*: the anchor: Ibn Masʿūd's scene — the Prophet asked him to recite, he recited the verse of the witness from every nation, *"and how shall it be when We bring a witness from every people and bring thee as witness over these?"* (4:41), and the Prophet said: *"that is enough"* — and Ibn Masʿūd turned to see his eyes flowing (al-Bukhārī 5051; Muslim 800). *Weeping from fear*: the seven shaded on the Day — *"a man who remembers God in private and his eyes flow"* (Muslim 1031); *"two eyes the Fire shall not touch: an eye that wept from fear of God..."* (al-Tirmidhī 1639, graded ṣaḥīḥ); the paired image the commentators cite stands on the Tirmidhī text above. *Weeping at sin and death*: the Prophet wept at his son's death — *"the eye tears and the heart grieves, and we say naught but what pleases our Lord"* (al-Bukhārī 1303; Muslim 2315); the Ṣaḥābah's tears at the Prophet's own pulpit. And the *compulsory weeping*: at every funeral prayer, the community's tears are the ummah's weekly rehearsal of verse 60 — the Prophet's counsel to the weeper: *"this is the mercy God has placed in the hearts of His servants"* (al-Bukhārī 1284).

**The Sociology of Laughter: What a Community's Face Reveals**

Al-Rāzī and the later scholars read verse 60 sociologically: *a community's ratio of laughter to weeping is a moral instrument.* The Jahili audience laughed at the warning and wept at *nothing* — the Qur'an's diagnosis of their era: the verse: *"so leave them to plunge and play until they meet their day which they are promised"* (70:42); *"and they marveled that a warner came"* — the comedy was their armor. The commentators note the modern resonance without exaggeration: an economy of entertainment that monetizes laughter as industry and treats tears as malfunction is the verse's case study; the believer is not summoned to gloom — the Prophet's humor was real, his smile constant — but to *proportion*: the man who laughs at everything has no instrument left to weigh anything; *"and among men is he whose speech pleases thee in the life of the world... and God is the best knower of the mischief-makers"* (2:204's family of verses: 2:204–206). The believers' gatherings the Prophet described: *"a people who gather in a house of God's houses, reciting the Book and studying it together — tranquility descends upon them, mercy covers them, angels surround them, and God mentions them to those with Him"* (Muslim 2699) — the covered assembly of v. 16's mercy, not v. 54's ruin.

**The Believer's Balance: The Face as an Index of the Heart**

The commentators close with the verse's devotional psychology: the face is the heart's gauge, and Islam *trains* both. The believer weeps — at the ledger's errors, at the Qur'an's verses, at the graves of parents and prophets — and the weeping is *worship*: *"two eyes shall not the Fire touch: an eye that wept from fear of God, and an eye that spent the night guarding in the way of God"* (al-Tirmidhī 1639, graded ṣaḥīḥ). And the believer laughs — with family, at the innocent, in the Prophet's manner — and the laughter is *recreation*, fenced by the rules of truth and mercy. The man of the sūrah's final verses holds both: verse 60's tears in his private nights, verse 62's prostration in his public hours — *"their sides forsake their beds, calling upon their Lord in fear and hope"* (32:16) — and *"faces that day shall be radiant, gazing at their Lord, laughing, joyous"* (80:38–39) — the laughter of the Hereafter, authored by the same Lord (v. 43), guaranteed to the faces that learned, in this world, when *not* to laugh. The verse's question stands over every generation's entertainment: *you laugh — and do you not weep?* — and the next verse names the posture the laughter must finally abandon: *while you stand with head held high.*

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:61

> **while you stand with head held high?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Final Portrait: Sāmidūn**

The sūrah's last human portrait: *wa antum sāmidūna* — *"while you are sāmidūn."* The word is one of the Qur'an's hapax-leggedomena — it occurs here only — and the commentators savor its layered senses, all converging on one posture. Al-Ṭabarī transmits the base meaning: *sāmidūn* — *turning away, diverting, heedless* — the man who *diverts himself* (samada = he became diverted, busied) from what matters. Ibn Kathīr and al-Zamakhsharī add the Arabic image: the *sāmid* stands *inattentive and insolent*, as the drowsy stand leaning — the insolent posture of a man who hears the warning and *strolls*. And the dialectal reading the source preserves (Ṭ): in the Yemeni tongue, *sāmidūn* meant *singing* — *"sammidū: they sing"* — so that the verse pictures the audience *humming and chanting while the warning is recited to them* — the ancient ancestor of every generation's soundtrack over the sermon. The commentators thus give the word its triple crown: *diversion, insolence, and song* — the full posture of a soul that has heard everything and moved nothing.

**The Posture Against the Sūrah's Postures**

Al-Ālūsī notes the sūrah's deliberate postural architecture: the chapter began with *standing* — the teacher of awesome power *"stood upright"* on the horizon (v. 6) — the standing of commissioned majesty; it proceeds through the *drawing near* of vv. 8–9, the *gazing* of v. 17, and ends with the command of v. 62 — *prostrate*; against all of these stands the audience's *sāmidūn* — standing with the insolence of diversion. The verse thus stages the sūrah's final contrast: two ways to stand before the revelation — the upright attention of Gabriel receiving command, and the humming inattention of Makkah's assembly. The Qur'an's parallel portrait: *"and there are of men who purchase diverting discourse (lahw al-ḥadīth) to lead astray from God's path without knowledge..."* (31:6); *"and they stand at nothing..."* — the ṣaḥīḥ parallel: *"when Our signs are recited to them they smile"* — the verse: *"and when those who believe are mentioned they smile"* (83:29–30); and the Prophetic portrait of the sāmid's end: *"the Hour shall not come until it is said on earth: God, God — and none says it"* — the ṣaḥīḥ report of the final heedlessness — the heedless stance *continuing into* the Hour's arrival, the diversion interrupted only by the unveiling of v. 58.

**The Doctrine of Lahw: Diversion as the Soft War**

The commentators widen the verse into the doctrine of *lahw* — diversion — which the Qur'an treats as unbelief's preferred *method* (it rarely argues; it *amuses away*). The analysis, as the scholars render it: the sāmid does not deny the warning; he *schedules himself past it* — diversion is procrastination wearing entertainment. The Qur'an's own diagnosis of the type: *"leave them to eat and enjoy and be diverted by hope; for they shall come to know"* (15:3); *"race toward forgiveness... and hasten"* (3:133) against the mockers of 23:110. The Prophet's counsel maps the cure: *"part of the beauty of a man's Islam is his leaving what does not concern him"* (al-Tirmidhī 2317, graded ṣaḥīḥ) — the anti-diversion principle; and the classical counsel that closes the ledger: diversion is lawful in measure, ruinous in mastery — the boundary of ʿUmar's oversight of the community's speech: nothing idle but the tongue of one remembering God, the girth of a horse, or a man teaching his son.

**The Singing that Mocked: The Verse's Historical Edge**

The Yemeni reading — *they sang while the Qur'an was recited* — gives the verse a specific scandal: the audience did not merely stay silent; they *answered revelation with music*. The commentators connect the scene to the Qur'an's own record of the Makkans' acoustic mockery: *"and when they see thee they take thee only in mockery: is this he whom God sent as a messenger?"* (25:41); and to the later Jizyah-era anecdote the tradition preserves of the man who *sang over the Qur'an to overpower it* — the scholars' note: the verse's tolerance ends where mockery begins: *"when you see those who engage in Our signs in mockery, turn away from them until they engage in other discourse"* (6:68) — the command of v. 29, already operative. The believer's application is precise: entertainment is one thing; *entertainment deployed against revelation* is another — and the sāmidūn of every generation are known by the test: does the amusement *rest* the ear from the warning, or *drown* it?

**From Standing to Prostration: The Verse's Only Exit**

The commentators close with the verse's position as the last description before the last command: *sāmidūn* (v. 61) → *fa-usjudū lillāhi wa uʿbudū* (v. 62). The exit the Qur'an writes for the sāmid is not an argument (the arguments are done) but a *posture*: the standing that diverts must become the standing that *stops* — for prostration begins with standing still. The Prophet's description of prayer's opening is the anti-sāmid charter: *"when the imam says 'God hears him who praises Him,' say it... and when he says 'not of those who earned wrath'..."* — rather the core counsel: *"worship God as though you see Him"* (the iḥsān of the ḥadīth of Gabriel, al-Bukhārī 50; Muslim 8) — the presence that diversion cannot survive; and his instruction for the distracted worshipper: *"when you stand to pray, say takbīr, then recite... then bow until calm (ṭumāʾinīnah), then rise until calm..."* (al-Bukhārī 793) — the *stillness* prescription: ṭumāʾīnah at every stage, the body taught to *halt*, the soul's diversion starved of motion. The man who prays like that has retired from the sāmidūn; the man who will not, stands — humming — while the imminent (v. 57) walks toward him. And the sūrah's last verse will offer the standing audience its last command and its oldest comfort: the forehead, finally, on the floor.

---

## Sūrah al-Najm 53:62

> **So prostrate unto God and worship!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Last Command of the Chapter**

The sūrah ends where every argument must end: at a body part and a floor. *Fa-usjudū lillāhi wa uʿbudū* — *"so prostrate unto God and worship!"* The commentators note the order of the two imperatives: *prostration* first, *worship* second — as though the prostration is the *gate* of all worship, the body's full surrender teaching the heart's. The verse is the fifteenth (per the majority count; fourteen per the alternative count) *verse of prostration* (sajdat al-tilāwah) in the Qur'an, and Ibn Kathīr records the positions of the scholars: most hold the prostration *obligatory* upon the reciter and hearer; Imam Mālik, per the source (Āl, Q), holds it *strongly recommended* here; and the report (Āl) that the Prophet's prostration upon completing this sūrah was a supererogatory custom — the Muslims' consensus across the schools has been to perform it: *when the reciter reaches it, both he and the hearers prostrate, saying in the prostration: *"My face has prostrated to Him who created it and formed it, and brought forth its hearing and its sight by God's power; blessed is God, the best of creators"* (the transmitted prostration formula, Muslim 772's companion texts; Abū Dāwūd 1414).

**The Verse That Made History Bow: The First Public Recitation**

This is the verse at which the sūrah's own history was made, as the introduction recorded. When Ibn Masʿūd stood in the Kaʿbah's court — the first man to recite the Qur'an aloud before the assembled Quraysh — he recited this sūrah, and at its final command: *"I prostrated, and the idolater prostrated, and the believer prostrated — and Satan"* (al-Bukhārī 3423; Muslim 402, in Ibn Masʿūd's wording). The scholars pause over the scene's theology: the command soaked into the body of even the unwilling — the idolater's forehead touched the ground of the God whose idols he kept; Satan's did too, per the report, hiding among the crowd. Ibn Masʿūd's fear — *"until I feared they would make it a pretext"* (Muslim 401) — shows the community's wisdom: a prostration extracted by the word's majesty is not yet faith; the body can be commanded by eloquence that the heart has not consented to. The verse's two imperatives answer the gap: *prostrate — and worship*: the prostration of the body must be *completed* by the worship of the life; the first without the second is the idolater's prostration of that day; the second without the first is the sāmidūn's religion of v. 61 — standing divested of surrender. Islam's genius is the two together.

**The Sunnah of the Prostration: The Angels' Posture and the Devil's Defeat**

The commentators gather the ḥadīth corpus of sajdat al-tilāwah, and its most vivid text is the one the tradition cites at this verse:

> *"When the son of Adam recites a verse of prostration and prostrates, Satan withdraws weeping, saying: woe to me! The son of Adam was commanded to prostrate and he prostrated, and for him is the Garden; and I was commanded to prostrate and I refused, and for me is the Fire."* (Muslim 133)

The irony the tradition savors: the *first* refusal of prostration — Iblīs's at Adam's creation (7:11–12) — is the origin of every rebellion; and the verse of prostration is its *reversal*: every Muslim forehead on the floor is the rehearsal of the original command, the un-doing of the first refusal. Muslim 133's companion report: when Abū Hurayrah prostrated in the Prophet's presence, he called out: *"my back has prostrated to You, so enlarge my back"* — the companions *spoke* in the prostration of recitation, showing it to be a moment of supplication's license; the jurists allow speech (duʿā) in it, the scholars noting it is not prayer's formal prostration. The Prophet's practice: he prostrated in al-Najm, per al-Bukhārī 1070 (Ibn Masʿūd's testimony); his prostrations in recitation were *unannounced* and the Companions followed (the basis of the jurists' rule that the hearer prostrates too).

**The Theology of the Command: Why Prostration Ends the Argument**

Al-Rāzī and the rhetoricians note the sūrah's logic: sixty-one verses of demonstration — the star, the visions, the idols' demolition, the axioms, the ruins, the questions — and then, with nothing left to prove, a single imperative. The structure preaches: *argument is not the end of religion; surrender is.* The Qur'an is not a debate society's charter; it is a call to *usjuddū* — and the verse's second verb makes the surrender's scope: *wa uʿbudū* — "and worship": not this prostration only, but the *whole life* turned service — prayer, charity, fasting, honesty, mercy — the worship that outlives the posture. The commentators recall the sūrah's own verse 9: *two bows' length or nearer* — the distance closed by the Prophet's ascent is closed for every believer, degree by degree, in the descent of his own forehead: *"the nearest a servant is to his Lord is while he is prostrating; so increase supplication therein"* (Muslim 482). The geometry of the sūrah is now complete: the chapter that began with the eyes lifted to a star ends with the forehead lowered to the ground — and the tradition's devotional science names the *same direction* in both postures: up and down meet in the heart's address to the One Lord of the star and the floor.

**The Believer's Lifetime of Foreheads**

The tradition turns the final command into a lifetime's arithmetic, and the scholars love the numbers: seventeen rakʿahs of obligation daily carry *thirty-four* prostrations; a life of prayer is *thousands* of foreheads placed on wood, earth, stone, carpet — each one a consent renewed. The Prophet's counsel refines the arithmetic into quality: *"when one of you prostrates, he is nearest to his Lord"* (Muslim 482); *"return and pray, for you did not pray"* — the Prophet's correction of the man who prayed badly, thrice repeated (al-Bukhārī 757; Muslim 397) — the school of the *real* prostration: stillness at every stage, presence at every count. The reciter who reaches verse 62 tonight places his forehead with sixty-one verses of proof under his cheek: the star he saw, the companion who did not stray, the revelation revealed, the heart that did not lie, the idols that were names, the striving that will be seen, the ruins that answered every denial. And the sūrah's last word — *worship* — hands the reader his life's remaining assignment: *uʿbudū*: the prostration of the forehead extended to the prostration of the hours. The commentators close the chapter as the community has always closed it — with the body, not the pen: *God has spoken truly, and His servant has conveyed: so prostrate, and worship.*

---

## Concluding Note to the Commentary

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Chapter in One Look**

Sūrah al-Najm is the Qur'an's courtroom of the night sky: it swears by a star (v. 1), acquits a companion (vv. 2–4), unveils a teacher of awesome power upon the highest horizon (vv. 5–9), certifies a heart that did not lie (v. 11), records the two bows' length and the lote tree and the covering beyond description (vv. 10–18), demolishes three idols and every theology of conjecture (vv. 19–30), builds the moral physics of burden, labor, sight, requital, laughter, weeping, death, life, the pair, the second genesis, and provision (vv. 31–48), returns to the star to name its Lord (v. 49), files the ruins of ʿĀd, Thamūd, Noah's people, and the razed cities as executed warnings (vv. 50–54), and closes with the questions that outlast every age — *which boons do you dispute? do you marvel? do you laugh and not weep? will you stand diverted?* — before its last command: *prostrate, and worship* (vv. 55–62). The chapter's first public recitation bowed the idolater and the believer and the hidden Satan together (al-Bukhārī 3423; Muslim 402); its final recitation, fourteen centuries on, bows the believer alone — by choice, with proof, upon the floor. Such is Sūrah al-Najm, complete from *By the star when it sets* to *So prostrate unto God and worship* — and all praise belongs to God, the Exalted and the Magnificent.

---
